- 2026-09-28: Step 1.3.a1 — Qdrant authentication (Settings/Compose/env-ripple half). `qdrant_api_key` added to `Settings` as a **required** `SecretStr` (no default, never optional) with its own validator rejecting empty values and any whitespace or control character (`ch.isspace() or not ch.isprintable()`) — the key is sent as a raw HTTP header value, so an embedded newline would allow header injection; the error message never contains the offending value, matching `database_url`'s existing discipline. `test_config_guard.py` extended with `ALLOWED_QDRANT_API_KEY_CALLERS`, starting **empty** (matching how `ALLOWED_DATABASE_URL_CALLERS` started in 1.1.c) — proved by hand with a scratch file under `backend/app/` calling `qdrant_api_key_str()` (guard failed with the expected message), then removed, guard passing again; `app/qdrant.py` will be the sole entry once it exists (1.3.b). Two new leak tests mirror `DATABASE_URL`'s existing pair exactly (`test_get_settings_raises_settings_error_on_malformed_qdrant_api_key`, `test_settings_error_chain_never_carries_the_qdrant_api_key`, walking `__cause__`/`__context__` via the existing shared `_exception_chain` helper) using the task's own example value (`"distinctive-key 123"`); the missing-key case needed no new test — `test_missing_required_variable_raises_and_names_field` is already parametrized over `REQUIRED_VARS` (itself derived from `Settings.model_fields`), so it picked up `QDRANT_API_KEY` automatically, confirmed by the real test run. `.env.example` gains `QDRANT_API_KEY=change-me`. `deploy/docker-compose.yml`: `x-app-env` gains the required-interpolation entry; the real `qdrant` service gains its own `QDRANT__SERVICE__API_KEY: ${QDRANT_API_KEY:?...}` (services don't inherit `x-app-env`, so it needs its own guard, matching `postgres`'s existing per-service pattern). Live finding, verified twice (a bare `docker run` probe container, then the real Compose stack from inside the `api` container via `python3`'s `urllib`, no curl in the image): Qdrant's `/healthz` is **exempt from its own API-key auth** — it answers `200` with no key, a wrong key, or the right key — while a real data endpoint (`/collections`) returns **401** with no key or a wrong key, `200` with the right key. Confirmed both ways rather than assumed; the existing bash `/dev/tcp` healthcheck therefore needed **no change**. - 2026-09-28: Step 1.3.c — Collection schema, in `app/qdrant.py`. Module constants (not `Settings` fields, rule 11 — nothing else needs them configurable yet): `COLLECTION_NAME = "knowledge_chunks"`, `DENSE_VECTOR_NAME = "dense"`, `SPARSE_VECTOR_NAME = "sparse"`, `DENSE_VECTOR_SIZE = 1536` (`ASSUMPTION`: `text-embedding-3-small`'s output dimension — see the new open marker below, owned by Step 2.5), `CLIENT_ID_FIELD = "client_id"`, `SOURCE_ID_FIELD = "source_id"`. Distance is `Distance.COSINE` (`qdrant_client.http.models`), matching docs/SPEC.md §5.2 and `text-embedding-3-small`'s own intended similarity metric. §5.2's fuller payload field list (`source_type`, `doc_id`, `title`, etc.) is documented there but deliberately not indexed here — only `client_id` and `source_id` get a payload index at this step, per the approved design ("no other payload indexes"); the rest arrive with ingestion (2.4/2.5) if/when a query needs to filter on them.

  Repair-versus-fail rule: `ensure_collection(client, name=COLLECTION_NAME)` creates the collection and both indexes if the collection does not exist; if it exists, it fetches the real config and diffs it against the expected schema. A payload index that is entirely *absent* is repaired (created — additive, covers a crash between `create_collection` and index creation); every other difference (wrong dense size, wrong distance, a missing vector, a sparse vector without the IDF modifier, an index present with the wrong parameters, e.g. `client_id` without `is_tenant`) raises `CollectionSchemaMismatch` naming every difference, never silently replaced. A 409 from `create_collection` (two callers racing) is caught narrowly by status code and falls through to the same verify-then-repair-or-raise path, rather than being swallowed or treated as any other error.

  The comparison lives in a pure function, `_schema_differences(actual_collection_info, name) -> list[str]` (composing four small per-aspect helpers, `_dense_vector_diff`/`_sparse_vector_diff`/`_client_id_index_diff`/`_source_id_index_diff`, plus `_mismatch_message(diffs, name) -> str` for the exception text) — no IO, unit-tested offline with hand-built `qdrant_client.http.models.CollectionInfo` objects (`backend/tests/test_qdrant_collection.py`).

  Exact API surface and field paths, verified live against qdrant-client==1.19.1 and the pinned qdrant/qdrant:v1.19.1 server (test-qdrant), not assumed from memory: `AsyncQdrantClient.create_collection(vectors_config={name: VectorParams(size, distance)}, sparse_vectors_config={name: SparseVectorParams(modifier=Modifier.IDF)})`; `create_payload_index(field_schema=KeywordIndexParams(type=PayloadSchemaType.KEYWORD, is_tenant=True))` for client_id, `field_schema=PayloadSchemaType.KEYWORD` (bare) for source_id. Read back via `get_collection()` → `CollectionInfo`: `.config.params.vectors` is `dict[str, VectorParams]` (`.size`, `.distance`); `.config.params.sparse_vectors` is `dict[str, SparseVectorParams]` (`.modifier`, an enum, `None` when no modifier was set); `.payload_schema` is `dict[str, PayloadIndexInfo]` (`.data_type`, a `PayloadSchemaType` enum; `.params` is `None` unless the index was created with explicit `KeywordIndexParams`, in which case `.params.is_tenant` is the real boolean — `False` if explicitly set that way, `None`/absent-`params` if never set). A duplicate `create_collection` call on an existing name raises `qdrant_client.http.exceptions.UnexpectedResponse` with `status_code == 409`, confirmed live.

  HNSW finding (Qdrant's own docs, fetched directly, not recalled from memory): multitenancy guidance recommends `hnsw_config.m = 0` (disables the global HNSW index) together with `hnsw_config.payload_m` (a non-zero per-tenant value, e.g. 16) alongside `is_tenant=True` on the tenant payload index, so Qdrant builds one HNSW index per tenant group instead of one global index — meaningfully faster for large multi-tenant collections. Separately confirmed: `hnsw_config` (including `m`/`payload_m`) can be changed on an *existing* collection via the update-collection-parameters API without recreating it (supported since Qdrant 1.4) — the server rebuilds affected segments in the background to match. Not built now (out of this task's approved design); see the new open marker below.

  Verified: `make lint` clean; full `backend/tests` without a test database: **165 passed, 35 skipped** (up from 153 passed, 29 skipped — the 18 new tests in `test_qdrant_collection.py`, 12 running for real offline, 6 skipping cleanly without `TEST_QDRANT_URL`); `make test-all`: **200 passed** (up from 182 — the same 18 tests, all running live this time), both `test-db` and `test-qdrant` cleaned up afterward; `make lock-check` clean (no dependency added); `git status --short` showed exactly `backend/app/qdrant.py` (modified) and `backend/tests/test_qdrant_collection.py` (new), no `.env`. Sabotage proof, scratch copies only (real files untouched): dropping `is_tenant=True` from the client_id index's creation failed 3 tests (the full-schema read-back, the twice-is-a-no-op test — the second `ensure_collection()` call now sees the created-but-wrong index and raises — and the repair test); separately dropping `Modifier.IDF` from the sparse vector's creation failed 2 tests (the full-schema read-back and the twice-is-a-no-op test).

- 2026-09-28: Step 1.3.d — Isolation proof at the filter/index level, in `app/qdrant.py` (`tenant_filter()`) and `backend/tests/test_qdrant_isolation.py`. `tenant_filter(client_id: uuid.UUID) -> Filter` accepts only a real `uuid.UUID` instance — `isinstance(client_id, uuid.UUID)`, rejecting `None`, `""`, a plain `str`, an `int`, and even a UUID-shaped `str` — raising `TypeError` for anything else. Why: tenant identity already exists as a `uuid.UUID` by the time it reaches Qdrant (it comes from the verified token/database per the engineering rules), so accepting a looser type here would only hide an upstream bug; requiring the real type also forces the matched value to always be `uuid.UUID`'s own canonical lowercase `str()` form, so there is exactly one way a payload's `client_id` string can ever match. The function returns exactly one `must` condition (`FieldCondition(key=CLIENT_ID_FIELD, match=MatchValue(value=str(client_id)))`) and structurally cannot return `should`/`must_not` or an empty `Filter()` — an empty filter matches every point in Qdrant, which would be a silent cross-tenant leak.

  Hybrid outer-filter finding (run once, live, per the approved design; not asserted by any committed test): with `qdrant-client==1.19.1` against the pinned `qdrant/qdrant:v1.19.1` server, a `query_points()` hybrid call (two `Prefetch` entries plus `FusionQuery(fusion=Fusion.RRF)`) with `tenant_filter` applied **only** to the outer `query_filter` and to neither `Prefetch`, returned byte-for-byte the same result as applying it to both prefetches (and adding it to the outer query on top of both prefetches also changed nothing) — the outer filter alone did not leak in this run. This is **not** relied on: the committed tests assert only the mandated configuration (filter inside both prefetches), because relying on outer-only filtering is unverified beyond this one empirical check, is not documented as a guaranteed Qdrant contract, and a small per-`Prefetch` `limit` could still let another tenant's candidates crowd out a tenant's own genuine matches before fusion even runs (a correctness risk distinct from the leak question, observed while probing this — see the open marker below for 3.1). Re-verified at Task 1.3.e (H1 housekeeping): re-ran the same probe fresh this session — unfiltered dense control, unfiltered sparse control, filtered dense (A), filtered sparse (A), filtered hybrid (A) with the filter in both prefetches, and the same hybrid query with the filter ALSO added to the outer query on top of both prefetches — the outer-plus-both-prefetches variant again returned byte-for-byte identical ids, scores and ordering to the both-prefetches-only variant. Real output (tenant A = `76866161-…b512`, tenant B = `ab844dc2-…52e4b`):

  | Query | ids returned (ranked) | client_id |
  |---|---|---|
  | CONTROL dense (unfiltered) | `d0709a44…` (1.0), `9b2fd967…` (1.0, orphan), `9a078d89…` (1.0), `a3a3c770…` (0.0), `fa5f2e5c…` (0.0), `b10e350f…` (0.0), `2d175944…` (0.0) | B, *(none)*, A, B, A, A, A |
  | CONTROL sparse (unfiltered) | `b10e350f…` (0.547), `a3a3c770…` (0.547) | A, B |
  | Filtered dense (A) | `9a078d89…` (1.0), `b10e350f…` (0.0), `fa5f2e5c…` (0.0), `2d175944…` (0.0) | A, A, A, A |
  | Filtered sparse (A) | `b10e350f…` (0.547) | A |
  | Filtered hybrid (A), both prefetches, and again with the outer filter added on top | `b10e350f…` (0.833), `9a078d89…` (0.5), `fa5f2e5c…` (0.25), `2d175944…` (0.2) — identical both times | A (all four) |

  This confirms (does not newly discover) that the outer-plus-both-prefetches configuration is safe and behaves identically to both-prefetches-alone; the open marker below for Step 3.1 is worded accordingly (filter both prefetches, and also the outer query as defense in depth).

  Per-sub-query seed design and what each sabotage proved: two tenants (A, B) were seeded with a deliberately leak-prone set — a B point dense-identical to the query vector (`b_identical`), a B point with zero dense overlap but sparse indices identical to the query (`b_sparse_only`), an A point that is an exact duplicate of `b_identical`'s vector (`a_dup`), two weaker-but-nonzero A dense matches (`a_weak1`/`a_weak2`), an A point analogous to `b_sparse_only` (`a_sparse`), and an orphan point with a matching vector but no `client_id` key in its payload at all. CONTROL (unfiltered) queries prove this data would leak; every filtered query is asserted by exact id-set equality, never "B is absent" alone. Sabotage proof (scratch copies, real files untouched): dropping the filter from only the dense `Prefetch` failed 5 tests; dropping it from only the sparse `Prefetch` failed a different 5 tests — the two sets share the main hybrid/symmetry/no-points/near-miss tests (since the mandated seed gives both tenants points reachable on both channels) but diverge on exactly one test each (`test_hybrid_dense_leak_is_caught_independently_of_the_sparse_prefetch` only under the dense-drop, `test_hybrid_sparse_leak_is_caught_independently_of_the_dense_prefetch` only under the sparse-drop), proving the two sub-queries are checked independently as required. Those two dedicated tests were added specifically to make that divergence possible: with a large, no-truncation limit and no score threshold, an *unfiltered* dense sub-query provably returns every point that has a dense vector at all regardless of score (confirmed live), so any test whose collection contains a cross-tenant point reachable by dense fails under a dropped dense filter no matter what the sparse side does — genuine per-channel isolation required two minimal collections where each tenant's only point omits the *other* named vector entirely (Qdrant allows a point to omit a named vector; confirmed live that such a point is then invisible to every query on that vector name, exactly like the orphan point is invisible to every tenant filter). Making `tenant_filter` return an empty `Filter()`, and separately `Filter(must=[])`, each failed 10 of the 16 live+offline tests (every test that touches a real query or `tenant_filter`'s own structure).

  Verified: `make lint` clean; full `backend/tests` without a test database: **172 passed, 44 skipped** (up from 165 passed, 35 skipped — the 16 new tests in `test_qdrant_isolation.py`, 7 running for real offline, 9 skipping cleanly without `TEST_QDRANT_URL`); `make test-all`: **216 passed** (up from 200 — the same 16 tests, all running live), both `test-db` and `test-qdrant` cleaned up afterward; `git status --short` showed exactly `backend/app/qdrant.py` (modified) and `backend/tests/test_qdrant_isolation.py` (new), no `.env`.

- 2026-09-28: Step 1.3.e — Read-path guard, `backend/tests/test_qdrant_read_path_guard.py` (no application code changes, other than one comment sentence added under H2 below). Import confinement (rule a) is the primary guard, not a method-name search, because docs/SPEC.md names the function `retrieve()` and `AsyncQdrantClient` also has a method literally named `retrieve` — a name-only scan would wrongly flag legitimate application code doing `from app.retrieval.service import retrieve` or `service.retrieve(...)`. A file that cannot import `qdrant_client` or obtain a client at all cannot call a read method on one, so confining *who can hold a client* (rule a) does the real work; the read-method check (rule b) only ever applies to the handful of files rule (a) already allows through. Two allow-lists, each with a NOTE comment (matching `test_config_guard.py`'s convention): `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST = {"app/qdrant.py", "app/retrieval/service.py"}` (the only files allowed to import `qdrant_client` or obtain a client via `get_qdrant_client()`/`build_qdrant_client()`) and `READ_ALLOWLIST = {"app/retrieval/service.py"}` (the only file allowed to call a method classified READ — this includes `app/qdrant.py` itself, which has client access but no read allowance). Rule (c) (raw transport surfaces `http`/`grpc_points`/`grpc_collections`/`grpc_snapshots`/`_client`) is checked in **every** file under `backend/app`, including the two allow-listed files, since either allow-list existing at all would otherwise let a file reach a read endpoint under a name the classification never sees. UPDATED at the duplication check following 1.3.c/1.3.d/1.3.e (2026-09-28, see that entry below): rule (a) was tightened from a name-specific check (only `get_qdrant_client`/`build_qdrant_client` imports) to catching **any** import of the `app.qdrant` module, in every form (plain `import app.qdrant`, `from app import qdrant`, `from app.qdrant import <anything>`, and the relative equivalents resolved against each file's own package) — closing a real bypass found in review (`from app import qdrant` followed by `qdrant.get_qdrant_client()` attribute access), reproduced live before the fix and confirmed caught after it. A new rule (d), `FORBIDDEN_IN_APP`, was also added with no allow-list at all (checked even in `app/qdrant.py`): `create_snapshot`/`create_full_snapshot`/`create_shard_snapshot`/`recover_snapshot`/`recover_shard_snapshot`/`recreate_collection`/`delete_collection` are forbidden everywhere in `backend/app` (snapshots are an unfiltered cross-tenant bulk export/import path; `recreate_collection`/`delete_collection` would destroy every tenant's data at once). The READ/WRITE/CONFIG classification table above is unchanged by either addition — rule (d) is an orthogonal restriction on top of the existing CONFIG classification for those methods, not a reclassification.

  Classification approach: every one of `AsyncQdrantClient`'s 64 public callables (introspected live via `inspect.getmembers`, qdrant-client==1.19.1 — not assumed from the task's own illustrative name list) was classified by hand into exactly one of READ/WRITE/CONFIG in the test file itself, cross-checked by a completeness test that fails if the installed client gains an unclassified method or loses a classified one, or if any method lands in more than one set. Real finding, reported before building: qdrant-client 1.19.1 has **no** bare `search`/`recommend`/`discover` method at all (confirmed via `hasattr`) — those older REST operations are fully unified into `query_points()`'s `query` argument (`NearestQuery`/`RecommendQuery`/`DiscoverQuery` etc.), so the task's own example method list named operations that no longer exist on the pinned version; the classification table instead reflects the real, introspected surface. READ (10): `count`, `facet`, `migrate` (unimplemented on the async client today — raises `NotImplementedError` — but its intended semantics and the sync client's real implementation read every point of a collection to copy it elsewhere, so it is classified READ per the "when unsure, READ" rule), `query_batch_points`, `query_points`, `query_points_groups`, `retrieve`, `scroll`, `search_matrix_offsets`, `search_matrix_pairs`. WRITE (11): `batch_update_points`, `clear_payload`, `delete`, `delete_payload`, `delete_vectors`, `overwrite_payload`, `set_payload`, `update_vectors`, `upload_collection`, `upload_points`, `upsert`. CONFIG (43): collection/index/alias/snapshot/cluster administration and inspection (`create_collection`, `delete_collection`, `recreate_collection`, `get_collection`, `collection_exists`, `get_collections`, `update_collection`, `create_payload_index`, `delete_payload_index`, `create_vector_name`, `delete_vector_name`, `get_aliases`, `get_collection_aliases`, `update_collection_aliases`, every `*_snapshot*`/`*_shard*` method, `cluster_status`, `cluster_telemetry`, `cluster_collection_update`, `collection_cluster_info`, `recover_current_peer`, `remove_peer`, `get_optimizations`, `info`, `close`) plus embedding-model metadata utilities unrelated to any collection's stored points (`get_embedding_size`, `list_image_models`, `list_late_interaction_multimodal_models`, `list_late_interaction_text_models`, `list_sparse_models`, `list_text_models`).

  Rule (b) scope trade-off, stated as a comment in the test file: rule (b) (read-method calls) applies only to files with client access (i.e. only files in `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST`) — an unrelated file with no client access whose own object happens to have a method named `scroll` is not flagged, a deliberate accepted false-negative, because rule (a) already guarantees that file cannot hold a real Qdrant client, so there is nothing for rule (b) to protect against there. Guard unit-tested on synthetic `tmp_path` trees (no Qdrant needed): every required FAIL case (import outside the allow-list, a dynamic `importlib.import_module("qdrant_client")`, a client-access file calling `query_points`/`scroll`, `app/qdrant.py` itself calling `retrieve` (the exact ambiguous name), the raw `http`/`_client` surfaces) and every required PASS case (the allow-listed file calling `query_points`, `app/qdrant.py` calling `get_collection`/`collection_exists`, a bare-name `retrieve(...)` call, a `service.retrieve(...)` attribute call, and the unrelated same-named `.scroll()` method) is its own test. The guard passes today against the real `backend/app` tree (nothing reads yet). Sabotage proof (scratch copy of the real tree, real files untouched): adding a `query_points()` call to a copy of `app/qdrant.py` was caught by rule (b); separately adding `import qdrant_client` to a copy of `app/db.py` was caught by rule (a).

The Step 3.1 open marker (below) is updated accordingly: filter both prefetches, and also the outer query as defense in depth.

This is attribution noise only, not a real compatibility failure against the live authenticated server; one sentence recording this was added to the existing header comment in `app/qdrant.py` (the only application-code change in this task, explicitly requested by this housekeeping item).

- 2026-09-28: Step 1.4 breakdown approved — twelve subtasks 1.4.a–1.4.l (see the Step 1.4 task list above), based on docs/SPEC.md §4.2 (session flow), §4.3 (JWT/origin rules), §4.5 (thread/conversation identity), §6.3 (draft/live/suspended) and §16 (the identity-and-threads security tests). Decisions confirmed: (1) **PyJWT** as the JWT library (rule 8; exact version re-verified live on PyPI at 1.4.b's actual build time, not copied from this planning session); decode requires `exp`, `iss`, `aud`, `sub`, `tid` and `org` all present, and the algorithm is pinned to `HS256` (docs/SPEC.md §4.3 already mandates HS256 — not a fresh choice here, just the library that implements it); (2) `JWT_SIGNING_KEY` is a required string, minimum 32 characters, rejecting whitespace and control characters (mirrors `qdrant_api_key`'s validator exactly), with a 32-or-more-character placeholder in `.env.example`; `JWT_SIGNING_KEY_PREVIOUS` is optional, validated the same way when present, and enables rotation with no downtime (docs/SPEC.md §4.3); (3) `kid` is the first 12 hex characters of the signing key's own SHA-256 digest — deterministic, so both the current and previous key's `kid` can be computed and compared without storing a separate rotation table; (4) no refresh-token mechanism — a token nearing/past its 15-minute lifetime is replaced by calling `/api/v1/session` again with the stored `visitor_secret`, never a dedicated refresh endpoint; (5) the visitor secret is `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`, stored as a SHA-256 hex digest, looked up by hash through `TenantScopedRepository` scoped additionally to the given `site_key_id`; a wrong or unknown secret behaves **exactly** like no secret at all — a fresh visitor is created and 200 is returned, so the endpoint can never be used to test whether a given secret is merely wrong versus never having existed; (6) a draft key from an allowed origin **succeeds**, with `test_mode` set in the response (docs/SPEC.md §6.3: draft keys work from allowed origins) — this is a success path, not a failure category; (7) the one true failure category — unknown key, missing or disallowed origin, a suspended key, and a non-active tenant — all produce one byte-identical response: a fixed 403 body, never `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, with `Vary: Origin` present on every response regardless of outcome, so none of the four conditions can be distinguished from any other (no oracle); (8) origin rules: exact match on scheme, host and effective port; `allowed_origins` entries are stored and compared in the exact form `scheme://host[:port]`; no wildcards; `Referer` is never consulted; a missing `Origin`, a literal `"null"` `Origin`, or a repeated `Origin` header are all rejected; (9) CORS is dynamic per site key, not a static middleware list: preflight (`OPTIONS`) is answered without any DB lookup at all, and a real response only ever carries `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` once the origin check has actually passed — this clarifies docs/SPEC.md §4.2's wording for the preflight case specifically, which the spec text alone doesn't disambiguate; (10) `/ready` is a new, separate endpoint from the existing liveness-only `/health`, which must keep never touching Postgres; (11) the lifespan hook disposes only what 1.4 itself created (`get_engine()`, `get_qdrant_client()`) — it is not a general app/worker lifecycle framework, and the worker's own shutdown needs stay a separate, later concern; (12) the public session-config response is built from an explicit field allow-list, never a raw model/row dump, so an accidentally-added column can never leak into a public response by default; (13) `tenants.status` gets a fixed two-value vocabulary, `active`/`suspended`, fail-closed (anything else is treated as not-active), enforced by a `CHECK` constraint in 1.4.d — resolving the previously-open tenants.status vocabulary marker now, rather than waiting for Step 6.5. Current task set to 1.4.a, next to 1.4.b. Counter stays at n=1 (this breakdown is planning only, no files changed, so it does not itself count toward the next duplication check — matching the precedent set at the Step 1.3 breakdown); 1.4.a will make it n=2, and 1.4.b would make it n=3, so the check falls due after 1.4.b.
- 2026-09-28: Step 1.4.a — JWT signing key in `Settings` plus the full environment ripple. `jwt_signing_key: SecretStr = Field(min_length=32)` (required, no default) uses pydantic's own built-in length constraint rather than a hand-rolled `len(raw) < 32` check (rule 11 — a well-known mechanism); a `field_validator` adds the whitespace/control-character check (shared with the previous key via one small `_require_no_whitespace_or_control_chars(raw, field_name)` helper, since the same two rules apply to both fields and a second hand-copy would be an obvious, avoidable duplication at write time, not something to wait three tasks to notice). `jwt_signing_key_previous: SecretStr | None = Field(default=None, min_length=32)`: a `mode="before"` validator converts an empty string to `None` *before* the length constraint would otherwise reject `""` as too short (confirmed live against the installed pydantic==2.13.5 that `Field(min_length=...)` on a `SecretStr | None` field applies only to the `SecretStr` arm, never to `None` — verified with a throwaway scratch model before writing the real one); a `model_validator(mode="after")` rejects `jwt_signing_key_previous` when it exactly equals `jwt_signing_key`, with a message naming only the rule, never either value. Accessors `jwt_signing_key_str() -> str` and `jwt_signing_key_previous_str() -> str | None` mirror `qdrant_api_key_str()`'s "the one explicit call that unwraps the secret" discipline exactly. `test_config_guard.py`'s `_SECRET_ACCESSOR_ALLOW_LISTS` gained both accessor names, sharing one new `ALLOWED_JWT_KEY_CALLERS: tuple[str, ...] = ()` (starts empty, same convention as `ALLOWED_QDRANT_API_KEY_CALLERS` did at 1.3.a1 — `app/auth/tokens.py` becomes the sole entry once 1.4.b builds it) — proved by hand with two scratch caller files under `backend/app/` (one per accessor), each caught by the guard with the expected message, then removed, guard passing again, real files confirmed untouched via `git status --short`.

  `REQUIRED_VARS` in `conftest.py` changed meaning: `jwt_signing_key_previous` is the *first* Settings field with a default, so `REQUIRED_VARS = [name.upper() for name, field in Settings.model_fields.items() if field.is_required()]` now actually filters (previously every field happened to be required, so a plain field-name list and "the required ones" were silently the same list). `field.is_required()` is pydantic's own built-in "has no default" check (rule 11), confirmed against the installed pydantic==2.13.5. A new `OPTIONAL_VARS` and `ALL_SETTINGS_VARS = REQUIRED_VARS + OPTIONAL_VARS` were added alongside it: `REQUIRED_VARS` alone now correctly drives the "missing this var must raise" parametrized test and the `.env.example` required-set check (an optional field must never be treated as required by either), while `ALL_SETTINGS_VARS` is the superset for anywhere the actual intent is "no Settings-related env var at all" — the autouse `_isolated_env` fixture (so a value one test sets for the optional key never leaks into the next), `test_worker.py`'s `_worker_subprocess_env()`, and `test_config.py`'s `test_module_imports_cleanly_with_empty_environment`, all three previously using `REQUIRED_VARS` for what was actually always meant to be "every Settings var, none of them" rather than "only the required ones." `JWT_SIGNING_KEY_PREVIOUS` is declared in `.env.example` (empty) via `EXTRA_EXAMPLE_KEYS`, the same escape hatch already used for `POSTGRES_USER`/`PASSWORD`/`DB` — matching the general rule that `EXTRA_EXAMPLE_KEYS` is for anything `.env.example` must declare beyond what `REQUIRED_VARS` alone would demand.

- 2026-09-28: Step 1.4.b — JWT module (`backend/app/auth/tokens.py`). `PyJWT==2.15.1` approved as a new runtime dependency (rule 8), version re-verified live on PyPI at build time (not copied from the 1.4-research report's `2.15.0`, superseded by this exact-same-day release): `requires_python>=3.9` (fine under the pinned `==3.12.*`); `requires_dist` lists only `typing_extensions` (conditional on `python_version<3.11`, not applicable) and `cryptography` (conditional on the `crypto` extra, not installed — HS256 needs no asymmetric-algorithm support). `make lock` gained exactly one new package in both lockfiles, `pyjwt==2.15.1`, with exactly 2 hashes (one wheel, one sdist — the wheel is `py3-none-any`, confirming no compiled/platform-specific pieces and no sdist-only surprise); no other package appeared or changed. `make audit`: **0 findings**, reported plainly as instructed, no action taken.

  Claims: `iss` (constant `TOKEN_ISSUER = "widgetplatform"`, an `ASSUMPTION` — docs/SPEC.md §4.2 pins `aud` to `"widget-api"` explicitly but never gives `iss` a literal value; `"widgetplatform"` matches the same neutral code name already used elsewhere, e.g. `app/main.py`'s `_TITLE`), `aud="widget-api"`, `sub=str(vid)`, `tid=str(tenant_id)`, `org=<origin>`, `exp` (15 minutes from encode time, via a timezone-aware `datetime`, confirmed live that PyJWT accepts and round-trips this directly to a Unix timestamp — no manual conversion needed). `kid` header = the first 12 hex characters of `sha256(current_key_bytes)`; the same computation (`_key_id()`, one shared private function) is used both when encoding (to label which key signed the token) and when decoding (to help choose which configured key to try first) — confirmed live against the installed PyJWT: (1) `algorithms=["HS256"]` passed explicitly and unconditionally to `jwt.decode()` is what actually defeats a hand-forged `{"alg": "none"}` token, not any PyJWT-side special-casing of "none" — proven with a token built entirely by hand (no `jwt.encode()` involved), not merely asserted; (2) PyJWT's `options={"require": [...]}` enforces presence generically for *any* claim name, including the two custom ones (`tid`, `org`) alongside the four it already recognizes (`exp`, `iss`, `aud`, `sub`) — one option list covers all six uniformly, rather than a hand-rolled presence check for the two PyJWT doesn't know about itself.

  Key-selection order for decode: current key is tried first, then the previous key if one is configured and the current key's attempt fails, otherwise `InvalidSessionToken` is raised — exactly matching the task's own plain-English description. The unverified `kid` header is read (via `jwt.get_unverified_header()`, itself wrapped so a malformed header just means "no ordering hint," not a crash) and used **only** to swap that order — trying the previous key first specifically when `kid` unambiguously names it and not the current key — while both configured keys are *always* still tried regardless of what `kid` says, so a missing, wrong, or attacker-forged `kid` can never skip a real verification attempt, only reorder two attempts that both still happen. `decode_session_token()` raises one exception type, `InvalidSessionToken`, for every failure category (expired, forged, wrong `aud`/`iss`, a missing claim, wrong/no key, a tampered signature, a `kid` matching neither key, or a `tid`/`sub` that isn't a well-formed UUID) — deliberately no distinct subclass per cause, so nothing about *why* a token failed is ever visible to a caller from the exception type alone. `test_config_guard.py`'s `ALLOWED_JWT_KEY_CALLERS` (started empty at 1.4.a) gets its first real entry, `app/auth/tokens.py` — proved by hand with a scratch caller file under `backend/app/` (caught by the guard with the expected message, then removed, guard passing again, real files confirmed untouched).

  Verified: `make lint` clean; full `backend/tests` without a test database: **239 passed, 44 skipped** (up from 221 passed, 44 skipped — the 18 new tests in `test_tokens.py`, all running for real, none needing a database); run standalone first, then inside the full suite, both give the same 18/18; `make lock-check` clean; `make audit`: 0 findings; `git status --short` showed exactly the six expected files (`backend/pyproject.toml`, `backend/requirements.lock`, `backend/requirements-dev.lock`, `backend/tests/test_config_guard.py` modified; `backend/app/auth/tokens.py`, `backend/tests/test_tokens.py` new), `.env` correctly absent. No ASSUMPTION markers beyond the `iss` value noted above; no UNCERTAIN markers — every claim (the alg=none rejection mechanism, the `require` option's generic-claim-name behavior, the exception-chaining-free raise after the key-selection loop, the exact lockfile addition) was verified directly, not assumed. Current task set to 1.4.c, next to 1.4.d. Counter reached n=3 (1.3.f, 1.4.a, 1.4.b); the duplication check is due, but per the task's own instruction it is **not** run now and is reported here as due, to be requested separately.
- 2026-09-28: Duplication check after 1.4.a/b: qdrant_api_key delegates to the shared whitespace/control-char helper, shared leak-assertion helper across config and token tests, added tests proving encode_session_token cannot use the previous key, an algorithm-confusion rejection, and previous-key leak-safety through the decode path; A3 already done, A4 and both B items declined as premature or proportionate. Specifically: `_require_no_whitespace_or_control_chars()` (`app/config.py`) gained a `reason` parameter and is now the one shared check called by `qdrant_api_key`, `jwt_signing_key` and `jwt_signing_key_previous`'s validators, replacing `qdrant_api_key`'s own duplicated `any(ch.isspace() or not ch.isprintable() ...)` line (behavior unchanged, message wording per-field so it stays truthful for each secret's own reason). `assert_secret_not_in_exception_chain(exc, *secrets)` added to `tests/conftest.py` (wraps the existing `_exception_chain()` walk plus the not-in-str/repr/args assertions); used at `test_config.py`'s three leak-test sites (`database_url`, `qdrant_api_key`, `jwt_signing_key`) and `test_tokens.py`'s leak test, replacing four copies of the same inline loop. `test_qdrant.py`'s two leak tests intentionally left untouched — they additionally check a rendered traceback, a surface the shared helper doesn't cover. Three new tests added to `test_tokens.py`: `test_encode_session_token_never_uses_the_previous_key` (configures a previous key different from current, encodes, asserts the token's `kid` header equals `_key_id()` of the current key only); `test_algorithm_confusion_signature_is_rejected` (header claims `HS256`, signature actually computed with HMAC-SHA384 over the correct key — proves `jwt.decode()` recomputes and compares the real MAC rather than trusting the header); `test_decode_error_never_contains_either_configured_key_during_rotation` (both `JWT_SIGNING_KEY` and `JWT_SIGNING_KEY_PREVIOUS` set to distinctive fake values, token signed with a third unrelated key so both configured keys fail, neither configured value appears anywhere in the raised exception). Declined as-is: A3 (the three secret-accessor allow-lists in `test_config_guard.py` were already unified behind one dict-driven check in the prior duplication check, after 1.3.a1/a2/b — nothing left to do); A4 (the empty-string-means-None pattern for `jwt_signing_key_previous` has exactly one occurrence — generalizing it now would be a premature abstraction, per rule 11); B1 (always trying both configured keys regardless of `kid` costs at most one extra cheap HMAC verification and is what keeps an attacker-controlled `kid` from ever skipping a real check — the right tradeoff, not over-engineering); B2 (`InvalidSessionToken` as a single exception type for every failure remains proportionate — it is the no-oracle property the task explicitly required, not accidental complexity).

- 2026-09-28: Step 1.4.c — visitor-secret utilities (`backend/app/auth/secrets.py`, new; no new dependency, `secrets`/`hashlib`/`hmac` are all stdlib). `generate_visitor_secret()` is `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)` (256 bits, docs/SPEC.md §4.2), always 43 characters for that fixed input size — checked live, not assumed. `hash_visitor_secret()` is `hashlib.sha256(...).hexdigest()`, lowercase hex — the same hex-encoding convention `app/auth/tokens.py`'s `_key_id()` already uses for the JWT `kid` header, deliberately kept as one project-wide convention rather than a second one for this column. `verify_visitor_secret()` uses `hmac.compare_digest()` exclusively (never `==`/`!=`) to compare the freshly-computed hash against the stored one. Module named `secrets.py`, the same as the stdlib module it wraps: confirmed live (not assumed from general Python knowledge) that `import secrets` inside it resolves to the stdlib module, not itself — printed `visitor_secrets.secrets is (the imported stdlib secrets module)` → `True`, since this file's own directory is never added to `sys.path`. `hmac.compare_digest()`'s own str-argument behavior confirmed live: it requires ASCII-only content and raises `TypeError` for anything else (reproduced directly: `hmac.compare_digest("a"*64, "日"*32)` raises `"comparing strings with non-ASCII characters is not supported"`), so `verify_visitor_secret()` checks `stored_hash.isascii()` first and returns `False` immediately rather than letting a non-ASCII `stored_hash` raise; a wrong-length or non-hex-but-ASCII `stored_hash` needs no such guard, since `compare_digest()` itself already returns `False` for those without raising.

  Tests (`test_visitor_secrets.py`, 10 new, all offline — no database, no Qdrant): generation length/no-fixed-prefix (20 samples, no shared 2-character prefix, all unique) and two-calls-differ; hash determinism and fixed-length lowercase hex (regex `^[0-9a-f]{64}$`); hash output never contains the plaintext secret as a substring; verify true for the correct secret; verify false for a wholly different secret; verify false for a near-miss that differs by exactly its last character only (proves the comparison isn't a short-circuit prefix/substring match); verify false for an empty-string secret; verify false for three shapes of malformed `stored_hash` without raising — too short, wrong-but-ASCII content, and (the case that actually exercises the `isascii()` guard) non-ASCII content that would otherwise raise `TypeError`. For proving `hmac.compare_digest` is the actual comparison used (the task's item (h)): chose monkeypatching over bytecode/source inspection — it is this project's already-established way of proving a library call really happens (the same technique `test_qdrant.py`'s warnings-filter test uses), and it proves the real call with the real arguments, not merely that some instruction referencing the name exists in the compiled bytecode, which would be both more fragile (breaks on any harmless refactor of how the name is looked up) and disproportionate for a two-argument stdlib call. The spy wraps the real `hmac.compare_digest`, so the test still exercises genuine constant-time comparison logic, not a stub.

Model changes first, migration generated against the real test database second (1.2.c's own discipline): `app/tenancy/models.py`'s `Visitor.__table_args__` gets `Index("ix_visitors_site_key_id_secret_hash", "site_key_id", "secret_hash", unique=True)`, `Tenant.__table_args__` gets `CheckConstraint("status IN ('active', 'suspended')", name="ck_tenants_status")` (the vocabulary decided at the Step 1.4 breakdown; this also resolves the field's own long-standing ASSUMPTION comment, now removed since the vocabulary is no longer open). Index scope: deliberately `(site_key_id, secret_hash)` together, never a global unique index on `secret_hash` alone — a hash collision across two different site keys is cryptographically negligible (SHA-256 over a 256-bit random secret, Task 1.4.c) and isn't the threat this index defends against; it exists to make one site key's own lookup fast and to reject an accidental duplicate insert within that one key. `alembic revision --autogenerate` detected the index but not the CHECK constraint — confirmed live that Alembic's autogenerate does not compare CHECK constraints at all (a documented limitation, not a bug); the constraint was added by hand into the generated migration, `op.create_check_constraint("ck_tenants_status", "tenants", "status IN ('active', 'suspended')")`, the same way `ck_site_keys_status` itself was hand-written into the first migration's `op.create_table()` call. Fixture check (before adding the constraint, as instructed): searched every test fixture and the one production code path that constructs a `Tenant` row (`app/tenancy/repository.py`'s `create_tenant()`) for any status value outside `active`/`suspended` — found none; all three `Tenant(...)`/`create_tenant()` call sites (`test_tenancy_repository.py` lines 96, 97, 202) already use `"active"`, so nothing needed fixing. No-backfill reasoning confirmed correct: this is a fresh migration history (only one prior migration, `541870ecc5d9`) with no production data anywhere, so there is no existing row a bad `tenants.status` value or a duplicate `(site_key_id, secret_hash)` pair could already occupy — a backfill step would have nothing to do.

Full upgrade/downgrade/upgrade cycle run against the real test database: `upgrade head` (541870ecc5d9 → dcd1f5b27bb7) succeeded, `downgrade -1` (back to 541870ecc5d9) succeeded, `upgrade head` again succeeded — no errors in either direction. - 2026-09-29: Step 1.4.e — unscoped site-key lookup and scoped visitor lookup-by-hash (`backend/app/tenancy/repository.py`, extended). `docker info` confirmed the Docker engine running before any Docker-dependent step. `get_site_key_by_key(session, key)` added as a module-level function next to `create_tenant()` — same structural reasoning: no tenant is known yet at this point in the session flow (docs/SPEC.md §4.2's very first request carries only `{site_key, visitor_secret}`), so a `TenantScopedRepository` (which requires a real `tenant_id` to construct) cannot fit here. Two design points the task named as open, both already settled by the Step 1.4 breakdown's own 1.4.e task-list row rather than decided fresh here — confirmed by re-reading that row before writing any code, then built to match it exactly: (1) **`site_key_id` parameter**: `get_visitor_by_secret(secret, site_key_id)` is scoped to BOTH `self.tenant_id` AND the given `site_key_id`, per the row's own words ("scoped both to the repository's own `tenant_id` and to the given `site_key_id`") — matches 1.4.d's own index scope (per-site-key, not tenant-wide), since a visitor secret is only ever meaningful within the one site key that issued it (docs/SPEC.md §4.2's localStorage key is `aw:{site_key}`); searching across all of a tenant's site keys was considered and rejected as unnecessarily broad and inconsistent with the index it's meant to use. (2) **`last_seen_at` update on match**: called automatically by `get_visitor_by_secret()` itself (via a new `touch_visitor_last_seen(vid)` method), not left as a separate step for 1.4.f/g to remember — again matching the breakdown row's own phrasing ("scoped visitor lookup-by-hash ... **and** a `last_seen_at` update on match", one bundled unit), and independently justified: docs/SPEC.md §4.2 has no scenario where verifying a secret successfully should NOT count as a visit, and there is exactly one planned call site (1.4.g), so nothing is lost by not exposing two steps.

  `get_visitor_by_secret()` queries candidates scoped to `(tenant_id, site_key_id)`, then checks each with `verify_visitor_secret()` (1.4.c's constant-time comparison) in Python — deliberately never a SQL `secret_hash = <computed hash>` equality filter, which would let the query itself decide "matches" instead of 1.4.c's own comparison function. A wrong secret for a real row and a secret matching no row at all both fall through the same loop to the same `return None` — no distinguishing branch exists in the code, so nothing needs to be invented to make them indistinguishable, and the tests assert this directly. `touch_visitor_last_seen(vid)` uses a bounded `UPDATE ... WHERE vid = ? AND tenant_id = self.tenant_id` (the same "automatic filter" shape `get_visitor_by_id`/`get_conversation_by_id` already use for reads, applied to a write) rather than `create_visitor`/`create_conversation`'s `_verify_owned()`-then-raise pattern: reasoned and confirmed correct that raising isn't needed here, since (unlike those two methods' externally-supplied foreign ids establishing a new permanent reference) this method's one call site always passes a `vid` it just read from a row already scoped to this same `tenant_id` — a `vid` outside that scope silently updates zero rows, matching every read method's own "automatically filtered, no error" behavior rather than introducing a third response shape.

  **1.4.f** (`backend/app/auth/origin.py`, new): `origin_is_allowed(origin: str, allowed_origins: list[str]) -> bool` normalizes both sides to `(scheme, lowercase host, effective port)` before comparing — effective port matters because an omitted default port (`https://a.example`) must match its explicit form (`https://a.example:443`) and vice versa; verified live that `urlsplit()` handles a completely malformed string (empty, "not-a-url", the literal `"null"`) by simply returning no hostname, so these are rejected by the same one code path, never a special case. A missing or repeated `Origin` header is NOT this module's job (it only ever receives one already-extracted string) — that is `routes.py`'s job, via `Request.headers.getlist("origin")` (verified live against the installed Starlette that this is the real method name; not `get_list`, which does not exist). 16 offline, table-driven tests (`test_origin.py`): exact match, effective-port equivalence both directions, wrong scheme/host/port, host case-insensitivity, `"null"`, malformed strings, an empty allow-list, and proof that a bare `"*"` allow-list entry never matches anything (no wildcard code path exists to exploit).

  **1.4.g** (`backend/app/auth/routes.py`, replacing the 1.1b stub; `backend/app/auth/schemas.py`, new; `backend/app/main.py` wires the new router in). Handler order matches the task's own (a)-(g) exactly: Origin presence (single value only) → `get_site_key_by_key` (1.4.e) → site-key status (suspended fails; draft/live proceed, draft sets `test_mode`) → tenant status via `TenantScopedRepository.get_tenant()` → `origin_is_allowed` (1.4.f) against the site key's `allowed_origins` → `get_visitor_by_secret` (1.4.e) if a secret was given → `encode_session_token` (1.4.b) either way. A wrong or absent secret is NOT a failure — both fall through to the identical first-visit branch (`create_visitor` + a freshly generated/hashed secret, 1.2.e/1.4.c), matching the Step 1.4 breakdown's own decision (5): a wrong secret behaves exactly like no secret. **Byte-identical failure proof**: one fixed `_FAILURE_BODY`/`_FAILURE_STATUS`/`Vary: Origin`-only header set, built once and returned identically from all five failure branches — proved with ONE test (`test_all_failure_categories_produce_byte_identical_responses`) that constructs missing-Origin, unknown-site-key, suspended-site-key, non-active-tenant and disallowed-origin scenarios and asserts status + raw body bytes + the full header set (minus `Date`) are identical across all five, not five separate "it's a 403" tests. **OPTIONS preflight** (`session_preflight`, `@router.options("/session")`): a separate handler with no `Depends(get_db_session)` and no body parameter at all in its signature — it structurally cannot query the database or read `site_key`/`visitor_secret` even if it tried; it echoes the request `Origin` into `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` unconditionally (real authorization happens only on the actual `POST`), sets `Access-Control-Allow-Methods: POST, OPTIONS` and `Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Content-Type`, and `Vary: Origin`. - 2026-09-29: Step 1.4.h — verified-identity dependency (`backend/app/auth/dependencies.py`, new), the first authenticated-request dependency; every authenticated endpoint from here forward depends on it. `docker info` confirmed the Docker engine running before any Docker-dependent step. `VerifiedIdentity(tenant_id, vid, org)`: an immutable (`frozen=True`) dataclass carrying exactly these three fields, nothing else — the one thing downstream code is meant to read for caller identity, never re-derived from a request body/query field (matches the existing open-marker note for Step 3.1's `retrieve()`).

  `get_current_visitor(request, session)`: (a) `Authorization` must be exactly `"Bearer <token>"` — a single space, non-empty token, no trailing text, case-sensitive `"Bearer"` — anything else (missing header, wrong scheme, empty token, extra text, no separating space) is a 401; (b) `decode_session_token()` (1.4.b) — any `InvalidSessionToken` is a 401, proving the WIRING with a hand-built expired token, a tampered signature, and a token signed with an unrelated key, rather than re-deriving all of 1.4.b's own 18 cryptographic tests; (c) the request's `Origin` (via `request.headers.getlist("origin")`, so a missing or repeated header also fails) must equal the token's own `org` claim EXACTLY — a **direct equality check**, deliberately NOT `origin_is_allowed()` (1.4.f): that function answers "is this origin on a site key's allow-list" (checked once, at session-creation time, 1.4.g); this question is narrower and different — "is this the exact origin THIS token was issued for" — checked on every subsequent authenticated request, and conflating the two would let a token minted for one allowed origin be replayed from a DIFFERENT origin that also happens to be on the same site key's allow-list, which the `org` claim binding exists specifically to prevent (docs/SPEC.md §4.3's own "Origin binding" rule); (d) tenant and site-key status via the 60-second TTL cache below — a non-`active` tenant or a `suspended` site key (draft and live both continue to work, matching 1.4.g's own session-creation rule) is a 401, checked against the cached value, never a fresh read on every request.

  **Why an extra lookup exists that the cache doesn't cover**: the JWT carries no `site_key_id` claim (1.4.b's claim set is fixed: `iss`/`aud`/`sub`/`tid`/`org`/`exp`/`kid`), so checking "site key status" requires first resolving the visitor's `site_key_id` via `vid` — one indexed, primary-key-scoped lookup on `visitors`, done on every request, NOT behind the 60-second cache. This is deliberate: a visitor's `site_key_id` is immutable once set (no code path anywhere changes it after `create_visitor`), so it can never go stale, unlike the two STATUSES, which really can change — that's what the cache exists for. A stricter reading of docs/SPEC.md §4.3's "no DB hit per request" would need this resolution cached too; noted as an UNCERTAIN marker below rather than silently over-building a second cache for an immutable mapping that costs one indexed lookup per request either way.

  **Cache design** (`StatusCache`, keyed by `(tenant_id, site_key_id)`): lives on `app.state.status_cache` (the same place `Settings` itself already lives, `app/main.py`'s `create_app()`), created lazily on first use — NOT a module-level `lru_cache` singleton like `get_settings()`/`get_engine()`. This means every `create_app()` call gets a fresh, empty cache automatically: no cross-test leakage is possible (a new FastAPI app has a new `app.state` namespace), and no `cache_clear()` step needed anywhere, unlike the existing singletons. Tests inject a controllable clock by pre-seeding `app.state.status_cache = StatusCache(clock=fake_clock)` before the first request. Keyed by `(tenant_id, site_key_id)` together, not `site_key_id` alone (which is already a globally-unique UUID) — matches this project's established tenant-scoping philosophy (never trust an id in isolation) and is what makes cross-tenant isolation trivially provable by construction of the key itself, proved directly in the tests by inspecting the cache's own stored entries, not just the HTTP outcome. **Cache-miss behavior**: a fresh DB read (one query each for `tenants.status` and `site_keys.status`, the latter also scoped by `tenant_id`) populates the cache with whatever it finds. **Deleted tenant/site-key**: `None` (row not found) is cached and treated as a plain, cacheable status value — it fails the "must be active/live" check the exact same way `"suspended"` does, and caching it too means a token for a permanently-deleted identity doesn't force a DB read on every single request either, matching the same "no DB hit per request" spirit as any other cached status.

  Tests (`test_dependencies.py`, 19 new, all live against the real test database, exercised through a small throwaway FastAPI app defined in the test file — `get_current_visitor` is not yet wired into any real endpoint, that begins at 1.4.i — using the same `httpx.AsyncClient`+`ASGITransport` pattern `test_session.py` already established): missing/malformed `Authorization` (6 shapes); expired/tampered-signature/wrong-key tokens reaching a 401 (wiring proof); a valid token replayed from a different Origin, and with a repeated Origin header; success for an active tenant + live site key, and separately for a draft site key; a suspended site key rejected; a suspended tenant's rejection proved to come from the CACHE specifically (not a coincidentally-matching fresh read): seed suspended → first request populates the cache as suspended and is rejected → flip the tenant to active directly in the DB, WITHOUT advancing the clock → a second request is STILL rejected (proving the stale cached value, not a fresh read, decided this) → advance the injectable clock past 60 seconds → a third request now succeeds (proving the TTL boundary itself, not some other mechanism, picks up the change); cross-tenant cache isolation, checked both by HTTP outcome and by directly inspecting the two tenants' distinct cache entries; a token for a never-seeded `vid`, and a token for a tenant deleted (cascade-deleting its visitor) after the token was issued — both a clean 401, never an unhandled exception.

UNCERTAIN: whether "no DB hit per request" (docs/SPEC.md §4.3) was meant to cover the one-time, cheap, PK-indexed `vid -> site_key_id` resolution too, or only the two STATUS reads the 60-second cache actually targets — built the narrower reading (only statuses are cached) since the resolved mapping is immutable and the lookup is already minimal; revisit if a later load-test surfaces this as a real hot-path cost. **Resolved at the duplication check after 1.4.f/g/h (its own B2)**: assessed directly rather than left open — the lookup is cheap, indexed and can never go stale, so a second cache would be over-engineering in the other direction; no further action needed, this marker is closed.   **C1** (`backend/app/main.py`): each successful disposal in `_lifespan()` now also clears that singleton's own `lru_cache` — `get_engine.cache_clear()` (paired with `_session_factory.cache_clear()`, a pairing every test fixture touching `DATABASE_URL` already does, since a stale cached sessionmaker would keep binding to the just-disposed engine object otherwise — the first production call site to need this) and `get_qdrant_client.cache_clear()`. Confirmed live, not assumed: a disposed `AsyncEngine` is safe to reuse (SQLAlchemy recreates its connection pool on the next checkout — a query after `dispose()` against the real test database succeeds normally); a closed `AsyncQdrantClient` is **not** — any call after `close()` raises `ResponseHandlingException: Cannot send a request, as the client has been closed`. Without the fix, a second lifespan cycle in the same process would have handed back that permanently-broken client. New test `test_lifespan_second_cycle_gets_fresh_engine_and_qdrant_client` (`test_main.py`): two full `lifespan_context` cycles in succession, touching both Postgres (`/ready`) and Qdrant (direct `get_qdrant_client()`, the same substitute the existing test (b) already uses) in each, asserting the second cycle's engine and client are different objects from the first's, and that the second Qdrant client specifically is not the first's closed one.

  **A discovered-and-fixed gap surfaced by C2's own live proof, not anticipated going in**: the first live-stack run showed neither expected log line at all. Read the installed uvicorn's own `uvicorn.config.LOGGING_CONFIG` directly: its default config (entrypoint.sh runs plain `uvicorn ... --factory`, no `--log-config`) attaches handlers only to its own `"uvicorn"`/`"uvicorn.access"` loggers, never the root logger — so `logger.info()`/`.exception()` calls anywhere in this application (the API process had no equivalent of `worker.py`'s own `logging.basicConfig()` call in its `main()`) were silently dropped by Python logging's WARNING-level "handler of last resort". - 2026-09-29: Step 1.5.a — pure `RateLimiter` (`backend/app/ratelimit.py`, new; `backend/tests/test_ratelimit.py`, new). Framework-agnostic: no imports from `fastapi`, `app.config`, or anything web-specific, matching `TenantScopedRepository`'s own layering precedent (`app/tenancy/repository.py`). Four decisions: (1) **sliding window log, not fixed window**: a fixed window lets a caller send up to 2x the limit in a burst straddling a window boundary (limit's worth just before, limit's worth just after — both buckets individually "under limit"), which is exactly the deliberate-abuse shape this exists to catch (docs/SPEC.md §9); a sliding window log (each key's own deque of recent request timestamps, pruned from the front as it goes) closes that gap directly. (2) **sliding-window-LOG chosen over token-bucket/leaky-bucket**: this project's own requirement is a flat "N per trailing window" count with no smoothing/burst-allowance requirement, so the log approach implements it with no extra tunables (bucket size, refill rate) to get wrong and no more code than the fixed-window version it replaces (rule 11) — no evidence a more elaborate algorithm is needed. (3) **limit/window fixed at construction, not per-check**: one `RateLimiter` instance is one policy; allowing different limits/windows per call for the *same* key would corrupt that key's own timestamp log's meaning (the log is only interpretable relative to one window). 1.5.c's `(IP, site_key)` policy and 1.5.e's broader per-IP policy are two different policies and will use two separate `RateLimiter` instances with disjoint key spaces, not one instance juggling two limits. (4) **rejected attempts are never recorded**: falls out of `check()`'s own ordering (the `len(attempts) >= limit` test happens strictly before the `append(now)` call) — no special-casing needed. Justification: a client that correctly backs off after a 429 must not have its next legitimate attempt further delayed by the rejected one; test (e) confirms this directly (a rejected attempt at t=2 does not push the recovery point past what the two genuine attempts at t=0/t=1 alone would require). (5) **memory bounding via full-sweep-per-check pruning**: `_prune_expired` sweeps every currently-tracked key on every single `check()` call (not just the key being checked), deleting any key whose deque empties out — pruning only the checked key would leak forever for a key that is checked exactly once and never rechecked, since nothing else would ever trigger its cleanup. Cost is O(number of currently-active keys) per check, judged acceptable at this project's realistic scale (one in-process limiter on a single API instance, docs/SPEC.md §9) — no LRU cap or background sweep thread built without evidence it's needed (rule 11). This sweep-every-key-on-every-check design is deliberate, not an oversight: it costs O(K) per `check()` call (K = currently-tracked distinct keys) regardless of which key is being checked, acceptable today (one endpoint, one API instance, small K) but flagged to reconsider before this limiter is reused for `/chat` in Step 4.1 — much higher call frequency (per message) and a larger K (all active visitors) would make that O(K)-per-check cost worth revisiting, e.g. via Redis (already docs/SPEC.md §17's named answer at scale). Injectable clock: `clock: Callable[[], float] = time.monotonic`, same pattern as Task 1.4.h's `StatusCache`. Filename/class name kept as originally planned: `app/ratelimit.py` (matching the existing single-word module convention — `app/qdrant.py`, `app/db.py`, `app/health.py`, `app/worker.py`), class `RateLimiter`.

  Tests (all offline, `_FakeClock` injectable-clock pattern from `test_dependencies.py`, no real sleeps, no database, no network): (a) exactly `limit` requests allowed, the `limit+1`th rejected; (b) a blocked key becomes allowed again once the full window elapses via `clock.advance()`; (c) the sliding-vs-fixed-window proof — `limit=10, window=60`, a full burst of 10 requests concentrated at t=1059.0–1059.9 (all allowed), then one more at t=1060.1 correctly rejected (a naive fixed window bucketing on `floor(t/60)` would wrongly allow this, since 1060.1 falls in a fresh bucket with its own empty counter — a sliding window's trailing-60s check from 1060.1 still contains the full burst); (d) two independent keys never share a counter; (e) a rejected attempt is not counted and does not further delay recovery, confirmed by advancing the clock to exactly when the two genuine (non-rejected) attempts alone would age out, not later; (f) memory bounding — 50 one-off keys each checked once (`len(limiter._attempts) == 50`), then the clock advances well past the window and one unrelated new key is checked, confirming all 50 stale entries are swept away in that single call (`len(limiter._attempts) == 1`, only the new key remains) — proving the full-sweep behavior, not just inspection.

  Verified: `make lint` clean; full `backend/tests` without a test database: **283 passed, 96 skipped** (up from 277 passed, 96 skipped — exactly the 6 new offline tests run for real, 0 new skips since nothing here needs a database or network); `backend/tests/test_ratelimit.py` run standalone and verbosely: all 6 pass individually; `git status --short` showed exactly the two expected new files (`backend/app/ratelimit.py`, `backend/tests/test_ratelimit.py`), `.env` correctly absent. No ASSUMPTION/UNCERTAIN markers needed — every design choice (sliding vs. fixed window, log vs. token/leaky bucket, construction-time vs. per-check limit/window, rejected-attempt semantics, the memory-bounding sweep) was fully specified by the task itself or resolved directly with justification recorded above; no open questions remain for 1.5.a's own scope. Checked first, as instructed — that assumption doesn't hold: `status_cache` is NOT initialized in `create_app()` at all; it is lazily created in `app/auth/dependencies.py`'s `_get_status_cache()` (getattr-fallback-then-construct-then-store on `app.state`, on first access). Followed that real, verified pattern instead: `_get_per_ip_limiter()`/`_get_site_key_limiter()` (new, `app/auth/routes.py`) use the identical shape, so `app/main.py` needed no changes at all. **Confirmed created once per app instance, not per request**: the getattr-fallback stores the constructed instance back onto `request.app.state`, and `request.app` is the same object across every request through one ASGI app/AsyncClient — proved directly, not just assumed, by `test_eleventh_request_from_one_site_key_within_the_window_is_429` and every other new live test in `test_session.py`, each of which only trips its 429 case because the SAME limiter instance's counter persisted across multiple prior requests in the same test.

  **Check order and reasoning**: per-IP limiter checked FIRST, then `(IP, site_key)` — both BEFORE the origin well-formedness check and all DB work, not just before DB work as literally asked: rate limiting is at least as cheap as the origin header check, and this is the worst-case abuse path (a flood), so "cheapest first" (docs/SPEC.md §9) is taken to its logical start rather than stopping at "before the database." Per-IP first because it is the broader, cheaper-to-reject check, and must trip even when no site-key-specific state exists yet (docs/SPEC.md §16's "many new visitors from one IP hit the IP cap") — proved directly by `test_per_ip_limiter_checked_first_and_can_trip_before_any_site_key_state_exists` and `test_per_ip_limiter_trips_across_many_different_site_keys`.

  **Key formats**: per-IP limiter key is `request.client.host` directly (a `None` client — possible per the ASGI spec though rare behind a real server — falls back to the literal string `"unknown"` rather than crashing). The `(IP, site_key)` limiter's key is `f"{len(ip)}:{ip}:{site_key}"` (`_site_key_rate_limit_key()`), a netstring-style length-prefix encoding, not naive concatenation: `site_key` is fully client-controlled (`SessionRequest.site_key` has no charset/length constraint) and read from the request body BEFORE any DB validation, so a plain `f"{ip}:{site_key}"` would let a crafted `site_key` value collide two different `(ip, site_key)` pairs onto the same combined string (e.g. `ip="1.2.3.4"`, `site_key="5:x"` vs. `ip="1.2.3.4:5"`, `site_key="x"`), letting one visitor's requests silently share, or poison, another's rate-limit counter — a real concern given this is a [SECURITY] task. Length-prefixing the IP makes the encoding provably injective regardless of what characters either part contains.

  **`RateLimiter.get_retry_after(key) -> float` added to `app/ratelimit.py`**, an already-Done module from 1.5.a — flagged clearly, as the task asked: a small, additive method (no existing behavior changed), returning a real countdown against the key's own oldest currently-counted timestamp (`0.0` for any key not currently at/over its limit, never a stale or misleading wait time for an unblocked key). Read-only, no pruning needed: a key that just failed `check()` was already pruned by that same call's own full sweep.

  **429 response** (`_rate_limited_response()`, `app/auth/routes.py`): a NEW, distinct shape from 1.4.g's 403 `_FAILURE_BODY`/`_FAILURE_STATUS` — never reused or extended, per Step 1.5 breakdown decision (d). Body: `{"detail": "too many requests", "retry_after": <float, rounded to 1 decimal place>}`. Header: `Retry-After: <integer seconds>`, rounded UP via `math.ceil` (never down, never to `0` — `max(1, ...)`) since RFC 9110 §10.2.3's "delay-seconds" form is a non-negative integer, and a client should never be told to retry a moment too early.

- 2026-09-30: Step 2.1 breakdown approved (docs-only, no code) — eight subtasks 2.1.a–2.1.h (see the Step 2.1 task list above), based on docs/SPEC.md §5.1–§5.3 (source types, storage, jobs/consistency), §5.6 (upload/database-connector security requirements, including "credentials are encrypted at rest"), §17 (queue-lag/multi-worker scale-out trigger) and §18's own Phase 2 step split (2.1 owns tables/queue only; 2.2–2.9 own the individual adapters/features). Five decisions confirmed:

  (a) **Table scope and field types**: 2.1 owns exactly `sources`, `documents`, `db_connections`, `jobs` — **`verified_domains` and `audit_log` are explicitly deferred to Step 2.2** (Domain verification `[SECURITY]`), not built now, even though §5.2 lists them in the same Postgres bullet: 2.2 is independently security-reviewed and the tables are meaningless without their own verification/audit logic, so building them ahead of that review would leave an unreviewed, unused `[SECURITY]`-adjacent surface sitting in the schema. `sources.config`: JSONB, matching `plans.limits`'s own precedent (1.2's decision (5) — most concrete fields are adapter-specific and not yet needed as structured columns). `jobs`: UUID PK (consistent with every other table so far) and **`source_id` nullable, a deliberate decision, not an afterthought** — reconcile and refresh-scheduling jobs are not always tied to one source row. `db_connections`' credential fields: **structured columns, not JSONB** — see the dedicated research below.

  **`db_connections` credential-column research** (done before building 2.1.a, as instructed): does `Settings`' `SecretStr` + guarded-accessor pattern (`qdrant_api_key_str()`, etc.) translate directly to a SQLAlchemy ORM column? **No — confirmed by reading the code, not assumed**: `SecretStr` is a pydantic `BaseModel` feature — pydantic's own `BaseModel.__repr__`/`__str__` dumps every field by default, which is exactly the failure mode `SecretStr` exists to mask. `backend/app/db.py`'s `Base(DeclarativeBase)` has **no custom `__repr__`** anywhere in this codebase (confirmed by grep) — SQLAlchemy's own declarative default `__repr__` is the plain `<ClassName object at 0x...>`, which does **not** dump column values. So the specific leak vector `SecretStr` defends against doesn't exist the same way for a plain ORM column with no custom `__repr__` — a `SecretStr`-equivalent *column type* would be solving a problem this layer doesn't actually have.

  The real risk surface for a DB-stored credential is different, and the proposed smallest correct equivalent addresses that surface instead, one layer down (repository/model, not Settings): (1) **encryption at rest**, spec-mandated explicitly (§5.6: "Credentials are encrypted at rest") — the actual primary defense, not an application-level nicety; mechanism (pgcrypto vs. application-level encryption, e.g. `cryptography`'s Fernet with a key sourced from a new required `Settings` field, matching every other secret's existing discipline) is **not decided here** — both are well-known/standard (rule 11), compared live at 2.1.a's own build time, not assumed from this planning session (matching 1.3.b's own "re-verify at build time" precedent for exactly this kind of choice); (2) a **guarded accessor method** on whichever repository owns `db_connections` access (e.g. a `get_decrypted_credentials()`-shaped method), mirroring `qdrant_api_key_str()`'s "one explicit call that unwraps the secret, never log this" pattern — never raw column access anywhere else; (3) a **new guard test**, the same AST-based allow-list mechanism `test_config_guard.py` already uses for `database_url_str()`/`qdrant_api_key_str()`/`jwt_signing_key_str()`, scoped to this new accessor instead — either extending that existing file or a small new sibling test, decided at 2.1.a's own build time; (4) **never include these columns in a raw dict/model dump**, matching `_public_widget_config()`'s own explicit-field-allow-list precedent (`app/auth/routes.py`) — relevant once an admin endpoint exists to list `db_connections` (Phase 6), noted here as a forward pointer, not built now.

  (b) **Job claiming mechanism**: `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` with the `pending → running` status flip in the same transaction — a well-known, standard Postgres queue pattern (rule 11), not a custom scheme. Confirmed necessary now, not merely future-proofing: docs/SPEC.md §17's own scale-out trigger table says "Queue lag above 5 minutes → Add worker instances (**same image**)" — multiple concurrent workers are an anticipated, spec-named event, so the claiming query must already be safe against double-claiming even though only one `worker` Compose service exists today.

  (c) **2.1 builds the real job-processing loop** (claim → dispatch-by-`job_type` → mark done/failed/retry), proven with a no-op/stub handler — not schema-and-enqueue-only. No real crawl/upload/database-sync logic is built here (that stays 2.4–2.8's job, per §18's own step split); the loop mechanism itself is what "job queue" in this step's own title means, matching `ensure_collection()`'s own precedent (1.3.c) of a real, fully-tested function built ahead of its eventual real caller.

  (d) **`ensure_collection()` wiring bundled into Step 2.1** as its own subtask (2.1.g), now that Phase 2 — specifically this step — is where it first becomes relevant (reassigned from Step 1.3.c's own marker at the Step 1.6 close-out, 2026-09-30).

The smallest correct equivalent, one layer down (repository, not Settings): (1) encryption at rest (§5.6 mandates this explicitly); (2) a guarded accessor method (`get_decrypted_credentials()`) mirroring `qdrant_api_key_str()`'s "one explicit call unwraps the secret" pattern; (3) a new AST-based guard test restricting which file may ever call it; (4) never including the column in a raw dict/model dump once an admin endpoint exists (Phase 6, noted as a forward pointer only).

  **pgcrypto vs. application-level encryption, decided from what was actually found live, not memory**: connected to the real pinned `postgres:18.2-trixie` test-db container and ran `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgcrypto` (succeeded) followed by a real `pgp_sym_encrypt`/`pgp_sym_decrypt` round trip (succeeded, correct plaintext recovered). **Chose pgcrypto** over application-level encryption (e.g. `cryptography`'s Fernet): zero new runtime dependencies (no rule-8 approval process, no lockfile/image-size impact — a genuine practical advantage, not just a tie-breaker), a well-known/standard mechanism (rule 11), and the passphrase is always passed as a bound `sqlalchemy.func` parameter, never string-interpolated (avoids both SQL injection and the key appearing in a query log). `encrypted_credentials` is one dedicated `LargeBinary` (BYTEA) column, not split per credential sub-field and not JSONB — BYTEA cannot be partially queried/introspected in SQL the way JSONB can (no `credentials->>'password'`-style bypass of the guarded accessor); the credential's own internal shape (username/password/etc.) is deliberately left undecided at the schema level, encoded inside the encrypted value itself, decided once Step 2.8's real adapter knows what it needs.

  **[SECURITY] A real, live leak found and fixed while writing the C1 coverage, not a pre-existing test gap being closed**: proving C1 required a genuine `IngestRepository` failure path, so a wrong/rotated `db_connection_encryption_key` decrypt attempt was reproduced live against the real test database. `str(exc)` on the resulting `sqlalchemy.exc.OperationalError` contained the literal key value, verbatim — SQLAlchemy's default `StatementError.__str__()` embeds every bound parameter of the failing statement (here, the key itself), and `app/db.py`'s `create_async_engine()` call does not set `hide_parameters=True`. This directly contradicted `get_decrypted_credentials()`'s own "never log this" comment: if this exception is ever logged (normal handling for an unhandled exception), the key ends up in logs. Flagged to the user before writing C1 as originally worded (asserting the key's absence), since that test would otherwise have failed, inverting its own purpose, and fixing the underlying leak was beyond this task's stated "behaviour must stay identical" scope without confirmation. User confirmed (2026-09-30): fix at the source (catch-and-re-raise-sanitized), not `hide_parameters=True` at any scope — an engine-wide setting would strip bound parameters from every exception across the whole app, including ordinary non-sensitive queries where seeing real parameter values is genuinely useful for debugging, too broad a trade for one credential-specific leak; a query-scoped `hide_parameters` was judged a blunter, less certain mechanism than handling it at the source.

  **Fix**: `backend/app/ingest/repository.py` gained a new `CredentialEncryptionError` exception class (module-level, following `app/auth/tokens.py`'s `InvalidSessionToken` convention — a fixed, generic message, documented as never containing the original exception's string form). Both `create_db_connection()` and `get_decrypted_credentials()` now catch `SQLAlchemyError` from their own `pgp_sym_encrypt`/`pgp_sym_decrypt` statement and re-raise `CredentialEncryptionError`, always **outside** the `except` block — confirmed live, before choosing this, that `raise ... from None` alone does **not** clear `__context__` (only `__suppress_context__`, which affects default traceback printing, not programmatic access), so a chain-walking check like this project's own `assert_secret_not_in_exception_chain` would still have found the leaked key via `__context__` even with `from None`. Raising after leaving the `except` block entirely (matching `decode_session_token()`'s own established, commented convention for the identical reason) avoids Python setting `__context__` at all. `create_db_connection()`'s encrypt statement received the identical treatment even though a well-formed, length-validated key cannot itself fail *encryption* (only decryption has a "wrong key" failure mode) — the leak mechanism is that the key is a bound parameter of that statement too, so any other exception during that same `execute()` call (a dropped connection, a timeout) would carry it the same way. No live test was written for the encrypt path specifically: unlike decrypt's clean, realistic "wrong key" trigger, there is no realistic way to force `pgp_sym_encrypt()` itself to fail with a well-formed key without an artificial, unrelated fault (e.g. severing the connection mid-statement), and this project's live-only testing discipline argues against fabricating one.
