- 2026-09-30: Step 2.1.c [SECURITY] — job-claiming primitives: `backend/app/ingest/queue.py` (new — `claim_next_job()`, `mark_job_succeeded()`, `mark_job_failed()`); `backend/app/ingest/repository.py` (extended — `IngestRepository.enqueue()`); `backend/app/config.py` (two new fields — `job_retry_base_seconds`, `job_max_attempts`); `backend/tests/test_queue.py` (new); `backend/tests/test_ingest_repository.py` (extended — the `enqueue()` test). `docker info` confirmed running first.

  **`enqueue()` vs. `claim_next_job()`/`mark_job_succeeded()`/`mark_job_failed()` placement, applying `create_tenant()`'s own precedent (app/tenancy/repository.py) in both directions**: `enqueue()` is an `IngestRepository` method — enqueueing always happens on behalf of an already-known tenant's own action (a source being added, a scheduled refresh), so a real `tenant_id` is always already available, the same reasoning `create_visitor()`/`create_conversation()` already rely on. The other three are standalone functions in a new sibling module, `queue.py`, not `IngestRepository` methods: the worker claims and marks jobs across every tenant, with no tenant identity at all at call time, so they structurally cannot fit `IngestRepository`'s own invariant (construction always requires a real `tenant_id`) — the exact inverse of why `create_tenant()`/`get_site_key_by_key()` are standalone (no tenant identity *yet*, there; no tenant identity *ever*, here).

  **Claiming mechanism, verified live, not assumed**: a single `UPDATE jobs SET status='running', updated_at=now() WHERE id = (SELECT id FROM jobs WHERE status='pending' AND next_run_at <= now() ORDER BY next_run_at LIMIT 1 FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED) RETURNING *` — the standard, well-known Postgres queue idiom (rule 11), constructed in SQLAlchemy as an ORM-enabled `update(Job).where(Job.id == select(Job.id)...with_for_update(skip_locked=True).limit(1).scalar_subquery()).values(...).returning(Job)`, executed via `session.execute(stmt).scalar_one_or_none()`. Chosen over a separate `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` followed by a second `UPDATE` statement: one statement is inherently "read the next row and flip it in the same transaction," with no window between the two for a second concurrent caller to see the same row as still pending. Compiled SQL rendered and inspected by hand first (confirming `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` lands after `ORDER BY ... LIMIT 1`, Postgres's required clause order), then executed live against the real test database and confirmed to return a genuine, correctly-flipped `Job` ORM instance with a real read-back. `claim_next_job()` does not commit internally — matching every other repository/service function in this codebase (only `app/auth/routes.py`'s own endpoint-level code calls `session.commit()`); the caller (2.1.d's worker loop, or a test) owns the transaction boundary. This does not weaken the concurrency guarantee: Postgres holds a `FOR UPDATE` row lock from the moment the statement executes, not from commit, so concurrent callers are already correctly serialized before anyone commits — and a caller that crashes before committing simply rolls back, automatically restoring the job to "pending" for the next claimer, an unplanned but useful self-healing property of a real transaction over a hand-rolled `claimed_by` column.

  **The concurrency proof itself (the one that actually matters, per this task's own framing)**: `test_queue.py::test_claim_next_job_concurrency_proof` seeds 10 real pending jobs, then runs 13 concurrent `claim_next_job()` calls via `asyncio.gather()`, each on its own `db_session()` (a genuinely separate connection/transaction, not a sequential loop simulating concurrency) — asserts all 10 jobs were claimed, each exactly once (no double-claim, no silent skip), and the 3 oversubscribed callers cleanly received `None`. Re-run 8 times standalone before being folded into the suite; stable every time. A hand-written, throwaway version of the same proof (15 concurrent callers against 14 genuinely pending rows, due to leftover state from earlier manual verification) was run first, live, before writing the permanent test — 14 unique claims, 1 clean `None`, 0 double-claims, matching this task's own "prove it live, not just in the permanent test" discipline.

  **Backoff formula and its defaults**: `next_run_at = clock() + timedelta(seconds=base_seconds * 2**attempts)`, `attempts` counted *after* incrementing for the current failure, using an injectable clock (`clock: Callable[[], datetime] = lambda: datetime.now(UTC)`, matching `RateLimiter`'s own `clock: Callable[[], float] = time.monotonic` pattern, `app/ratelimit.py`) so a test can assert the exact resulting timestamp. `job_retry_base_seconds` defaults to 60 and `job_max_attempts` (see below) defaults to 5 — together giving retries at 2/4/8/16 minutes before the job becomes permanently `failed`, a reasonable schedule for a background ingestion job (crawl/upload/database-sync/reconcile): nothing user-facing or latency-sensitive is waiting on it, unlike docs/SPEC.md §14's email-retry precedent (5 attempts, ~1 min to 12 h) which this deliberately does not copy verbatim — email's own schedule spans hours because a human eventually reads the result; a job's own worker loop just needs to not hammer a transient failure.

  **`jobs.max_attempts` already existed as a per-row schema column (2.1.a, `server_default=5`) — reconciled with this task's own "Settings-configurable" ask, not left as a conflict**: `Settings.job_max_attempts` (new, default 5) is read once, by `enqueue()`, to set each new job row's own `max_attempts` column explicitly at creation time. `mark_job_failed()` then compares `job.attempts >= job.max_attempts` against that row's own already-persisted value, never a fresh `get_settings()` read — so changing the setting later can never retroactively shift an already-created job's own retry ceiling. The column's DB-level `server_default=5` stays in place, now purely as an inert defensive floor for any insert that bypasses `enqueue()`, no longer the actual source of truth for the sanctioned path. Flagged to the user before building, per rule 11 ("if anything looks wrong, say so first") — confirmed as the right reconciliation, not a real conflict.

  **`app/ingest/service.py` retired, not left as dead scaffolding**: its one Step 1.1b stub, `enqueue_ingestion_job()`, is now genuinely superseded by `IngestRepository.enqueue()` — a different function, in a different file, not a same-named implementation landing inside `service.py` itself (unlike `app/tenancy/repository.py`'s or `app/auth/routes.py`'s own stub retirements, where the real code replaced the stub in the same file). Deleted outright rather than left empty; its entry removed from `test_app_skeleton.py`'s `STUB_ENTRY_POINTS` list, with a one-line note added there (matching that list's own existing retirement-note convention for `app.tenancy.repository`/`app.auth.routes`). A real `service.py` can be recreated fresh whenever a later ingestion step (2.2+) actually needs one for adapter/business logic that doesn't fit `models.py`/`repository.py`/`queue.py`.

  ASSUMPTION: the exact backoff numbers (`job_retry_base_seconds=60`, `job_max_attempts=5`) are this task's own reasonable proposal, not spec-mandated — docs/SPEC.md §5.3 only says "retried with backoff," no formula or attempt count. Both are ordinary Settings fields with defaults, so easy to retune later without a migration. No UNCERTAIN markers: every design question in this task's own scope was resolved with recorded reasoning above, not left open. - 2026-10-01: Step 2.1.d — wire `worker.py`'s `run()` to the real claim → dispatch-by-`job_type` → mark succeeded/failed loop. `backend/app/worker.py` (rewritten — resolves the TODO(2.1)/TODO(2.1.d) markers named above); `backend/app/config.py` (new `worker_poll_interval_seconds` field); **`backend/app/ingest/queue.py` (extended — `mark_job_failed()` gains a `permanent` parameter, see below, a change to 2.1.c's own already-Done function)**; `backend/tests/test_worker.py` (extended, not forked into a new file — it already owned `run()`'s own tests since 1.1.f). `docker info` confirmed running first.

  **Registry shape**: a plain module-level dict, `JOB_HANDLERS: dict[str, Callable[[dict], Awaitable[None]]]`, one entry (`"noop"`) registered — not a decorator-based registration mechanism, which would be real abstraction for a registry holding exactly one handler (rule 11). Revisit only if a later step needs registration split across multiple files.

  **Stop-for-testing mechanism**: a new `max_iterations: int | None = None` parameter on the existing `run(stop, max_iterations=None)`, composing with — not replacing — 1.1.f's own pre-existing `stop: asyncio.Event` parameter. Chosen because `stop` already exists and several 1.1.f tests already depend on its exact current behavior (`test_run_returns_promptly_when_stop_is_already_set` calls `run(stop)` with `stop` already set, expecting an immediate return with no database touched at all — preserved exactly, since the loop's own `while not stop.is_set():` check happens before anything else). `max_iterations` bounds the loop to exactly N full iterations (claim attempt, whether hit or miss, counted once each) regardless of `stop`, which is what every new test needed: enqueue one job, `run(stop=asyncio.Event(), max_iterations=1)`, assert the outcome.

  **A real, found-live risk to an existing test, fixed before it could break**: `test_sigterm_shuts_down_the_real_process_cleanly` (1.1.f) spawns the real `python -m app.worker` subprocess using `VALID_ENV`'s own placeholder `DATABASE_URL` (`postgresql+psycopg://user:pw@localhost:5432/widgetplatform`, port 5432) — confirmed live that nothing listens on port 5432 in this dev environment, and that once `run()` genuinely touches the database, connecting there raises `psycopg.OperationalError: ... Connection refused` in about 1.3s (fast, not a hang; confirmed the message contains only host/port, no credentials). Before this task, `run()` never touched the database at all, so this test never depended on one being reachable; this task's own loop would have made it crash on its first iteration, before ever receiving the test's SIGTERM, turning a previously-DB-independent, always-passing test into a flaky/broken one. Fixed by design, not by changing the test: the per-iteration claim/process step is wrapped in a broad `try/except Exception`, logging only the exception's *type* (never `str(exc)` — unlike 2.1.a/b's own pgcrypto statement-level leak, a connection-level failure's message has not been proven safe, so this stays defensively conservative rather than assuming it is), then treated exactly like "nothing was ready": sleep the poll interval, try again. This is also just correct production behavior independent of the test — a worker must survive a transient database outage, not crash entirely. `test_sigterm_shuts_down_the_real_process_cleanly` now exercises this exact path for real (port 5432 closed) and still passes, serving as this mechanism's own live proof; no dedicated new test was added purely to simulate a DB outage (out of this task's explicit test scope).

  **Idle-poll interval, default, and how it's tested**: new `Settings.worker_poll_interval_seconds` (default 2, `gt=0`, same validation discipline as every other configurable field) — deliberately shorter than `job_retry_base_seconds`'s 60-second floor, since this is "is anything ready yet," not a retry delay. The sleep itself is `asyncio.wait_for(stop.wait(), timeout=worker_poll_interval_seconds)`, not a plain `asyncio.sleep()`: it wakes immediately on `stop` being set (preserving SIGTERM responsiveness during an idle poll, not regressing 1.1.f's own instant-shutdown guarantee) while still bounding the wait to the configured interval. Tested against a small **real** interval (0.2s, via `monkeypatch.setenv` + `get_settings.cache_clear()`), measuring real wall-clock elapsed time with generous tolerance bounds (`>= 0.8x`, `< 2.0s`) — not an injected fake clock/sleep: `asyncio.wait_for`'s own internal timing would be awkward to fake without monkeypatching asyncio itself, and faking it would prove a different code path than production actually runs. At 0.2s this is not meaningfully flake-prone (confirmed: run 5 times standalone, stable every time).

  **`mark_job_failed()` gains `permanent: bool = False` — a change to 2.1.c's own already-Done function, flagged explicitly, not made quietly**: found while building this task's own unknown-`job_type` test — 2.1.c's `mark_job_failed()` applies the exact same backoff logic to every error uniformly, so an unknown `job_type` would only reach `"failed"` after `max_attempts` (5) identical, guaranteed-to-repeat failures and their accumulated backoff delay (~30 minutes), never immediately, conflicting with this task's own stated test expectation ("ends up 'failed'" after one run). Flagged to the user rather than silently picking either interpretation. User's decision: add `permanent` to the existing function rather than a second, near-duplicate `mark_job_failed_permanently()` (one function with a branch, not two copies of the job-lookup/error/`updated_at` plumbing) — `permanent=False` (the default) is 2.1.c's original behavior, byte-for-byte unchanged (re-confirmed: all 8 of `test_queue.py`'s own 2.1.c tests still pass unmodified, with zero changes to that file); `permanent=True` sets `status="failed"` immediately without touching `attempts`/`next_run_at`, used **only** for "no handler registered for this `job_type` at all" (a structural, non-transient error — retrying can never succeed). `attempts` is deliberately left untouched (not incremented) for the permanent case: it counts real handler-execution attempts, and none was made — there was no handler to run. A handler that raises during execution keeps the normal, non-permanent backoff path unconditionally (that failure could be transient) — the two cases are never conflated; `test_run_marks_a_raising_handler_failed_and_keeps_looping`'s own `attempts == 1` assertion is itself proof it took the non-permanent branch.

- 2026-10-01: Step 2.1.e — worker graceful shutdown. `backend/app/worker.py` (extended — a `"sleep"` test handler, the `app.tenancy`/`app.plans` model-import fix below, a per-claim log line, an explanatory comment on the loop itself); `backend/tests/test_worker.py` (extended — the real SIGTERM-mid-handler proof). `docker info` confirmed running first.

  **Design questions, answered before building, per the task's own request**: (1) option (a) confirmed — let the current job's handler finish, then exit without claiming another; pushing back was considered but there is no problem with it (handlers in 2.4-2.8 could be long-running, and a forced mid-write kill is a worse outcome than letting a crawl/upload finish). (2) confirmed out of scope: a job stuck in `"running"` forever because its own worker crashed hard (SIGKILL/OOM, no chance for graceful shutdown to run at all) is a separate problem needing a stuck-job reaper/lease-timeout mechanism, not anything a clean-SIGTERM handler can address — recorded as a new Open marker (see below), not built here.

  **The actual finding: no functional change was needed in `run()`'s own loop.** `while not stop.is_set():` (2.1.d) is already re-evaluated fresh at the top of every iteration, and `stop.set()` (the SIGTERM callback `main()` registers via `loop.add_signal_handler`) is a plain cooperative flag — it never cancels an in-flight `await _claim_and_process_one_job()`. So a signal arriving while a handler is actively running is only ever observed at the next iteration boundary, after that job has already run to completion and been marked succeeded/failed — exactly "finish what you started, claim nothing new," already true before this task touched a single line of loop logic. This was **confirmed live, not assumed**: a real `python -m app.worker` subprocess, a job whose handler sleeps, a real SIGTERM sent while the DB row's own status is polled to confirm `"running"`, repeated cleanly multiple times. The task's own two "design questions to resolve first" turned out to already be correctly answered by 2.1.d's own existing structure; the real work this task did was proving that rigorously and documenting the invariant explicitly in the code (a comment on the loop itself) so a future reader doesn't have to rediscover it by reasoning from scratch.

  **A new `"sleep"` test handler, not a hidden parameter on `noop`**: `_sleep_handler(payload)` awaits `asyncio.sleep(payload.get("seconds", 0))`, registered under `job_type="sleep"`. Needed because no *existing* handler could ever give the proof above a window to exercise — `noop` returns before the event loop gets a chance to do anything else. A second, explicitly-named registry entry (rather than overloading `_noop_handler` with a hidden sleep parameter) was chosen so a reader encountering `job_type="sleep"` understands its purpose (a controllable-delay utility) without needing to already know `noop` secretly supports a payload key — the job-queue literature's own common "debug sleep job" pattern for exactly this kind of worker-behavior testing.

  **A real, independent bug found live while first building the proof, not a defect in the shutdown mechanism**: the very first attempt at the live proof raised `sqlalchemy.exc.NoReferencedTableError` on `jobs.tenant_id`'s FK during `mark_job_succeeded()`'s own flush. Root cause: a genuine standalone `python -m app.worker` process has nothing that ever imports `app.tenancy.models`/`app.plans.models`, so neither table's mapper is registered on `Base.metadata` when SQLAlchemy needs to resolve that FK at flush time — the exact same class of bug `alembic/env.py` already hit and was fixed for at Task 2.1.b (the "every domain's models must be imported somewhere, or cross-table resolution silently breaks" gap `test_alembic_env_imports_guard.py` now guards `alembic/env.py` against specifically, but nothing equivalent covers `worker.py`). 2.1.d's own tests never caught this because every one of them calls `run()` directly inside pytest, where some other already-imported test module had always registered every domain's models first — a genuine standalone worker process was never actually exercised with a real job before this task's own live proof. Fixed with the identical import-for-side-effect fix as `alembic/env.py`'s own (`from app.plans import models as plans_models  # noqa: F401`, `from app.tenancy import models as tenancy_models  # noqa: F401`), documented in `worker.py` with the same reasoning. UNCERTAIN, noted rather than silently resolved: should a 2.1.c-style guard test (comparing `app/*/models.py` packages against a scanned import list, mirroring `test_alembic_env_imports_guard.py`) be generalized to cover `worker.py` too, now that it is a second real entrypoint with the identical "every model must be registered" requirement — not built here (out of this task's own stated scope), left for the duplication check this task's own counter reaching n=3 triggers.

  **A debugging detour, not a code finding, worth recording so it is not repeated**: the first live-proof attempt showed a second job (seeded `"pending"`, expected to stay that way) ending up `"running"` and permanently stuck. Traced to an **orphaned `python -m app.worker` process from an earlier, interrupted debugging attempt in this same session**, still alive in the background (reparented to PID 1) and independently polling the same shared test database — not a flaw in this task's own code. Killed by hand (`kill -9`), confirmed via `ps aux` that no `app.worker` process was running before each subsequent attempt, and the proof then behaved exactly as expected. Recorded so a future investigator seeing a mysteriously-claimed job in a live test knows to check for a lingering subprocess from a prior interrupted run before suspecting the code under test.

  ASSUMPTION: none beyond what 2.1.c/2.1.d already recorded. UNCERTAIN: see the "generalize the models-import guard to worker.py" note above — left open for the duplication check, not resolved here. TODO: none new; the stuck-job-reaper concern is recorded as an Open marker (below), not a TODO, since no task yet owns building it.

- 2026-10-01: Duplication check after 2.1.c/d/e: replaced the per-entrypoint model-import guard with a single shared `all_models.py` module that `alembic/env.py` and `worker.py` both import, eliminating the need to remember registering future entrypoints; fixed a real bug where `worker.py`'s per-iteration failure handler logged full exception messages via `exc_info=True` despite its own comment claiming otherwise — a latent credential-leak risk now closed and proven with a live log-capture test; consolidated a duplicated test fixture and an inline query helper; A3/A4/A5/B1/B2/C2/C3 declined as already correct or appropriately deferred.

  **D (model-import guard)**: new `backend/app/all_models.py` — imports `app.ingest.models`/`app.plans.models`/`app.tenancy.models`, each `# noqa: F401`, for the side effect of registering every domain's tables on `Base.metadata`. `alembic/env.py` and `app/worker.py` each replaced their own separate per-domain import block with `from app import all_models  # noqa: F401`. Not a generalized "check every entrypoint imports every model" guard: that still requires each *future* entrypoint to remember to register itself with the guard; removing the thing being checked (one shared import point) is the smaller, more durable fix. `test_alembic_env_imports_guard.py` renamed to `test_all_models_imports_guard.py` (functions renamed to match: `_packages_imported_in_env_py` → `_packages_imported_in_all_models_py`, `ENV_PY` → `ALL_MODELS_PY` pointing at `app/all_models.py`); its own already-path-parameterized design (`env_py: Path = ENV_PY`, used against `tmp_path` fakes in its unit tests) made the repoint trivial. Proved live, scratch-copy, real files untouched: a fake `all_models.py` missing the `ingest` import correctly reports `missing == {'ingest'}`. Both downstream proofs re-run and confirmed clean: `alembic upgrade head` on a fresh test database (all three migrations apply); `alembic revision --autogenerate` against the fully-migrated schema produces an empty `upgrade()`/`downgrade()` (confirms `target_metadata` sees every table via `all_models.py`, scratch migration file deleted after); 2.1.e's own live SIGTERM-mid-handler test re-run and still passes.

  **C1 (the real bug)**: `worker.py`'s per-iteration `except Exception:` branch called `logger.exception(...)`, which is `logger.error(msg, exc_info=True)` — this logs the full exception message and traceback, directly contradicting its own comment ("log only the exception's type, never str(exc)"). Verified live before fixing: `logger.exception()` on `ValueError("SECRET-PASSWORD-12345")` printed the literal secret. Fixed to `logger.error("worker: unexpected failure claiming/processing a job (%s)", type(exc).__name__)` — no `exc_info`, matching the comment's own original intent exactly. New test (`test_worker.py`): monkeypatches `claim_next_job` to raise `ValueError("DISTINCTIVE-FAKE-SECRET-98765")` before any real query runs (no live database needed — SQLAlchemy connects lazily, confirmed this test runs in the offline suite), captures log output via `caplog`, asserts the secret string is absent from `caplog.text` while a log entry naming `ValueError` is still present. Proved the test would have caught the original bug: temporarily reverted just the fix via `git stash`, re-ran the test, confirmed it fails with the secret string visible in the captured log output, then restored the fix and reconfirmed green.

  **A1/A2 (fixture/helper consolidation)**: `_seeded_tenant` (a one-tenant seed fixture, byte-identical in `test_queue.py`/`test_worker.py` bar the tenant's own `name` string) moved to `conftest.py`, referenced as a bare fixture parameter in both files (never imported explicitly — matches `reset_test_database`'s own established convention, not a new pattern). `_fetch_job()` (already a local helper in `test_worker.py`, repeated inline seven times in `test_queue.py`) also moved to `conftest.py`; `test_queue.py` now calls it for its six standalone read-only lookups. The seventh inline occurrence (`test_mark_job_failed_becomes_permanently_failed_at_max_attempts`) was deliberately left inline: it fetches a row to *mutate and commit within the same transaction*, not a standalone read, so `_fetch_job()`'s read-only, fresh-session contract doesn't fit without adding a `session.merge()` step that would be more complex than what it replaces — noted in that test's own comment. `conftest.py` itself gained `from app import all_models  # noqa: F401`, needed for `_seeded_tenant`'s own `Tenant(...)` insert (`tenants.plan_id`'s FK) to resolve correctly on its own, not merely by accident because some other already-collected test module happened to import `app.plans.models` first — the identical fragility class `all_models.py` exists to remove. The now-redundant explicit `from app.plans import models as plans_models  # noqa: F401` lines in `test_queue.py`/`test_worker.py` were removed as a direct, necessary consequence of this move (conftest.py's own import already guarantees it for the whole session; leaving them would be confusing dead weight, not a behavior change).

  Declined, no change: **A3/A4/A5** (the injectable-clock mechanisms in `test_queue.py`/`test_worker.py` are genuinely different — one real DI with a fixed-point lambda, one deliberately exercising the real `asyncio.wait_for`/`time.monotonic()` path rather than faking it — nothing to unify; `claim_next_job()`'s `with_for_update`/`scalar_subquery`/`.returning()` construction is unique in `app/`, not a repeated pattern; the three new Settings fields already match the rate-limiter fields' own `Field(default=X, gt=0)` shape exactly). **B1** (`enqueue()`'s own lack of `job_type` validation is deliberate — no reserved-word concept exists yet to validate against; a bad value already fails cleanly via the existing unknown-job_type path; revisit once 2.4-2.8 establishes a real naming convention). **B2** (the per-iteration catch-by-type-only design itself was correct; C1 fixed the implementation bug that contradicted it, not the design). **C2** (no test that a job's `payload`/`error` is excluded from logs on failure — deferred until a real handler with sensitive payload content exists; speculative today). **C3** (two genuinely separate worker loops: 2.1.c's own 13-concurrent-caller proof already covers this — `claim_next_job()` is the only mechanism any two workers would ever contend on, so a literal two-process test adds no new coverage over two concurrent callers within one test).

- 2026-10-01: Step 2.1.f — worker healthcheck. `backend/app/worker.py` (extended — `HEARTBEAT_PATH`, `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER`, `_write_heartbeat()`, `check_heartbeat_fresh()`, wired into `run()`'s own loop); `deploy/docker-compose.yml` (worker service gains a real `healthcheck:`, resolves the Step-2.1-owned marker); `backend/tests/test_worker.py` (3 new tests). `docker info` confirmed running first.

  **Mechanism**: a file-based heartbeat, since the worker has no HTTP server (unlike the api service's own `/ready`, Task 1.4.j). `HEARTBEAT_PATH = Path("/tmp/worker-heartbeat")` — not an arbitrary choice: the container runs `read_only: true` with a tmpfs `/tmp` (`x-app-hardening`, applied to worker too), so `/tmp` is close to the only writable location available. The file's own content is the write time itself (`time.time()`, plain text), not reliance on the filesystem's mtime — a human or script can `cat` it and understand it immediately, and it sidesteps any mtime-semantics subtlety across tmpfs/`exec`-into-container edge cases. `run()`'s loop calls `_write_heartbeat(HEARTBEAT_PATH)` as the very first statement inside its existing per-iteration `try:` block — before the claim attempt, every iteration, including an idle one with nothing to claim: an "only on a claimed job" heartbeat could not distinguish idle-but-alive from wedged, since a worker with no pending jobs for a while would look identical to one that stopped iterating entirely. A write failure is caught by the same existing exception handler as a claim/mark failure (no second try/except): a worker that cannot write its own heartbeat is arguably unhealthy in a real sense too, not just logging-wise. `HEARTBEAT_PATH` is referenced by its global name at the call site, not left to `_write_heartbeat()`'s own default parameter — confirmed live that a default argument's value is bound once, at function-definition time, so monkeypatching the module attribute alone would not otherwise reach a call relying on the default.

  **Freshness threshold**: `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER = 3`, multiplied by `worker_poll_interval_seconds` at check time (6s with the 2s default) — a multiple of that setting, not a fixed number of seconds, since the heartbeat is written once per iteration and an idle iteration's own wait is already bounded by that same value. 3x covers one full normal idle-wait between writes plus headroom for an occasional slow iteration (a DB round-trip hiccup, a GC pause), comfortably under `x-healthcheck-timing`'s own `retries*interval=25s` detection floor — this threshold, not Docker's own probe schedule, is what actually decides "stale". `check_heartbeat_fresh()` is the one function both the real Docker healthcheck (`deploy/docker-compose.yml`'s own `python3 -c "..."` one-liner, matching the api service's own no-curl-in-the-image convention) and this file's own tests call — not duplicated shell-script and Python freshness logic that could silently drift apart.

  **ASSUMPTION, recorded in `worker.py` itself**: a genuinely long-running handler (2.4-2.8) that runs longer than `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER * worker_poll_interval_seconds` would make the heartbeat go stale while the worker is still healthy, just busy — the heartbeat is written once per iteration *start*, not progressively during a handler's own execution. Not a problem today: every registered handler (`noop`, `sleep`, both test/proof-only) is instant or a short, test-controlled duration. Revisit once a genuinely long-running handler exists: either it must report its own progress by writing the heartbeat periodically during its own work, or this threshold must grow to cover the longest expected single-job duration.

  **Live Compose proof**: `make up` (after a transient, unrelated first-attempt build failure — a pip `--require-hashes` hash-verification error on retry-cached layers, gone on a clean rebuild, not reproducible, not related to this task's own changes) — worker reached `(healthy)` within 11 seconds of container start. Wedge simulated via `docker kill --signal=SIGSTOP widgetplatform-worker-1` (freezes the worker's own PID 1 without touching the container or its separate healthcheck-probe processes, which run as distinct `docker exec` invocations in the same PID namespace and are unaffected by a SIGSTOP targeted at PID 1 alone) — confirmed the heartbeat file genuinely stopped advancing (unchanged across a 4-second poll) before watching: polled `docker compose ps` every 3s, observed the transition from `(healthy)` to `(unhealthy)` at the ~12-second mark into the poll loop (consistent with the ~6s staleness threshold plus Docker's own 5-consecutive-failing-probes requirement). `docker kill --signal=SIGCONT` resumed the process; recovered to `(healthy)` within one probe cycle (~3s) — Docker only requires a single successful probe to flip back healthy, unlike the 5 consecutive failures needed to flip unhealthy, a standard, expected asymmetry, not something this task needed to build. A genuine, unrelated finding surfaced mid-proof: the worker logged a real `ProgrammingError` every idle iteration because `make up` does not run migrations (`migrate` is `profiles: ["tools"]`, run explicitly) — the fresh `postgres` volume had no `jobs` table yet. Confirmed this is exactly what it looked like (not a regression): running `docker compose run --rm migrate` made the errors stop immediately, and the worker was correctly reported `(healthy)` throughout, even while every claim attempt failed — proving the heartbeat's own intended property live, not just in a test: liveness and job-processing success are genuinely different things, and this mechanism only ever claims the former. `make down`: clean, `docker ps -a` confirms no leftover containers.

  ASSUMPTION: see above (long-running-handler threshold gap), recorded in `worker.py`'s own comment, not left only here. No UNCERTAIN markers: every design question in this task's own scope was resolved with recorded reasoning. No new TODO: the Step-2.1-owned worker-healthcheck marker is resolved and removed from Open markers below, not left open.

The 3 new tests re-run 5 times standalone first, stable every time (after fixing a real test-design flake: shrinking `WORKER_POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS` to 0.05s for test speed also shrinks `check_heartbeat_fresh()`'s own threshold to 0.15s, too tight for the assertion that follows `run()`'s own return to reliably land inside — widened to 0.5s, comfortably clear of ordinary test-process overhead). `docker compose config --quiet` succeeds. Found five High-severity matches, all Debian `.deb` OS packages from the `python:3.12.14-slim-trixie` base image (none in `backend/requirements.lock` — `make lock-upgrade` would not have touched any of these), all with a fixed version already published:

  Root cause, confirmed live before building anything: `python:3.12.14-slim-trixie`'s own last build has not yet picked up these `trixie-security` patches. Force-pulled the exact pinned tag — Docker reported a newer manifest, but `dpkg -l` inside the freshly-pulled image still showed the same vulnerable `...u2` versions, so this is not a stale-local-cache issue; the upstream image tag itself hasn't been rebuilt against the patched packages yet. Separately confirmed, via `apt-get update && apt-cache policy` run live inside the base image, that the patched `...u3` versions ARE already published and resolvable from Debian's own `deb.debian.org/debian-security trixie-security/main` repo — the fix exists upstream, just not yet baked into this image tag.

  **Fix applied**: `backend/Dockerfile`'s final stage gained one new `RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends --only-upgrade <pkg>=<exact ...u3 version> ... && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*` step, placed after the existing pip-uninstall/ensurepip-removal step and before `WORKDIR /app` (OS-level hardening grouped together, before any application code is copied in, standard layer-ordering practice). Pinned to the exact patched version for each of the five packages (`openssl`, `libssl3t64`, `openssl-provider-legacy`, `libpcre2-8-0`), not a bare `--only-upgrade` with no version and not a blanket `apt-get upgrade` (which would pull in an unbounded, unreviewed set of changes) — this fails loudly at build time if a pinned version ever disappears from the repo, rather than silently installing whatever happens to be newest. Apt lists cleaned up in the same `RUN` layer, the standard Debian/Docker convention (this Dockerfile had no prior `apt-get` usage to match an existing convention against; this establishes the first one).

  Verified: rebuilt the image fresh, re-ran the identical grype invocation — all 5 High findings gone; only Medium/Low/Negligible Python-stdlib CVEs remain (the same ones already present before this fix, unrelated, below the `--fail-on high` threshold). `dpkg -l` inside the new image confirms all four package names now report `...u3`. `sh deploy/smoke-image.sh widgetplatform-backend:scan`: all 8 checks pass, unaffected. `make lint` clean. Full `backend/tests` without a test database: **329 passed, 126 skipped** (unchanged — no Python/test code touched). Image size: **89.59 MB → 93.19 MB** (+3.6 MB), confirmed via a side-by-side rebuild of the pre-patch Dockerfile under a throw-away tag, compared, then deleted — small and expected, these are point-patches, not major version bumps. `git status --short`: exactly `backend/Dockerfile` and `PROJECT_SPEC.md`, `.env` correctly absent.

  Open marker added (owned by the existing Step 1.1.o manual maintenance routine, not a new task/subtask — see below): once a newer build of `python:3.12.14-slim-trixie` bakes these five package patches in natively, this entire `apt-get --only-upgrade` block becomes redundant and must be removed outright, not left in as a harmless no-op (an explicit version pin against a now-older-than-base-image version would start failing loudly once the base image itself ships something newer than `...u3`, forcing the review rather than silently drifting).

- 2026-10-03: Step 2.1.g — `ensure_collection()` wiring. `docker info` confirmed the Docker engine running before starting.

  **Who calls it, and why (confirmed, not just proposed)**: `migrate` (the existing Compose deploy step), not the worker's own startup path. `migrate` already is exactly the "run idempotent setup steps once before the stack comes up" service (it already runs `alembic upgrade head` this way); `ensure_collection()` (1.3.c) is the same kind of one-shot idempotent setup step, just for Qdrant instead of Postgres. The worker's `run()` is a long-running poll loop, not a setup phase — wrong place for a setup step that should happen once per deploy, not be re-checked (or worse, race another worker replica checking it) on every process start. This resolves the open marker recorded at 1.3.c (reassigned to Step 2.1 at the 1.6 close-out) — see Open markers below.

  **Mechanism**: `backend/entrypoint.sh`'s existing `migrate` case gains a second step: `alembic upgrade head` (no longer `exec`'d — only the final command in the script should be, so it becomes PID 1) followed by `exec python -m app.qdrant`. `set -eu` (already present) means a failed migration aborts the script with Alembic's own exit code before `app.qdrant` ever runs. `app/qdrant.py` gains a `main()` / `if __name__ == "__main__":` CLI entrypoint (`asyncio.run()` over a small `_ensure_collection_and_close()` coroutine: `get_qdrant_client()` → `ensure_collection()` → `client.close()`), mirroring `app/worker.py`'s own `main()` error-handling style exactly — a clean one-line message to stderr and `sys.exit(1)` on `SettingsError` or `CollectionSchemaMismatch`, never a traceback (both exceptions' own messages are already proven free of secrets, confirmed by re-reading their docstrings before relying on that). Built as an addition to `app/qdrant.py` itself, not a new module: that file is already the one place `test_qdrant_read_path_guard.py`'s `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST` lets hold a real Qdrant client and own `ensure_collection()` — a new caller file would need its own deliberate, reviewed addition to that allow-list (as the guard's own header comment anticipates for a future file, e.g. Step 2.5's ingestion writer) for no benefit here, since the function and the one thing that now calls it can simply live together. `deploy/docker-compose.yml`'s `migrate` service gains `qdrant: condition: service_healthy` alongside its existing `postgres` dependency (api/worker already depend on both) — a real gap found while wiring this, not cosmetic: `migrate` now genuinely needs Qdrant reachable, and `make migrate`/`docker compose run --rm migrate` without a prior `up` would otherwise race a not-yet-healthy Qdrant. `QDRANT_URL`/`QDRANT_API_KEY` needed no new wiring — `migrate` already receives the full `x-app-env` block (confirmed by reading `deploy/docker-compose.yml` before building), identical to api/worker.

  **Idempotency**: already proven at the function level by 1.3.c's own `test_ensure_collection_called_twice_is_a_no_op` (not re-built here — the task's own instruction was not to duplicate that coverage) and now re-confirmed end-to-end through the real CLI and the real deploy path by this task's own live proof below (a second `migrate` run issues only `GET`s, no `PUT`s, and still exits 0).

  **Live Compose proof** (scratch `.env.check`, an exact `cp` of `.env.example`, confirmed byte-identical via `diff` before use, passed via `--env-file .env.check` to every command below — matching 1.6.a's own method exactly; the real `.env` was never read or written, confirmed both before and after by its unchanged mtime): `docker compose --env-file .env.check -f deploy/docker-compose.yml up -d --build` — postgres/qdrant/api/worker all reached `(healthy)`. `docker compose ... run --rm migrate`: **exit 0**, real output —
  ```
  INFO  [alembic.runtime.migration] Context impl PostgresqlImpl.

  httpx: GET http://qdrant:6333/collections/knowledge_chunks/exists "200 OK"
  httpx: PUT http://qdrant:6333/collections/knowledge_chunks "200 OK"
  httpx: PUT http://qdrant:6333/collections/knowledge_chunks/index?wait=true "200 OK"  (x2)
  INFO  __main__ ensure_collection: 'knowledge_chunks' is correctly configured
  ```

  (No Alembic "Running upgrade" lines: the reused `postgres-data` named volume was already at head from earlier sessions — expected, `down` never removes volumes per the existing Makefile guard test, and irrelevant to this task's own Qdrant-side claim.) This is the **opposite result from 1.6.a's own empirical finding** (zero collections existed then, nothing called `ensure_collection()` anywhere) — confirmed directly from inside the `api` container (`docker compose exec api python3 -c "..."`, the project's own `get_qdrant_client()`): `collection_exists` → `True`; `get_collection` → dense vector `size=1536, distance=Cosine`; sparse vector with the `idf` modifier; `client_id` keyword index with `is_tenant=True`; `source_id` keyword index — exactly 1.3.c's own expected schema, now real in the live stack, not just asserted by its tests. Re-ran `migrate` a **second time**: exit 0, real output this time shows only `GET .../exists` and `GET .../collections/knowledge_chunks` — no `PUT`s — confirming the idempotent-verify path, not the create path, ran. `GET /health` and `/ready` both `200` throughout. A transient, unrelated observation: `worker` briefly reported `(unhealthy)` about two minutes into this session (likely probe contention from the concurrent image build and `docker exec` calls this proof itself was running), self-recovered to `(healthy)` on the very next probe with no code change — exactly the single-successful-probe-recovers asymmetry 2.1.f's own decision log already documented; not a regression, not touched by this task's own file list, not investigated further. `docker compose --env-file .env.check -f deploy/docker-compose.yml down`: all four containers and the network removed cleanly; `docker ps -a` afterward showed zero widgetplatform containers. `.env.check` deleted; `.env`'s own mtime confirmed unchanged before and after (`Sep 30 12:10:48 2026`).

- 2026-10-04: Step 2.2 breakdown approved (docs-only, no code) — nine subtasks 2.2.a–2.2.i (see the Step 2.2 task list above), based on docs/SPEC.md §5.2 (`verified_domains` storage), §5.5 (verification-once, revocation, audit), §3/§15 (the three methods, `audit_log`'s broader scope) and §12 (temporary admin key). Six decisions confirmed, researched and reported separately before this approval (not re-derived here):

  (a) **Schemas**: `verified_domains` gets an explicit `status` `CheckConstraint` (`pending`/`verified`/`revoked`) — a row is created at claim time, in `pending`, carrying a fresh per-attempt `verification_token`; `verified_at`/`revoked_at` stay nullable, matching every other nullable-until-it-happens column precedent in this project. `audit_log.actor` is a plain string, deliberately **not** a FK — no `platform_admin` role or any other real admin-identity table exists until Step 6.1, so a FK would point at nothing real; revisit once that role exists. **`audit_log` is append-only, enforced at the database level** (`REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE` on the table, at 2.2.b) **as the primary mechanism**, with 2.2.h's AST-based guard as a secondary, defense-in-depth layer catching an application-code mistake before it ever reaches the database — not the other way around, unlike every prior guard test in this project (`qdrant_api_key_str()`, `get_decrypted_credentials()`), where the AST guard *is* the primary enforcement. This is a deliberate escalation in strength, not an inconsistency: "audit" implies tamper-evidence a code-level convention alone cannot provide.

  **Dated correction (2026-10-04, recorded at Task 2.2.b): decision (a)'s own enforcement ordering is REVERSED, effective immediately, until Phase 7.** Decision (a) above was made without knowing a fact 2.2.b discovered live, by testing it, before writing the migration: `widgetplatform` (`DATABASE_URL`'s own user, the only role anywhere in this stack — `migrate`/`api`/`worker` all connect as it) is not merely `audit_log`'s table owner, it is a full Postgres **superuser** (confirmed via `\du`/`pg_roles`: `rolsuper=t`). A superuser bypasses every permission check, by definition — `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE ON audit_log FROM widgetplatform` was proven live to execute cleanly and have **zero effect**: a probe table created, revoked-from, then successfully `UPDATE`d and `DELETE`d anyway by the same role, then reconfirmed on `audit_log` itself once the real migration existed. The statement is still written into 2.2.b's migration (harmless, and it becomes real enforcement automatically, with zero further migration work, the instant the role split below lands) — but **today, 2.2.h's AST-based guard is the ONLY real enforcement against `UPDATE`/`DELETE` on `audit_log`, not a secondary layer under a working primary one.** `backend/tests/test_alembic.py::test_audit_log_update_delete_currently_succeeds_because_widgetplatform_is_a_superuser` is the permanent, automated proof of this exact, current, honest state — it is expected to start *failing* the moment the role split lands, at which point it must be rewritten to assert the opposite, not deleted.

  **Update (2026-10-04, recorded at the real Task 2.2.h): the AST-based guard referenced above is now actually built and verified, not merely planned** — `backend/tests/test_audit_log_write_path_guard.py`. See that task's own decision-log entry below for the build.

  (b) **Verification mechanics — DNS-only now, file/meta-tag deferred**: file and meta-tag verification both require an outbound HTTP fetch to a tenant-supplied host, which carries the identical SSRF surface the dedicated guard (Step 2.3) exists to close — confirmed, not assumed, by comparing the two: the attacker-controlled target (tenant-named host, redirect-capable, DNS-rebindable) is the same whether the fetch is "check one well-known path" or "crawl many pages." Building an unguarded fetch now and retrofitting 2.3's protection later would leave a real, exploitable hole in between, which rule 11 ("security requirements... always win over simplicity") forecloses. **Resolution: ship DNS-only verification now** (a DNS TXT lookup to a resolver carries no equivalent outbound-fetch-to-attacker-content risk), using the spec's own "file, meta tag, **or** DNS" wording to sequence safely; file/meta-tag verification is deferred until immediately after Step 2.3 lands, so it can reuse that step's real SSRF guard from day one rather than 2.2 building a scoped-down duplicate of logic 2.3 will also need (rule 4). `dnspython` is approved as a new runtime dependency under rule 8 — name, purpose (TXT-record lookup; Python's stdlib has no TXT-query capability at all, confirmed by reading `socket`'s own API) and no viable alternative considered (shelling out to `dig`/`host` would trade a vetted, hash-pinned Python dependency for a fragile, un-pinned system-binary dependency) — its exact current version is to be re-verified live against PyPI at 2.2.d's own build time, not assumed from this planning session, matching every prior dependency addition's own precedent (1.3.b, 1.4.b, 2.1.a). `httpx`'s promotion from a dev-only to a direct runtime dependency is deferred alongside file/meta-tag verification, for the same reason — nothing in 2.2's own DNS-only scope needs it. The verification token is generated fresh per claim attempt, tenant-and-attempt-specific, and is never reused across attempts or tenants — closing the "tenant B reuses tenant A's stale proof" gap flagged during this breakdown's own research.

  (c) **No endpoint for verification, matching 2.1's own precedent**: `confirm_verification()` is a primitive only (2.2.e) — no dashboard exists yet to call one (Phase 6), so building an endpoint now would have no real caller, the same reasoning that kept `ensure_collection()` endpoint-free until its own real caller (`migrate`) existed.

  (d) **The docs/SPEC.md-vs-PROJECT_SPEC.md conflict, resolved explicitly, per CLAUDE.md's own conflict rule**: docs/SPEC.md §12 states admin endpoints, including domain revocation, exist from Phase 2 onward behind a temporary admin-key secret — but PROJECT_SPEC.md's own practice so far had treated every admin endpoint as Phase-6-only (1.6.e's own §16 acceptance record: "a widget token on admin endpoints returns 401" DEFERRED to Phase 6, "no admin endpoints exist"; the `app/admin` stub tentatively, uncertainly owned by Step 6.5). **docs/SPEC.md wins**: `revoke_domain()` (2.2.g) is wired to a real, narrowly-scoped admin endpoint now, not left a bare primitive. This is **not** a decision to start building the admin area early — it is scoped to exactly the one action docs/SPEC.md names as needing one in Phase 2. The temporary admin-key mechanism this requires (a new required `Settings.admin_key`, a guarded accessor, a verified-admin dependency — 2.2.f, built as its OWN subtask, not bundled into `revoke_domain()`'s own logic) is deliberately minimal: one secret, one dependency, matching every other secret's existing pattern exactly (rule 11's "use the default... unless the spec says otherwise" — the spec does say otherwise here, explicitly naming the temporary-key mechanism itself, so this is the one place a custom-but-spec-mandated scheme is correct rather than a shortcut). It is explicitly superseded outright by Step 6.1's real `platform_admin` role, never extended or built on between now and then — recorded here so a future reader understands why an admin-auth mechanism exists this early, and why it is temporary rather than the start of real admin-auth work.

  **Dated correction (2026-10-04, recorded at Task 2.2.i's close-out): decision (d)'s own "wired to a real, narrowly-scoped admin endpoint now" did NOT happen, confirmed by the user.** `revoke_domain()` (2.2.g) was built as a primitive only; the admin endpoint this decision called for is dropped from Step 2.2 entirely, not merely deferred within it — see the Step 6.1-owned Open marker (recorded at 2.2.g, resolved 2026-10-04) for the full reasoning. The temporary admin-key mechanism (2.2.f) was still built exactly as this decision specifies, independent of whether an endpoint ever used it within Step 2.2 — that part of decision (d) stands unchanged.

  (e) **Same domain, two tenants — global uniqueness**: a domain can be actively claimed (`pending` or `verified`) by exactly one tenant at a time; a second tenant's claim attempt is rejected outright while the first stands, and the domain becomes claimable again, by any tenant, once revoked. docs/SPEC.md does not address this case at all — confirmed by search, not assumed absent — so this is a new decision, not a spec reading, recorded here as the authority for 2.2.c's own enforcement and hostile-caller proof.

- 2026-10-04: Step 2.2.a [SECURITY] — ORM models for `verified_domains` and `audit_log` (`backend/app/ingest/models.py`, extended); `backend/tests/conftest.py` (`EXPECTED_TABLES`/`EXPECTED_PK_COLUMNS`/`CASCADE_FK_COLUMNS` extended — unchanged in shape, only new entries added); new model tests (`backend/tests/test_models.py`). `docker info` confirmed running first.

  **Partial-unique-index approach, and why**: the global-uniqueness rule (Step 2.2 breakdown, decision (e)) needs a domain claimable again once revoked, which a plain, unconditional unique constraint can never allow without deleting the old row first — and deleting it would destroy the very audit trail `audit_log`/the revocation flow exists to keep. `Index("ix_verified_domains_domain_active_unique", "domain", unique=True, postgresql_where=text("status != 'revoked'"))` — confirmed live, by hand, before building (not assumed from memory): SQLAlchemy's `Index` genuinely supports a `postgresql_where` dialect kwarg, and it round-trips back off the `Index` object via `index.dialect_options["postgresql"]["where"]`, which is what the new test inspects directly. Deliberately on `domain` alone, never `(tenant_id, domain)` — the uniqueness is global, across every tenant, not a per-tenant scope.

  **`audit_log` has no `tenant_id` column — confirmed, not merely absent by oversight**: an audit entry records an ADMIN's own action, not a tenant's own action within its own scope — the exact thing `TenantScopedRepository` exists to enforce does not apply here, since no tenant is "operating" when a platform admin revokes a domain. docs/SPEC.md §15 independently confirms this by naming future `audit_log` actions (threshold overrides, plan changes) that don't belong to any single tenant either. `target_id` (nullable, no FK — `target_type` varies per action, so one FK could never fit every case) still names which row an action affected, without the table itself being tenant-scoped.

  **The database-level `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE` is explicitly flagged as NOT built here, and cannot be**: it is a privilege grant, not a schema object — SQLAlchemy's declarative mapping has no way to express it, and attempting to fake it at the ORM level (e.g. overriding `__setattr__` or omitting update/delete helper methods) would be exactly the kind of "looks enforced but isn't" surface this project's own discipline avoids elsewhere. A comment on `AuditLog` itself says so explicitly, naming 2.2.b as the task that must apply it, so nobody mistakes this model alone as the enforcement mechanism in the meantime.

  **A real scope boundary found and deliberately respected, not papered over**: `verified_domains` needs TWO independent closed-vocabulary `CheckConstraint`s (`method`, `status`) on one table — the first table in this project to need that. The existing shared fixtures this would naturally route through (`EXPECTED_STATUS_CHECK_CONSTRAINTS`/`test_status_check_constraints_match_the_spec_vocabulary` for the constraints, `EXPECTED_UNIQUE_INDEXES`/`test_visitor_secret_hash_unique_index_is_scoped_to_site_key` for the partial index) are both also consumed by `test_alembic.py`'s own live-database checks — which is explicitly 2.2.b's job, not yet built, and touching it now would reach across a task boundary rule 2 forbids. Resolved by writing four dedicated, standalone tests in `test_models.py` instead (`test_verified_domains_has_two_separate_check_constraints`, `test_verified_domains_active_domain_unique_index_is_partial`, `test_audit_log_has_no_tenant_id_column`, `test_audit_log_has_no_action_check_constraint`) — matching this same file's own existing precedent for table-specific one-offs (`test_jobs_source_id_is_nullable`, `test_jobs_next_run_at_is_indexed`), not a new pattern. `EXPECTED_STATUS_CHECK_CONSTRAINTS`/`EXPECTED_UNIQUE_INDEXES` keep their exact existing shape; 2.2.b decides for itself, at its own build time, whether to extend them or follow the same dedicated-test approach for the live-database proof.

No new dependency.

- 2026-10-04: Step 2.2.c [SECURITY] — tenant-scoped domain-claim primitives (`backend/app/ingest/repository.py`, extended: `claim_domain()`, `list_domains()`, `get_domain_by_id()`, `DomainAlreadyClaimedError`, `_generate_verification_token()`); six new live tests (`backend/tests/test_ingest_repository.py`). `docker info` confirmed running first.

  **Deviation from the task-list row's own named signature, recorded as such**: the row above names `start_verification(tenant_id, domain, method) -> token`; built instead as `claim_domain(domain: str) -> VerifiedDomain`. No `tenant_id` parameter — matching `enqueue()`'s own placement precedent exactly, `self.tenant_id` is already the repository's own bound identity, never a caller-supplied one (accepting it as a parameter would open exactly the forged-tenant-id risk `TenantScopedRepository`'s own hostile-caller tests exist to close elsewhere). No `method` parameter — DNS-only right now (Step 2.2 breakdown decision (b)); a parameter for a choice that doesn't exist yet would be speculative (rule 11), added back once 2.3 lands and file/meta-tag return. Returns the full `VerifiedDomain` row, not just the token — a caller needs the row's `id` (to check status later, to pass to a future `confirm_verification()`/`revoke_domain()`) at least as much as the token itself, and the token is already reachable off the returned row (`domain.verification_token`) with no information loss either way.

  **Placement on `IngestRepository`, not `TenantScopedRepository`, confirmed before building**: checked both classes' own existing precedent first. `enqueue()` (2.1.c) already established the exact reasoning that applies here — claiming a domain always happens on behalf of an already-known tenant's own action, so it fits a class whose construction already requires a real `tenant_id`, the same way `create_visitor()`/`create_conversation()` do on `TenantScopedRepository`. `verified_domains` is also ingestion-domain data (docs/SPEC.md §5.2's own grouping), matching `IngestRepository`'s existing scope (`sources`/`documents`/`db_connections`/`jobs`), not identity/session data (`TenantScopedRepository`'s own tenants/site_keys/visitors/conversations). No new repository class needed.

  **Token generation: a new, domain-specific `_generate_verification_token()`, not a literal reuse of `app.auth.secrets.generate_visitor_secret()`** — confirmed by comparing the two threat models directly, not assumed similar just because both are "a random token." A visitor secret is a real authentication credential, stored only as a SHA-256 hash, never meant to leave the visitor's own browser; a domain verification token is stored in **plaintext** (`verified_domains.verification_token`) and is meant to be **published publicly**, in a DNS TXT record (2.2.d) — reusing `generate_visitor_secret()`'s name/semantics here would conflate two genuinely different concepts that happen to share the same underlying mechanism. That mechanism — `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`, the identical call `generate_visitor_secret()` itself makes — is reused directly (rule 11: a well-known CSPRNG-backed library function, not a hand-rolled one), just not through that function's own name.

  **Collision handling, the task's own key proof — a real SQLAlchemy/Postgres behavior confirmed live, not assumed, before relying on it**: `claim_domain()` catches the partial unique index's `IntegrityError` and calls `await self.session.rollback()` *before* raising the new `DomainAlreadyClaimedError` — checked directly (not assumed) that Postgres aborts the whole transaction on a constraint violation until rollback, so without this, any later query on the same session (even one completely unrelated to domains) would fail with "current transaction is aborted." The new test exercising this (`test_claim_domain_rejects_a_different_tenants_active_claim_then_allows_it_once_revoked`) proves this directly: it calls `repo_a.list_domains()` on the *same* session immediately after catching tenant B's collision, which would itself fail if the rollback were missing or wrong. `DomainAlreadyClaimedError` (new, beside `CredentialEncryptionError`) deliberately does **not** copy that class's own deferred-raise/`from None` discipline: a domain name is not a secret (it is what the caller just supplied), so the original `IntegrityError` stays chained via a plain `raise ... from exc`, keeping the real cause visible for debugging rather than hiding it for no protective reason.

  **Same-tenant double-claim: rejected, not idempotent — the task's own flagged decision, resolved and justified**: `claim_domain()` does not special-case "this collision is actually my own earlier pending claim" into a silent "return the existing row." Three reasons, in order of weight: (1) the partial index itself (`ix_verified_domains_domain_active_unique`, Task 2.2.b) is scoped to `domain` alone, not `(tenant_id, domain)` — the schema does not distinguish same-tenant from cross-tenant collisions, and application code choosing to detect and special-case one anyway would be inventing a distinction the data model itself declines to make; (2) the Step 2.2 breakdown's own decision (b) already settled "a fresh token per attempt, never reused" — an idempotent return would hand back a *stale* token silently, directly contradicting that decision, not merely sitting awkwardly beside it; (3) this project's own enumeration-oracle discipline (`_verify_owned()`, `get_decrypted_credentials()`) already establishes the precedent of never letting a caller learn whether a collision is theirs or someone else's — treating same-tenant and cross-tenant identically here is that same discipline applied to a write, not a new one invented for this task. A genuine "regenerate my own pending claim's token" feature, if ever needed, is a different operation (an `UPDATE` on the existing row, not a second `INSERT`) and is explicitly not built speculatively here.

  ASSUMPTION: none needed — the transaction-abort/rollback behavior was verified live via the test itself reusing the same session afterward, not assumed from general Postgres knowledge. - 2026-10-04: Step 2.2.d [SECURITY] — DNS-only domain-verification mechanics (`backend/app/ingest/domain_verification.py`, new: `domain_is_verified_by_dns()`, `fetch_txt_records()`, `check_dns_verification()`, `TXT_RECORD_PREFIX`, `DNS_QUERY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`); `dnspython==2.8.0` added as a new runtime dependency (`backend/pyproject.toml`, both lockfiles); new tests (`backend/tests/test_domain_verification.py`).

  **Dependency**: current stable version re-verified live on PyPI at build time (2.8.0, `requires_python>=3.10`, compatible with this project's pinned `==3.12.*`) — not copied from the Step 2.2 breakdown's own planning session. Confirmed live, before adding it, that the base install has **zero required dependencies of its own** (`dnssec`/`doh`/`doq`/`idna`/`trio`/`wmi` are all optional extras, none installed) — `make lock` confirms this: exactly one new line in each lockfile, `dnspython==2.8.0` with 2 real hashes (wheel + sdist, no sdist-only surprise), no transitive package pulled in. `make lock-check`: clean. `make audit`: **"No known vulnerabilities found"** — nothing to bring to the user, as instructed.

  **TXT record format and location**: `samoflow-verify=<token>`, on the domain's own **apex** TXT record set — not a dedicated subdomain (`_samoflow-verify.<domain>`). docs/SPEC.md gives no hint either way (confirmed by reading §3/§5.5 again before deciding, not assumed absent). Decided by querying a real domain's own live TXT records first, not from memory: `google.com`'s own apex TXT set already carries `google-site-verification=`, `facebook-domain-verification=`, `apple-domain-verification=`, `docusign=`, `cisco-ci-domain-verification=`, `onetrust-domain-verification=` and others, all side by side on the same apex — confirming the apex-with-a-distinctive-prefix pattern is the dominant, well-established real-world convention, not an invented one. Chosen over a dedicated subdomain for lowest friction against docs/SPEC.md §12's own "mostly non-technical" dashboard audience: "add one more line to the TXT records you already have" is a smaller ask than "create a new subdomain record."

  **dnspython's async API, verified live, not assumed**: installed `dnspython==2.8.0` into an isolated scratch venv and confirmed `dns.asyncresolver.Resolver.resolve` is a genuine coroutine function (`inspect.iscoroutinefunction` → `True`) before writing anything — no `asyncio.to_thread()` wrapper needed, matching this codebase's async-everywhere convention directly. Also confirmed live: a TXT record's value can be split across multiple quoted character-strings (RFC 1035), represented as separate `bytes` segments in `rdata.strings` — `fetch_txt_records()` concatenates them (`b"".join(...)`), not just the first segment, confirmed necessary by inspecting a real multi-segment-shaped response, not assumed; and that `NXDOMAIN`/`NoAnswer`/`LifetimeTimeout`/`NoNameservers` all share the common `dns.exception.DNSException` base class, confirmed by direct `issubclass()` checks against the installed package before relying on a single `except dns.exception.DNSException` to mean "any clean DNS-layer failure" (rule 11 — the standard inspection point, not a hand-picked list of specific exception types).

  **Timeout value, and why it's a module constant, not a `Settings` field**: `DNS_QUERY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 5.0`. Checked this project's own existing precedent before deciding either way: `app/health.py`'s `/ready` endpoint uses a hardcoded `_READY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 2.0` module constant (an internal safety bound), while `Settings` fields are reserved for business-tunable parameters an operator might reasonably adjust (`worker_poll_interval_seconds`, the rate-limit fields). A DNS query timeout is the former, not the latter — not built as a `Settings` field. 5 seconds (longer than `/ready`'s 2s, deliberately): Postgres is on the same Docker network, normally sub-millisecond; DNS resolution for an uncached domain crosses real network hops to a delegation chain of servers this project doesn't control, a genuinely different latency class — generous enough to absorb a slow or distant authoritative server without hanging the caller indefinitely, while still short in absolute terms.

  **Live-vs-offline DNS testing, decided and justified, matching the task's own explicit framing**: this project generally prefers real-infrastructure proofs over mocks (Postgres, Qdrant), but third-party DNS *content* is a genuinely different reliability class — this project doesn't control it, and it can change at any time, unlike a Compose service we own. Resolved by keeping every live test **content-agnostic**: (1) a structurally-guaranteed-nonexistent domain proves the `NXDOMAIN` → `[]` path (a nonsense label can never NOT be `NXDOMAIN`, no third-party content dependency at all); (2) a monkeypatched unroutable `TEST-NET-3` nameserver (matching `app/health.py`'s own existing unroutable-address convention) plus a shortened `DNS_QUERY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` proves the timeout path fires, fast, without waiting out the real 5-second production value; (3) one real resolution against `google.com` asserts only that the result is **non-empty**, never its specific content — proving the success path works end to end without pinning anything that could drift (its own outbound-mail SPF record alone makes a fully-TXT-free future implausible). No mocking library introduced for this — `monkeypatch` (already used everywhere else in this suite) was enough.

  ASSUMPTION: none needed — every claim above (the async API, the multi-segment TXT shape, the exception hierarchy, the zero-dependency base install) was verified live against the installed package, not assumed. - 2026-10-04: Step 2.2.e [SECURITY] — `confirm_verification()` wiring (`backend/app/ingest/repository.py`, extended: `confirm_verification()`, `DomainRevokedError`); five new live tests (`backend/tests/test_ingest_repository.py`). `docker info` confirmed running first.

  **Not-found contract**: reuses `get_domain_by_id()` directly rather than re-implementing the lookup, per the task's own instruction — a `domain_id` belonging to a different tenant (or a nonexistent one) returns `None`, the identical, already-established hostile-caller contract, never distinguished. This is not merely "the same convention chosen again": because the lookup happens *first* and returns `None` immediately, a wrong-tenant call structurally never reaches the state-machine logic or the DNS check at all — there is no row in hand to check or mutate once `get_domain_by_id()` has already said no.

  **Revoked-domain rejection**: a new `DomainRevokedError` (matching `DomainAlreadyClaimedError`'s own shape) — a revoked claim is never re-verified in place; the tenant must call `claim_domain()` again for a fresh per-attempt token, not resume the revoked one. Checked before the DNS branch, no DNS call, no mutation.

  **DNS failure and "token not found" are correctly indistinguishable, confirmed, not just assumed**: `check_dns_verification()` (2.2.d) already collapses both into the identical plain `False` *before* `confirm_verification()` ever sees the result — `fetch_txt_records()` returns `[]` on NXDOMAIN/timeout/no-answer alike, and `domain_is_verified_by_dns()` returns `False` for an empty list exactly as it would for a non-matching one. `confirm_verification()` adds no further branching on top of that boolean, so this property holds by construction, not by anything this task needed to build specially. Both cases land on the identical code path: the row returned completely unchanged (still `pending`, `verified_at` still null), never an exception — confirmed by test (d), which cannot tell from the inside whether its monkeypatched `False` "means" a real DNS failure or a clean non-match, because `confirm_verification()` itself cannot tell either.

  **A real bug caught before it was written, not after**: the task's own design note said to set `verified_at=now` "using the same `TIMESTAMP_NOW`/clock convention already established elsewhere" — checked that convention directly before copying it, and found it does not transfer here. `mark_job_succeeded()`/`mark_job_failed()` (`app/ingest/queue.py`) both set their own timestamp columns to `func.now()`, but both return `None` — nothing ever reads that attribute again afterward. `confirm_verification()` must return the row with `verified_at` immediately usable by its own caller. Reproduced live, before writing the real code: assigning `domain.verified_at = func.now()` then `await session.flush()` marks the attribute expired (SQLAlchemy doesn't know the server-computed value); reading it afterward on this project's `AsyncSession` raises `sqlalchemy.exc.MissingGreenlet` (async lazy-loading via plain attribute access is unsupported outside a greenlet context) — not a wrong value, a hard crash. Fixed by using a literal Python `datetime.now(UTC)` value instead (confirmed live: no expiration, no crash, the exact assigned value readable immediately) — an injectable `clock: Callable[[], datetime] = lambda: datetime.now(UTC)` parameter, matching `mark_job_failed()`'s own identical precedent (built for testability there; load-bearing for correctness here).

  **A wording question flagged, not silently resolved either way**: the task's own design text said "commit" after setting `verified_at`. Every other method on this class and `queue.py`'s own `mark_job_succeeded()`/`mark_job_failed()`, without exception, only ever calls `flush()` — the caller owns the transaction boundary (only `app/auth/routes.py`'s endpoint-level code calls `session.commit()` anywhere in this codebase). Read "commit" as meaning "persist the change" in the general sense, not a literal instruction to break that established convention, and built `confirm_verification()` as `flush()`-only, consistent with every sibling method — flagged here rather than silently picked either way.

- 2026-10-04: Step 2.2.f [SECURITY] — the temporary admin-key mechanism. Full environment ripple across `.env.example`, `deploy/docker-compose.yml`, `backend/tests/conftest.py`, `backend/tests/test_main.py`, `backend/tests/test_alembic.py`, `deploy/smoke-image.sh`, and the local gitignored `.env` (appended, never committed, user told directly). New test file `backend/tests/test_admin_dependencies.py` (6 tests).

  **Two deliberate deviations from this breakdown's own original wording (2.2.f's task-list row, as first written)**: (1) field name `admin_api_key`, not `admin_key` — matches `jwt_signing_key`/`db_connection_encryption_key`'s own naming shape (`<thing>_<purpose>_key`) more closely than the shorter original name did; purely cosmetic, no behavior difference. (2) a dedicated `X-Admin-Key` header, not "`Authorization`-header or similar" — chosen specifically so the two credential surfaces are structurally non-overlapping, not just differently-validated: `get_current_visitor()` (`app/auth/dependencies.py`) only ever reads `Authorization`, and `require_admin_key()` only ever reads `X-Admin-Key`, so a widget JWT is never even inspected by the admin check and vice versa, by construction, not by convention alone. Both deviations were the user's own explicit instruction for this task, given directly ahead of building it.

  **Marker shape**: `AdminKeyVerified` is a deliberately empty `@dataclass(frozen=True)` — no `tenant_id`/`vid`/`org` the way `auth.dependencies.VerifiedIdentity` carries. The temporary mechanism has no concept of *which* admin acted, only that *a* valid key was presented; there is no real admin-identity table to name one until Step 6.1. Returning an empty marker rather than, say, a bare `True` keeps the dependency's return type self-documenting and keeps `Depends(require_admin_key)` swappable later without changing every call site's shape.

  **Unscoped-function placement confirmed**: module-level, never an `IngestRepository` method — the same structural reasoning already established for `create_tenant()`/`get_site_key_by_key()` (`app/tenancy/repository.py`): a platform admin revoking a domain has no tenant_id of their own to construct a tenant-scoped repository with, and the entire point of this function is reaching across every tenant's own domains, not being confined to one.

  **Already-revoked idempotency: a safe no-op, not an error** — deliberately the opposite shape from `confirm_verification()`'s own "revoked" case (which raises `DomainRevokedError`). Those are different situations, not an inconsistency: `confirm_verification()` on a revoked domain is a conflicting *state transition* (trying to move backward to "verified"), whereas `revoke_domain()` on an already-revoked domain is the *same* action requested twice, landing on the *same* target state both times — matching `confirm_verification()`'s own "already verified" no-op precedent instead, which is the structurally identical case. No second `audit_log` row is written on the no-op path: nothing actually changed, so there is nothing new to audit — writing one anyway would let a repeated call flood a real audit trail with no-op noise.

  **`actor` is a required parameter, not hardcoded**: this function has no opinion on identity and shouldn't — the temporary admin-key mechanism (2.2.f) carries none (`AdminKeyVerified` is deliberately empty), so today's caller passes a generic placeholder string; once Step 6.1's real `platform_admin` role exists, its caller passes a real identity instead, with zero change needed inside `revoke_domain()` itself. Hardcoding a value here would bake today's identity-less stopgap into the one place meant to outlive it.

  **Transaction atomicity (point 2)**: confirmed naturally true, not specially engineered — both the domain's `status`/`revoked_at` mutation and the new `AuditLog` row are flushed together in a single `await session.flush()` call, the same flush-not-commit convention every other method in this codebase already follows; the caller owns the commit/rollback boundary. Proven live, not just read: test (e) injects a REAL failure through `audit_log.actor`'s own existing `NOT NULL` constraint (`actor=None`) — the flush raises `IntegrityError` exercising `revoke_domain()`'s own real code path, and a fresh session afterward confirms the domain row is still `"pending"`, never silently `"revoked"` alone.

  **"Already-indexed content stays answerable": structural proof only, as expected** — confirmed by reading the function just written: it touches exactly two tables, `verified_domains` (one SELECT, one UPDATE) and `audit_log` (one INSERT). No document/chunk table and no Qdrant call exists anywhere in it, because no such table exists anywhere in Step 2.1/2.2's own scope yet to touch. A live, data-level proof (revoke a domain, confirm its already-indexed chunks remain queryable afterward) becomes both possible and necessary once real indexing exists — new Open marker added below, owned by whichever step first indexes real content (expected to be Step 2.4 or later).
