  **Matcher design: name-based (`called_name()`), not import-confinement, and why that's the correct choice here, not a weaker shortcut**: the Qdrant read-path guard's own rule (a) confines imports specifically because `retrieve` collides with an unrelated, legitimately-named function elsewhere in this codebase (`app.retrieval.service.retrieve`) — a name-only scan there would wrongly flag real application code. Checked whether `AuditLog` has the same problem before copying that heavier design anyway: grepped the entire `app/` tree and confirmed `AuditLog` names exactly one thing everywhere it appears — the ORM class itself (`app/ingest/models.py`) and its one real constructor call (`app/ingest/repository.py`). No collision risk exists, so `check_call_allowlist()`/`called_name()` (the exact shared helper `test_config_guard.py`'s `Settings()`/`errors()` checks and `test_ingest_repository_guard.py` already use) is the correct, matching-weight tool — not a lesser version of the Qdrant guard's own design, a differently-shaped problem solved with the already-established matching solution.

  **Limits stated plainly, and proven live, not just claimed in prose**: this guard cannot see an import alias (`from app.ingest.models import AuditLog as AL` then `AL(...)` — the Call node's own name is `"AL"`, not `"AuditLog"`), a dynamically-resolved reference (`getattr(models, "AuditLog")(...)`, or a factory function), or a raw SQL `INSERT INTO audit_log` that never goes through the ORM class at all. The import-alias blind spot is backed by a real, running test (`test_fails_on_an_audit_log_construction_via_an_import_alias_evading_detection`) that asserts the accepted false negative, not merely a comment — so a future change to `called_name()` that starts resolving aliases would be caught here as a behavior change, rather than silently contradicting a comment nobody re-reads.

  **A real tooling gap found and worked around, not silently papered over**: `check_call_allowlist()` (`tests/conftest.py`) hardcodes `path.relative_to(BACKEND_DIR)`, so it cannot be pointed at a `tmp_path` synthetic tree the way the task's own point 3 asked for (a scratch-file proof, not touching any real file under `backend/app`) — confirmed live, by running it against `tmp_path` first and getting a `ValueError`, not assumed from reading the source alone. Modifying that shared helper to accept a `root` parameter was out of this task's own stated file scope ("a new test file," not `tests/conftest.py`), so a small local root-parameterized scan function lives in the new test file instead, used only by its own synthetic-tree unit tests — the real guard test still calls the shared `check_call_allowlist()` directly against the real tree, unchanged.

  **Proof the real tree has exactly one legitimate site, not merely zero violations**: `check_call_allowlist()` returning `[]` would also pass vacuously if `AuditLog` were constructed nowhere at all. `test_exactly_one_legitimate_audit_log_construction_site_exists_today` counts the real construction sites inside the one allow-listed file directly (exactly 1, `revoke_domain()`, confirmed by direct grep of the whole `app/` tree before writing this test, not assumed) and confirms `AuditLog.__tablename__ == "audit_log"` — the class is genuinely importable and real, not a name that happens to match without resolving to anything.

  **Why this guard matters right now, not generic defense-in-depth**: stated directly in the new file's own header comment, pointing at 2.2.b's live-proven finding (`widgetplatform` is a Postgres superuser, so the `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE ON audit_log` migration statement has zero real effect) — until Phase 7's role split lands, this guard is the ONLY real enforcement of `audit_log`'s append-only invariant, not a secondary layer under a working primary one. - 2026-10-04: Step 2.3 breakdown approved (docs-only, no code) — five subtasks 2.3.a–2.3.e (see the Step 2.3 task list below), based on docs/SPEC.md §5.5 (the core SSRF requirement: http/https only, DNS resolved, private/loopback/link-local/cloud-metadata addresses rejected, re-checked after every redirect, connection pinned to the validated IP) and §16's own acceptance wording ("URLs resolving to `127.0.0.1`, `10.x` or `169.254.169.254` are blocked, including through redirects and DNS rebinding"). Six decisions confirmed, researched and reported separately before this approval:

  (a) **IP classification covers both IPv4 and IPv6, with one fail-closed function**: `not ip.is_global or ip.is_multicast or ip.is_reserved or ip.is_unspecified`, built on the stdlib `ipaddress` module. Verified live against the installed module (Python 3.12), not assumed: this correctly rejects RFC 1918, loopback, link-local, the metadata IP (`169.254.169.254`, a link-local instance), IPv6 unique-local/loopback/link-local — and, found live rather than copied from docs/SPEC.md's own wording, two ranges a naive `ip.is_private` check alone would miss: CGNAT (`100.64.0.0/10`, `is_private=False`) and multicast (`is_private=False`). docs/SPEC.md never mentions IPv6 (confirmed by grep, zero hits); checking both families anyway closes what would otherwise be a silent IPv6 bypass of an IPv4-only guard on any network with IPv6 connectivity.

  (b) **"Pin to the validated IP" — the mechanism, confirmed by reading the installed `httpcore==1.0.9` source directly, not from documentation or memory**: httpx has no built-in "connect to this IP" option, but the standard pattern needs no custom transport — build the request URL with the validated IP literal as the host, set the `Host` header to the original hostname, and pass `extensions={"sni_hostname": original_hostname}`. Confirmed in `httpcore/_async/connection.py`'s `_connect()`: the TLS handshake's `server_hostname` — used for **both** the SNI ClientHello field and certificate hostname verification — comes from the `sni_hostname` extension when present, falling back to the origin host otherwise. This means certificate verification correctly checks the real hostname, not the raw IP, with no further code needed.

  (c) **DNS rebinding — closed completely by IP-literal connection, not just mitigated, with one narrow named exception**: confirmed empirically, not assumed, that `socket.getaddrinfo()` on an IP literal returns in ~12ms with zero network traffic (vs. 1.9s+ for a real DNS lookup) — the OS short-circuits resolution entirely for address literals. Since httpcore's connection layer calls `connect_tcp(host=<whatever is passed>, ...)`, passing the already-validated IP (never the hostname) means **no second DNS lookup ever happens** at connect time — there is no window left for an attacker's DNS server to flip the answer between validation and connection, because connection never asks DNS again. This closes the classic TOCTOU rebinding gap completely, not partially. The one thing it does not address, named plainly rather than silently ignored: IP address *reassignment* at the network-ownership level between validate and connect — a different, far narrower, largely theoretical concern unrelated to DNS, with no known practical SSRF mitigation addressing it either.

  (d) **2.3 owns all three fetch-limit numbers** (5MB size cap, 15s timeout, 3 redirect max) as configurable parameters of the safe-fetch primitive, defaults matching docs/SPEC.md §5.5 exactly — not deferred to 2.6, since redirect-count and size-cap are inseparable from this guard's own redirect-recheck and streaming-size-check logic. 2.6 may override its own instance's values later for crawl-specific needs; "2 concurrent requests per host with a delay" and robots.txt/User-Agent/HTML-cleanup stay 2.6's own job, not built here (they are crawl-level, not single-fetch, concerns).

  (e) **A reusable, generic primitive only, no real caller wired here** — matching `confirm_verification()`/`revoke_domain()`'s own precedent exactly. The first real caller is file/meta-tag domain verification (resolved at 2.3.e), not 2.6, which is several steps away.

  (f) **`httpx` promoted from dev-only to a direct runtime dependency at 2.3.c** (the first subtask that actually needs it — 2.3.a/b need neither). Re-verified live on PyPI at this breakdown's own research stage, not assumed from the existing dev pin: `httpx==0.28.1` is still current, and `httpcore==1.0.9` (the transitive dependency implementing the `sni_hostname` mechanism decision (b) depends on) is also current.

  Current task set to 2.3.a, next to 2.3.b. Counter left at n=0 (this breakdown is planning/documentation only, no files outside PROJECT_SPEC.md changed, matching the precedent set at every prior Step breakdown).

- 2026-10-05: Step 2.3.d [SECURITY] — redirect-chain handling (`backend/app/ingest/safe_fetch.py`, extended: `fetch_with_redirects()`, `_validate_and_pick_ip()`, `_PinnedResponse`; `_fetch_pinned()`'s own return type widened); 5 new tests plus 1 existing test simplified (`backend/tests/test_safe_fetch.py`).

  **A current/next bookkeeping error in this task's own instructions, caught and corrected before writing it**: given as "current 2.3.e, next Step 2.4," with the task's own text already second-guessing itself toward "next is simply 2.3.e itself" — neither is right. Checked the file's own established precedent directly rather than guessing: the exact analogous transition (2.2.h → 2.2.i, the second-to-last subtask advancing to a step's own close-out) set current to the close-out subtask (`2.2.i`) and next to the placeholder for the step AFTER the whole one closing (`"Step 2.3 breakdown"`), never the close-out subtask's own name repeated as "next." Set below to match that precedent exactly: current `2.3.e`, next `"Step 2.4 breakdown"`.

  **Return-shape mechanism chosen: widen `_fetch_pinned()`'s own return type**, not a second lower-level function. `_fetch_pinned()` now returns a new internal `_PinnedResponse` dataclass (`status_code`, `headers`, `body`) instead of bare `bytes`. `safe_fetch()` immediately narrows back to `.body`, so its own public signature and behavior are completely unchanged for any non-redirect response — confirmed by re-running all 6 of 2.3.c's own tests completely unmodified against the extended module before touching the test file at all, every one passing identically. The one existing direct caller of `_fetch_pinned()` (the SNI-mismatch test) only ever asserts on a raised exception, never a successful return value, so the widened type did not affect it either. The new `fetch_with_redirects()` is a separate, additional entry point — matching the original breakdown's own "wraps 2.3.c in a loop" framing — not a redesign of `safe_fetch()` itself. The validation logic `safe_fetch()` originally had inline (scheme check, literal-IP-or-DNS branch, IP pick) is factored out into a new shared `_validate_and_pick_ip()`, called fresh by both `safe_fetch()` and every iteration of `fetch_with_redirects()`'s own loop — the mechanism that makes "re-run the ENTIRE validation cycle from scratch on each hop" literally true by construction, not just by intent.

  **Relative Location resolution: `httpx.URL.join()`, confirmed live, not hand-rolled.** Verified directly before relying on it: correctly resolves absolute, root-relative, path-relative, and protocol-relative (`//host/path`) forms per RFC 3986. Redirect detection uses `httpx.codes.is_redirect()` (the library's own 300–399 classification, reused rather than hand-picking a status-code set) combined with a `location` header actually being present.

  **Redirect-count semantics**: "max 3 redirects" reads as *3 redirects followed*, i.e. up to 4 total requests — matching the everyday convention (e.g. curl's own `--max-redirs`), not "3 total requests." The loop runs `max_redirects + 1` times; if the response at the final allowed iteration is still a redirect, that would be a 4th follow and fails closed there, never a 5th request.

  **Per-hop, not cumulative, size/timeout limits — my own reading, stated rather than silently picked**: docs/SPEC.md's "5MB per page" reads naturally as per RESPONSE — a redirect hop's own response is its own distinct fetch, not a continuation of a prior one — and nothing in docs/SPEC.md addresses a chained/cumulative budget at all. Built and tested accordingly: every hop gets the full, fresh `max_size_bytes`/`timeout_seconds` budget from `_fetch_pinned()`'s own unchanged per-call enforcement; a dedicated test proves two 80KB hops (summing to 160KB, over a 100KB cap) both succeed, which a cumulative implementation would not allow.

  **Test design note: `assert_no_socket_connections()` doesn't fit the key hostile-proof test, used surgically instead** — that shared helper fails on ANY `socket.socket.connect()` call, but the hop-2-is-unsafe test's own hop 1 legitimately needs one (a real connection to the local test server) to produce the real redirect response being re-validated. A more targeted patch (fails only for the specific unsafe hop-2 address, passes every other call through to the real `connect`) is used instead, with the reasoning recorded directly in the test's own comment.

  **Index shape: TWO separate partial unique indexes, confirmed correct before building, not assumed.** Empirically confirmed live against the real test database first (a temp table, a plain unique index on two columns, two rows both with the second column `NULL`): Postgres never treats two `NULL`s as equal under a unique index, so `postgresql_where="... IS NOT NULL"` is **not** functionally required to stop null-vs-null false collisions — it is added anyway purely for explicitness, matching the user's own stated reasoning exactly and `verified_domains`'s own partial-index precedent of stating the intended scope rather than relying on an incidental side effect of NULL semantics. Two indexes, not one combined index, because url/file_name's mutual exclusivity makes them two independent uniqueness rules — no row is ever compared on both at once.

  **A real gap found in the model's own prior comment, flagged rather than silently resolved either way**: that comment framed the url/file_name split as only two cases (web source → url, upload → file_name), but `sources.type` has a THIRD relevant case this comment never named — `database`. A `database`-source document (a row/table extraction) has neither a URL nor a filename. So a row with both `url` and `file_name` NULL is a genuinely valid state, not a gap to close with an "at least one must be set" `CheckConstraint` — confirmed live (inserted such a row against the real migration, succeeded). The model's own comment is corrected to name all three cases.

  **Autogenerate coverage, confirmed live, not assumed from precedent alone**: `alembic revision --autogenerate` correctly detected BOTH new partial indexes on this ALTER to an already-existing table (the console log explicitly reported "Detected added index" for each) — this is a NEW finding, extending 2.2.b's own prior confirmation (partial-index detection on a CREATE TABLE only) to also cover an ADD to an existing table. It did NOT detect the new `CheckConstraint` at all (empty diff for it) — consistent with 1.4.d's/2.3.e's own prior finding that autogenerate misses CheckConstraint changes on an ALTER, now confirmed to extend to a brand-new CheckConstraint being added, not just an existing one's condition changing. The two index calls in the migration are autogenerate's own unedited output; the CheckConstraint calls are hand-written.

  **Stale-head-downgrade bug (task point 4): investigated for a durable general fix, found that one already exists and is already applied.** The bug hit three times before (the visitor-index/tenant-status-check test, found live at Task 2.1.b; the ingestion-tables test, found live at Task 2.2.b; the verified_domains/audit_log test, found live at Task 2.3.e) — each originally wrote upgrade `"head"` + downgrade `"-1"`, which broke the moment a later migration moved what `"head"` meant. The fix already applied to all three — upgrade to the specific, immutable revision ID that introduces the feature under test (never the word `"head"`), then downgrade `"-1"` (one relative step) from there — **is** the durable, general fix, not a stopgap: a revision ID never changes meaning once committed (rule 2: past migrations are never modified), so pinning to one cannot go stale no matter how many later migrations land on top. Considered and rejected a dynamic alternative (deriving "the previous head" via Alembic's `ScriptDirectory`/revision-walking API): this would be WORSE, not more durable, since these tests deliberately target a SPECIFIC historical migration, not "whatever head happens to be today" — a helper resolving "current head" dynamically just relocates the exact same fragile assumption into a function instead of the literal word `"head"`. No retroactive change was needed to the three prior tests; they already use the correct pattern. The new test for this migration (`test_downgrade_from_head_removes_documents_status_check_and_partial_indexes`) applies it identically from the start: upgrades to `3f3fbac167b7` explicitly.

- 2026-10-05: Step 2.4.b — HTML extraction primitive (new `backend/app/ingest/extract_html.py`: `extract_html()`, `ExtractedContent`, `ContentBlock`; new dependencies `beautifulsoup4==4.15.0` + `lxml==6.1.3`; new `backend/tests/test_extract_html.py` against 7 real fixture files under `backend/tests/fixtures/html/`).

  **Dependencies, re-verified live on PyPI at this task's own build time, not assumed from the Step 2.4 breakdown's approval-in-principle**: `beautifulsoup4==4.15.0` (requires `>=3.7.0`, wheel+sdist) and `lxml==6.1.3` (requires `>=3.8`, confirmed cp312 wheels on manylinux2014/_2_28 aarch64/x86_64/ppc64le and macOS universal2/x86_64 — no sdist-only build, no extra system `libxml2`/`libxslt` packages needed in the Docker image, since manylinux wheels bundle them statically). `make lock` gained exactly 3 new entries in BOTH lockfiles, no removals, no version bumps to anything already pinned: `beautifulsoup4==4.15.0` (2 hashes, wheel+sdist), `lxml==6.1.3` (176 hashes — the full prebuilt-wheel matrix plus sdist), `soupsieve==2.10` (2 hashes, wheel+sdist — bs4's own required CSS-selector dependency; `typing-extensions`, bs4's other declared dependency, was already present from an existing package, not new). `make lock-check` clean (zero diff, exit 0). `make audit`: **no known vulnerabilities found** across both lockfiles.

  **heading_path algorithm, researched and confirmed live before building, not assumed**: h1–h6 are siblings in the DOM, never nested inside each other the way a `<div>` would be — confirmed by reading real parsed output. heading_path is therefore computed by walking headings and `<p>` tags in DOCUMENT ORDER (`BeautifulSoup.find_all([...])`'s own single-call result is already in document order) while maintaining a stack of "currently open" headings keyed by level: a new heading pops every stacked entry at its own level or deeper, then pushes itself; a `<p>`'s own heading_path is the stack's texts at that moment, outermost first. Verified against a real 4-level nested fixture (`nested_headings.html`): produces exactly `("Top Section", "Subsection", "Sub-subsection")` for the deepest paragraph, and confirms a later sibling `<h2>` correctly ends both the prior `<h2>` and `<h3>`.

  **Hidden-element mechanisms covered, a deliberate subset, stated rather than silently picked**: `style="display:none"`, `style="visibility:hidden"` (both whitespace/case-normalized), the `hidden` attribute, and `aria-hidden="true"` — the four candidates the task itself named, each a cheap direct-attribute check. Deliberately NOT covered: hiding via a CSS class matched by a rule in an external or `<style>`-block stylesheet (e.g. `class="hidden"` with `.hidden { display: none }` defined elsewhere) — correctly detecting that needs a real CSS selector engine evaluated against the DOM, a different order of complexity than a primitive warrants (rule 11); recorded as a new Open marker below for whoever first hits a real page that needs it.

  **A real implementation hazard found and fixed, not assumed away**: decomposing a hidden ancestor tag also marks its own already-collected descendant tags `.decomposed` and clears their `.attrs` to `None` — confirmed live that calling `.get()`/`.has_attr()` on such a descendant (reached via a pre-materialized `find_all(True)` list, needed to avoid mutating the tree mid-iteration) raises `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'get'`. Fixed by checking `tag.decomposed` and skipping before any attribute check — `extract_html.py`'s own code comment carries the full reasoning.

  **A fixture bug caught and fixed before it could produce a misleading test**: the malformed-HTML fixture's first draft had an unclosed `<title>` tag — confirmed live that lxml then treats EVERYTHING until end-of-document as the title's own text (no crash, but not what test (g) intends to prove). Fixed by closing `<title>` properly while keeping the body genuinely malformed (unclosed `<p>`, a stray `<b>`, an unclosed `<div><span>`) — confirmed live afterward: no crash, correct title, correct block extraction, and the bare `<div><span>` text correctly excluded from blocks (scoped to headings + `<p>` only, by design, not a bug).

  **`ExtractedContent`/`ContentBlock` shape**: `ContentBlock(heading_path: tuple[str, ...], text: str)`; `ExtractedContent(title: str | None, blocks: tuple[ContentBlock, ...], word_count: int)` with an `is_nearly_empty` property (`word_count < NEARLY_EMPTY_WORD_THRESHOLD`, the module constant from the Step 2.4 decision entry). Both frozen dataclasses, matching `safe_fetch.py`'s own `_PinnedResponse` precedent. `word_count` is computed over ALL visible post-stripping text (not just the sum of blocks' own text, and not the title) — a page could carry real content entirely inside headings with no `<p>` tags, which should still count toward "nearly empty"; the title is excluded as metadata, not body content. Block extraction itself is scoped to `h1`–`h6` and `<p>` only (not every block-level tag, e.g. bare `<div>` text) — a deliberate v1 scope for a "primitive," not exhaustive real-world HTML-layout coverage (rule 11); flagged as an ASSUMPTION below for whoever builds the real crawler adapter (Step 2.6) against real-world pages.

  ASSUMPTION: block extraction covers `h1`–`h6` + `<p>` only — a real crawled page using bare `<div>`/`<span>` text without `<p>` wrapping would contribute to `word_count` (computed over the whole page) but not to any `ContentBlock`, until Step 2.6's own real-page testing shows this needs widening. UNCERTAIN: none — the heading_path algorithm, the hidden-element hazard, and the lxml title-parsing quirk were all verified live. TODO: none new beyond the hidden-element-mechanism Open marker below. - 2026-10-05: Build-tooling fix, found mid-Task-2.4.c, not a subtask of its own (a planning/documentation-only or pure-tooling change does not count toward the rule 9 counter, matching this project's own established precedent) — `make lock`/`make lock-upgrade`/`make lock-check` (`Makefile`) now compile `backend/requirements-dev.lock` constrained against `backend/requirements.lock` (`uv pip compile`'s own `-c`/`--constraints` flag).

  **Root cause**: `requirements.lock` and `requirements-dev.lock` were each compiled by its own fully independent `uv pip compile` invocation, with no shared state between them. This was invisible as long as no package appeared in both dependency graphs with any resolution freedom — but Task 2.4.c's own `tiktoken` pulls in `requests` (and transitively `charset-normalizer`) as a RUNTIME dependency for the first time; `requests` already existed dev-side (via `pip-audit`'s own chain). The two independent resolutions landed on different `charset-normalizer` versions (`3.5.2` runtime, `3.5.1` dev) — confirmed live, reproducibly (not a one-off timing fluke: `make lock-check`'s own fresh recompile reproduced the identical split three separate times). This broke `make audit` outright (`ResolutionImpossible`, since it installs from both lockfiles together).

  **Fix, verified live before applying**: `uv pip compile --help` confirmed `-c, --constraints <CONSTRAINTS>` is a real, supported flag. Tested live, using this project's own real seeded-recompile discipline (not a naive fresh-unseeded test, which initially showed misleading unrelated-package churn — isolated and correctly attributed to real-time PyPI publishing between test runs, nothing to do with `-c` itself): compiling the dev lockfile with `-c backend/requirements.lock` produces **exactly one change** — `charset-normalizer` `3.5.1` → `3.5.2`, matching the runtime lockfile exactly, byte-stable across repeated re-runs. Applied to all three Makefile targets that compile the dev lockfile (`lock`, `lock-upgrade`, `lock-check`), each already in lockstep with the other two.

  **A second bug, caught live before reporting success, not assumed fixed**: `lock-check`'s own first draft of this fix used `-c $$tmp/requirements.lock` (the temp directory's own freshly recompiled copy) rather than the real committed path. `uv` embeds the literal `-c` path STRING into every affected package's own "via" comment — so the temp directory's absolute path made `lock-check`'s own recompiled output permanently byte-differ from what the real `lock` target produces (a comment-text artifact, not a version difference), which would have made `lock-check` fail forever after this fix, the opposite of the intent. Fixed by pointing `lock-check` at the literal `backend/requirements.lock` path instead — the exact same argument the real `lock` target's own second line uses — confirmed this is safe: when the runtime lockfile hasn't drifted (the case `lock-check` needs to pass), its content is byte-identical to the temp copy anyway, so the constraint resolves identically either way; when it has drifted, the runtime-lockfile diff already reports "out of date" independently of this.

  **Durability**: unlike `platformdirs==4.11.15`'s own precedent (a per-package direct pin, needed again for the next package that flaps), this is a structural fix — any future dependency that happens to be needed by both the runtime and dev graphs will now be forced to agree by construction, not by chance, for every package, not just `charset-normalizer`.

  ASSUMPTION: none needed — both the `-c` mechanism and the path-string hazard were verified live, not assumed. UNCERTAIN: none. TODO: none new. Does not change the current/next task pointers or the counter (a tooling fix, not a subtask).

  Verified: `requirements.lock` confirmed byte-unaffected by this fix (`-c` only touches the dev compile) — diffed against its pre-fix content, zero lines changed. `requirements-dev.lock`'s only real change: the one `charset-normalizer` version fix — confirmed by diffing against its pre-fix content, re-run twice more for full stability (byte-identical both times). `make lock-check`: clean, exit 0, re-run 3 times for stability. `make audit`: **no known vulnerabilities found**, no `ResolutionImpossible`. `make lint` clean. Full `backend/tests` without a test database: **421 passed, 151 skipped** (unchanged — no app code touched by this fix).

  **`tiktoken==0.14.0`, re-verified live on PyPI at this task's own build time**: requires `>=3.9`; confirmed prebuilt cp312 wheels on `manylinux_2_28` (x86_64/aarch64) and macOS — despite its Rust extension, no Rust toolchain is needed in the Docker image, not an sdist-only build. `make lock` gained exactly 4 new entries in `requirements.lock` (`tiktoken`, `regex`, `requests`, `charset-normalizer` — `requests` becoming a runtime dependency for the first time is what surfaced the lockfile-tooling bug fixed separately above) and 2 in `requirements-dev.lock` (`tiktoken`, `regex` — `requests`/`charset-normalizer` already existed there). All hash-covered with real wheel matrices, no sdist-only entries. `make lock-check`/`make audit` both clean.

  **Encoding confirmed live, not assumed**: `tiktoken.encoding_for_model("text-embedding-3-small")` returns `cl100k_base` — confirmed by calling it directly, not inferred from documentation. A real, separately-verified operational finding: `cl100k_base` is NOT bundled in the installed `tiktoken` package — `tiktoken_ext.openai_public.cl100k_base()`'s own source shows it fetches its merge-rank file from `openaipublic.blob.core.windows.net` over HTTPS on first use (confirmed live: the fetch succeeded and cached under the OS temp dir, since no cache existed yet). This is a genuine new network dependency for whichever process calls `chunk_text()` first — recorded as a new Open marker below for Phase 7 deployment hardening, not addressed further here (a primitive, no real caller yet, matching 2.3's/2.4.b's own precedent). Built as a lazy `@lru_cache` singleton accessor (matching `get_settings()`/`get_engine()`/`get_qdrant_client()`'s own established pattern) specifically so merely IMPORTING `chunking.py` never triggers the fetch — only a real call to `chunk_text()` does.

  **Block-boundary design, researched and decided**: chunk WITHIN each `ContentBlock` independently, NEVER merging text across a heading_path boundary into one chunk — rejecting the "flow continuously across blocks" alternative, because a chunk straddling two different heading_paths would have no single correct answer for which one to report, directly undermining docs/SPEC.md's own "keeping the heading path" per-chunk guarantee. Each chunk inherits its own source block's heading_path exactly; `chunk_index` still increments sequentially across the WHOLE document, never resetting per block — verified against the real `nested_headings.html` fixture (4 blocks, 4 chunks, indices `[0,1,2,3]`, heading_paths matching each source block exactly).

  **Overlap correctness, proved at the token level, not assumed from the sliding-window formula alone**: a new real fixture (`long_block.html`, one 549-token paragraph) produces exactly 2 chunks (512 + 101 tokens); confirmed live that the first chunk's LAST 64 tokens are literally identical (`==`) to the second chunk's FIRST 64 tokens, via direct token-id comparison, not a text/string comparison. **Short-final-block overlap edge case, resolved by construction, not by a special case**: `_token_windows()`'s own stride formula (`start = previous_end - CHUNK_OVERLAP_TOKENS`) mathematically guarantees any non-first window is never shorter than `CHUNK_OVERLAP_TOKENS + 1` tokens (proved algebraically in the function's own comment, confirmed by the `long_block.html` case: its own second window is 101 tokens, comfortably above the 65-token theoretical minimum) — the overlap region itself can never be truncated; only the amount of genuinely NEW content in a small final window can shrink. A block smaller than `MAX_CHUNK_TOKENS` produces exactly one window with no second window and therefore no overlap concept at all (the `nearly_empty.html` case, test (c)).

  **`is_nearly_empty` deliberately NOT special-cased inside `chunk_text()`**: an empty `blocks` tuple simply never enters the per-block loop, naturally producing `[]` with no explicit rejection branch. Whether a near-empty document is even worth chunking/indexing at all is a policy decision for whichever future adapter (Step 2.6+) calls this, not `chunk_text()`'s own job to enforce.

  **`compute_content_hash()` shape, confirmed correct via two separate empirical checks, not assumed**: hashes `"\n".join([title?, *block.text for block in blocks])` (document order, a fixed separator to avoid an ambiguous-concatenation collision) via SHA-256 (`hashlib.sha256(...).hexdigest()`, matching this project's own established fingerprinting default — `app/auth/secrets.py`, `app/auth/tokens.py`). Confirmed live that this is deterministic and content-only (two separately-constructed `ExtractedContent` objects with identical fields hash identically). **The "hash normalized text, not raw bytes" property verified to have a REAL effect, not just a theoretical one**: two HTML strings with identical visible content but different inter-tag whitespace/indentation extract to byte-identical `ExtractedContent` and therefore hash identically, while a raw-bytes SHA-256 of the same two strings does NOT match — confirmed live, both directions. **A narrower claim deliberately NOT made**: confirmed live that `extract_html()`'s own `get_text(strip=True)` does NOT collapse whitespace WITHIN a single text run (`"hello   world"` survives as-is, only leading/trailing whitespace per node is stripped) — so word-internal spacing noise is honestly NOT normalized away today; the test and this entry both state the real, narrower property (inter-tag/structural noise only) rather than overclaiming a broader one that doesn't hold.

- 2026-10-05: Step 2.4.d — PDF extraction primitive (new `backend/app/ingest/extract_pdf.py`: `extract_pdf()`, reusing 2.4.b's own `ExtractedContent`/`ContentBlock` unchanged; new dependency `pypdf==6.19.0`; new `backend/tests/test_extract_pdf.py` plus two new real, hand-constructed fixture PDFs under `backend/tests/fixtures/pdf/`).

  **`pypdf==6.19.0`, re-verified live on PyPI at this task's own build time**: requires `>=3.9`; confirmed still genuinely pure Python — a single `py3-none-any` wheel, no platform-specific wheels at all since there is nothing to compile (the other half of why it was chosen over PyMuPDF, alongside the AGPL/license reason already recorded at the Step 2.4 breakdown). Its own declared dependency (`typing_extensions`) is conditional on `python_version < "3.11"`, so it resolves to zero required dependencies on this project's 3.12 pin. `make lock` (now using 2.4.c's own `-c`-constrained mechanism) gained exactly ONE new entry in BOTH lockfiles — `pypdf==6.19.0`, 2 hashes (wheel + sdist), no sdist-only surprise — confirming the cross-lockfile-conflict class of bug from 2.4.c does not recur here: with zero transitive dependencies on this Python version, there was nothing for the two independent resolutions to disagree about. `make lock-check`/`make audit` both clean.

  **Multi-page handling, decided**: ALL pages flatten into exactly ONE `ContentBlock` per document, matching the user's own stated lean — a page boundary is a print-layout artifact (where a physical sheet happened to end), not semantic document structure, the identical reasoning the flat-`heading_path` decision already applies to font size. Each page's own extracted text is joined with `"\n"` before building the single block, confirmed live this prevents the last word of one page running directly into the first word of the next with no separating whitespace.

  **`title` always `None`**: the task-list row's own framing is "text only" — unlike HTML's near-universal `<title>` tag, PDF metadata's `/Info /Title` field is frequently absent or unreliable across real-world producers; reading it would add a new, untested surface this primitive does not need. Stated plainly as a deliberate scope match, not an oversight.

  **Fixture PDFs: hand-constructed via a small one-time script, not a library, and not committed** (the script lived in the scratchpad only; the OUTPUT `.pdf` bytes are the committed fixtures) — rule 8/11 reasoning: no PDF-WRITING library is a dependency anywhere in this project (`pypdf` itself has no real "draw text on a page" API), and adding one (e.g. `reportlab`) solely to generate a test fixture would be disproportionate. The script computes every object's exact byte offset programmatically (via running byte-length counts as it builds the file) rather than hand-counting them, so the resulting `xref` table is exactly correct by construction — confirmed live, `pypdf` parses both fixtures with no warnings on the valid path. Two fixtures: `multi_page.pdf` (2 pages, 80 words of genuine, assertable prose split across them, long enough to also prove the real "not nearly empty" direction) and `no_text.pdf` (1 page, a filled-rectangle-only content stream with zero text-showing operators — the smallest adequate proxy for a scanned-image-only page's own observable extraction result, without embedding real raster image data, which would have been impractical to construct reliably by hand for a primitive at this scope). `no_text.pdf` does double duty: it is both test (e)'s own "no extractable text" fixture AND test (b)'s own "near-empty" direction, avoiding a third near-duplicate fixture file.

  **Malformed input, confirmed live rather than assumed adequate**: truncating `multi_page.pdf`'s own real bytes at the halfway point (cutting into its object stream, not just empty/garbage bytes) makes `PdfReader` raise `pypdf.errors.PdfStreamError` — confirmed to subclass `PdfReadError` → `PyPdfError`, pypdf's own common base exception, already clear, specific, and documented. `extract_pdf()` deliberately does NOT catch or re-wrap this: there is no existing caller-facing contract here needing a different shape (unlike `app/ingest/repository.py`'s own `IntegrityError`-to-domain-error translation), so adding a second, redundant exception type would be pure overhead (rule 11).

- 2026-10-05: Step 2.4.e — DOCX extraction primitive (new `backend/app/ingest/extract_docx.py`: `extract_docx()`, reusing 2.4.b's own `ExtractedContent`/`ContentBlock` unchanged; new dependency `python-docx==1.2.0`; new `backend/tests/test_extract_docx.py` plus two new real fixture `.docx` files and their committed generator, `backend/tests/fixtures/docx/generate_fixtures.py`; `read_docx_fixture()` added to `conftest.py`, matching the HTML/PDF helper convention from the prior duplication check).

  **`python-docx==1.2.0`, re-verified live on PyPI at this task's own build time**: requires `>=3.9`; pure Python (`py3-none-any` wheel only). Its own two declared dependencies (`lxml>=3.1.0`, `typing_extensions>=4.9.0`) were both already satisfied by existing pins (`lxml==6.1.3` from Task 2.4.b) — confirmed live: `make lock` (using 2.4.c's own `-c`-constrained mechanism) gained exactly ONE new entry in both lockfiles, `python-docx==1.2.0` (2 hashes, wheel + sdist, no sdist-only surprise), no removals, no version bumps elsewhere, no repeat of the cross-lockfile conflict class. `make lock-check`/`make audit` both clean.

  **Multi-block shape confirmed correct, not PDF's flattened one**: unlike PDF's plain text, a DOCX genuinely carries semantic heading structure via Word's own built-in paragraph styles — `extract_docx()` produces HTML's richer multi-`ContentBlock` shape, each with a real, non-empty `heading_path` where applicable, matching the user's own expectation exactly.

  **Heading-level detection, confirmed live, not assumed**: `paragraph.style.name` for Word's built-in heading styles is exactly `"Heading 1"` through `"Heading 9"` — confirmed by building and re-reading a real probe document. Word's own level-0 `add_heading(level=0)` call maps to the SEPARATE `"Title"` style, confirmed not to collide with `"Heading N"` parsing. Detection is scoped to levels 1–6 only via `^Heading ([1-6])$`, matching HTML's own h1–h6 scope exactly (a symmetric design choice, not arbitrary) — `"Heading 7/8/9"` are real built-in styles but deliberately out of scope, the same way HTML has no h7/h8/h9. Confirmed live that the auto-generated `"TOC Heading"` style does NOT collide (it ends with, not starts with, "Heading"). Non-English Word templates (e.g. `"Titre 1"`) are not handled — already covered by this project's own existing "English only for v1" constraint (docs/SPEC.md §3), not a new gap needing its own marker.

  **heading_path algorithm: the SAME document-order stack algorithm as `extract_html.py`'s own, confirmed to apply directly, not a divergent one.** The difference is that DOCX needs none of HTML's own "an h3 is a DOM SIBLING of h2, never its child" workaround — `document.paragraphs` is already a flat, ordered sequence with no nesting concept at all, so the identical stack-based breadcrumb algorithm applies even more directly here. Verified against a real 4-block, 3-level fixture (`nested_headings.docx`): identical path shape to HTML's own `nested_headings.html` fixture, including a later sibling Heading 2 correctly ending both the prior Heading 2 and Heading 3.

  **`title`: `core_properties.title`, confirmed live to need normalization.** python-docx returns an empty STRING (`""`), not `None`, when no title is set — confirmed by building and re-reading a real probe document with no title property set. Normalized to `None` here so `ExtractedContent`'s own "no title" contract stays consistent with `extract_html.py`'s behavior. Same honest caveat as PDF's `/Info /Title`: may be empty or unreliable depending on how the document was authored — but structurally more meaningful than PDF's when populated, a real Word "File > Properties > Title" field, not a loosely-defined producer convention.

  **Fixture construction: python-docx's own document-writing API, considerably simpler than the PDF fixtures' hand-rolled byte-offset construction** — `doc.add_heading()`/`doc.add_paragraph()` directly, no manual object/xref assembly needed at all, since python-docx (unlike pypdf) has a real writing API. Generator committed at `backend/tests/fixtures/docx/generate_fixtures.py`, matching the discipline just established for PDF — confirmed live it reproduces both fixtures and all tests still pass after a fresh run. Confirmed via `--collect-only` that pytest's own default `python_files` pattern never picks it up.

  **Malformed input: confirmed live, same precedent as `extract_pdf.py` (left uncaught, not wrapped).** A DOCX is a ZIP archive — truncating a real fixture's own bytes raises `zipfile.BadZipFile` (a standard-library exception, not python-docx-specific), already clear and specific. python-docx also exposes its own `docx.opc.exceptions.PackageNotFoundError` for certain other package-level failures; neither is caught or re-wrapped here, for the same "no existing caller-facing contract needs a different shape" reasoning as PDF.

  **`chunk_text()`/`compute_content_hash()` proven against real DOCX-extracted content from the start** — per the duplication check after 2.4.b/c/d's own finding that this coverage gap must not recur for a third format. Confirmed live and in a committed test: zero code changes needed in `chunking.py` for either function to handle DOCX's shape correctly, confirming the shared `ExtractedContent` abstraction really does eliminate format-specific branching downstream, exactly as intended.

- 2026-10-05: Step 2.4.f — TXT/MD extraction primitives (new `backend/app/ingest/extract_text.py`: `extract_text()`, `extract_markdown()`, both reusing 2.4.b's own `ExtractedContent`/`ContentBlock` unchanged; no new dependency; new `backend/tests/test_extract_text.py` plus two new real fixtures, `backend/tests/fixtures/text/sample.txt` and `backend/tests/fixtures/markdown/nested_headings.md`; `read_text_fixture()`/`read_markdown_fixture()` added to `conftest.py`, matching the established helper convention).

  **No-new-dependency decision re-confirmed on close inspection, not silently assumed still valid**: a bare per-line ATX regex ALONE would have been a real bug, not just incomplete — a `#` at the start of a line inside a fenced code block must not be read as a heading, which needs tracking fence state across lines. The fix is still small (one boolean flag toggled on fence-delimiter lines), so the no-new-dependency decision holds, not overridden — a full Markdown parser library remains unnecessary. The other two named edge cases (an inline code span, a missing space after `#`) needed NO special handling at all: requiring the match to anchor at the START of the line already rejects both, confirmed live against all three cases (fenced `#`, `` `#not a heading` ``, `#hashtag`) before writing the module, not assumed from the regex alone.

  **Two separate functions, not one with a format flag**: matches this project's own established per-format-primitive naming precedent (`extract_html`/`extract_pdf`/`extract_docx` each get a dedicated top-level function) — a format-flag function would reintroduce exactly the caller-side branching separate functions exist to avoid, since the caller already knows which format it has.

  **heading_path: the IDENTICAL document-order stack algorithm proven twice already (`extract_html.py`, `extract_docx.py`), confirmed to apply directly, not a divergent one** — simpler to apply here than even DOCX's case, since Markdown source lines were never nested to begin with (no DOM-sibling reasoning needed at all). Verified against a real 4-heading, 3-level fixture (`nested_headings.md`) producing the identical path shape as both prior fixtures of the same name.

  **Title: the first level-1 heading, a common real-world convention (README.md's own H1 routinely doubles as a page title) — but DELIBERATELY NOT excluded from `word_count`, a genuine, reasoned divergence from HTML/PDF/DOCX, not an inconsistency.** In those three formats, title metadata lives in a field structurally separate from any body heading (an HTML `<title>` tag, PDF's `/Info/Title`, DOCX's `core_properties.title` — none ever render in that format's own body). Markdown's title IS also a real, visible body heading; a reader genuinely sees it on the page, so excluding its own words from `word_count` would make the reported count silently lower than what the page actually shows. It still participates normally in the heading_path stack for everything that follows, exactly like any other heading.

  **Encoding: strict UTF-8, no silent fallback — confirmed live, both functions.** A `latin-1`-style fallback would never raise on any byte sequence at all, which is exactly the problem: it would "succeed" on genuinely wrong-encoding input and silently ingest mojibake into the knowledge base with nothing to signal the mistake. A loud, specific `UnicodeDecodeError` is more honest and actionable, matching `extract_pdf.py`'s/`extract_docx.py`'s own "fail cleanly, don't paper over it" philosophy for malformed bytes. `str` input is passed through with no decoding at all.

  **Cross-extractor consistency pass (design point 7), done, no fix needed.** `ExtractedContent` return type and the "let the library's/stdlib's own clean exception propagate, never wrap it" philosophy are both genuinely uniform across all five functions (`extract_pdf`'s `PyPdfError`, `extract_docx`'s `zipfile.BadZipFile`, `extract_text`/`extract_markdown`'s `UnicodeDecodeError`). Two apparent differences were checked and found to be deliberate, justified consequences of each format's own nature, not accidental drift: (1) input type — `bytes`-only for the two true binary formats (PDF/DOCX, where a string representation makes no sense), `str`-only for HTML (decoding already happens upstream, an unchanged Task-2.4.b design choice, correctly left untouched), `bytes | str` for TXT/MD (the task's own requested signature, and a genuinely common case for text formats specifically — a caller might already hold a decoded string); (2) malformed-input behavior — HTML's own parser (BeautifulSoup/lxml) is tolerant BY DESIGN and never raises on malformed input (proven at 2.4.b), while PDF/DOCX/TXT/MD's own parsers genuinely do raise on structurally broken input — this is each library's own documented, correct behavior, not something to force into uniformity.

  ASSUMPTION: none needed — the regex edge cases, the fence-tracking fix, the title/word_count interaction, and the encoding-fallback risk were all reasoned through and verified live.   **Retry-layering reasoning, confirmed live by reading the installed SDK's own source, not assumed from documentation.** `openai`'s own `AsyncOpenAI` client defaults to `max_retries=2`; its `_should_retry()`/`_calculate_retry_timeout()` (read directly from `openai._base_client`) retry on 408/409/429/5xx, honor a server `Retry-After` header when present, otherwise apply exponential backoff with 0–25% jitter. Relied on entirely, not overridden, and nothing of this project's own added on top: the SDK's own inner retry budget is small and fixed, always resolving (success or final raise) within one `embed_dense()` call; Step 2.1's own job-level retry (`mark_job_failed()`'s own backoff) only ever engages AFTER that inner budget is exhausted, governing the NEXT, wholly separate attempt at the WHOLE job — different time scales, different failure granularities, confirmed by reading `app/worker.py` directly, no multiplicative stacking risk.

A small fake `_FakeClient`/`_FakeEmbeddingsResource` stands in for the real SDK client, recording each call's own `model`/`input` for assertion. Chosen over a recorded-real-response fixture: simpler, no fixture file to keep in sync with the SDK's own response schema across future `openai` version bumps, and the one real live call this task made already serves as the "verify live once" step design point 6 asked for — the committed suite itself never touches the network, confirmed by a fresh offline run.

- 2026-10-06: Tooling fix during Task 2.5.b — `onnxruntime` dependency-resolution conflict, recorded as its own decision entry (not folded into 2.5.b's own entry), matching the precedent set for the earlier charset-normalizer/`-c` tooling fix at Task 2.4.c.

  **Root cause, confirmed live, not assumed.** Adding `fastembed==0.8.1` let `uv`'s `--universal` resolver pick `onnxruntime==1.30.0` for both lockfiles. `make lock`/`make lock-check` both succeeded with this pin, but `make audit` then failed — `pip-audit`'s own internal dry-run install reported "Could not find a version that satisfies the requirement onnxruntime==1.30.0 (from versions: 1.17.0...1.23.2)" on this real machine, even though `uv` had just resolved cleanly moments earlier. Investigated live rather than dismissed as a caching artifact: `pip cache purge` (455.5MB removed) did not change the result, ruling out staleness; `pip index versions onnxruntime -vvv` revealed the real reason — none of `onnxruntime`'s wheel tags for 1.24.1+ are compatible with this machine. Cross-checked against the raw PyPI simple index directly (`curl https://pypi.org/simple/onnxruntime/`): `onnxruntime` genuinely dropped macOS x86_64 wheels starting at version 1.24.0 — only macOS `arm64` wheels ship from 1.24.0 onward. This dev machine is macOS x86_64 (confirmed via `sw_vers`/`platform.mac_ver()`), so `uv`'s `--universal` resolution (which only checks "does a wheel exist somewhere across all platforms," not "can this specific machine install it") picked a version genuinely uninstallable here. `1.23.2` is the last version shipping both macOS arm64 and x86_64 wheels, confirmed with full coverage (macOS arm64+x86_64, manylinux x86_64+aarch64, Windows) and within `fastembed==0.8.1`'s own declared constraint for Python 3.12. Production Linux/Docker targets were never affected (real manylinux wheels exist for 1.30.0 on both x86_64/aarch64) — this is a dev-machine-only gap, not a production risk.

  **The fix needed a different mechanism than first asked for, confirmed live before applying it.** A plain `pyproject.toml` `dependencies`-list pin (`onnxruntime==1.23.2`, matching the platformdirs/charset-normalizer precedent) was tried first, per the first-round approval. `make lock` failed: "No solution found when resolving dependencies for split (markers: python_full_version >= '3.14')... fastembed depends on onnxruntime{python_full_version >= '3.14'}>=1.24.2 and widgetplatform depends on onnxruntime==1.23.2... unsatisfiable." Three different marker-scoping attempts on the pin itself (bare; `; python_version == '3.12'`; `; python_version >= '3.11' and python_version < '3.13'`, matching fastembed's own exact declared bucket boundary) all failed identically, as did `--fork-strategy requires-python`. Root cause: `uv`'s `--universal` resolution must find one consistent answer across every theoretical Python-version split any dependency's markers reference (including 3.13/3.14), not just the one this project's own `requires-python = "==3.12.*"` actually targets — a direct pin collides with `fastembed`'s own separate, marker-scoped `onnxruntime` constraint for those unreachable splits, unsatisfiable no matter how the pin's own marker is scoped. Confirmed live (via a throwaway temp file, before touching any real project file) that `uv pip compile`'s own `--overrides <file>` flag resolves this cleanly — it forces a single package to a specific version unconditionally, bypassing marker-conflict checking entirely, rather than trying to add a new, separately-satisfiable constraint into the graph. Paused and asked before substituting this different mechanism for what was literally asked for (matching the same pause-before-detour precedent set before the earlier `-c`/`--constraints` fix at Task 2.4.c); explicitly confirmed by the user before applying it.

  **The fix, applied and proven.** `backend/requirements-overrides.txt` (new file): `onnxruntime==1.23.2`, with a header comment explaining the root cause and why a plain pin couldn't express it. `Makefile`'s shared `UV_COMPILE` variable (used by `lock`/`lock-upgrade`/`lock-check` alike) extended with `--overrides backend/requirements-overrides.txt` — applies automatically to all three targets with no per-target edits, unlike the 2.4.c `-c` fix's own per-target customization. `backend/pyproject.toml`'s own `onnxruntime==1.23.2` entry kept as a plain, unscoped pin (the three failed marker-scoping attempts were all reverted) — the override, not the pin's own marker, is what makes this take effect; the pin stays mainly for documentation/intent, the override is load-bearing. Regenerated both lockfiles: `onnxruntime` now resolves to `1.23.2` in both, confirmed via direct grep (hash `sha256:0be6a37a45e6719db5120e9986fcd30ea205ac8103fd1fb74b6c33348327a0cc`, 22 hashes total, no sdist-only entries). **Proven, not just resolved**: `pip install --require-hashes -r backend/requirements-dev.lock` (a real install, not `--dry-run`) now genuinely succeeds on this machine — installed `onnxruntime-1.23.2` and the rest of `fastembed`'s tree cleanly, no "could not find a version" error, the exact failure this fix targets. `make lock-check`: exit code 0, confirmed explicitly (zero diff lines against the committed lockfiles). `make audit`: "No known vulnerabilities found" — clean, where it had failed outright before this fix.

  **No unrelated package drift — checked, not assumed.** Comparing against the pre-override (1.30.0-pinned) lockfile state, 5 additional packages are now present that weren't before: `coloredlogs`, `humanfriendly`, `mpmath`, `sympy`, `pyreadline3`. Investigated each via its own lockfile `# via` comment: all five trace directly and legitimately to `onnxruntime==1.23.2`'s own (older, slightly larger) dependency tree — `coloredlogs`→onnxruntime direct, `sympy`→onnxruntime direct, `humanfriendly`→coloredlogs, `mpmath`→sympy, `pyreadline3`→humanfriendly (marker-gated to `sys_platform == 'win32'`, never actually installed here). All confirmed with real hash coverage (2 hashes each, no sdist-only entries) — genuine transitive consequences of the version downgrade, not unexplained resolver drift.

  **Note for whoever next hits a 3rd, unrelated dependency conflict of this kind**: this project now has two distinct `uv` mechanisms, and reaching for the wrong one wastes time. `-c`/`--constraints <file>` (Task 2.4.c) forces a package *shared between* the runtime and dev dependency graphs to agree on one version *across the two separately-compiled lockfiles* — a cross-lockfile agreement problem. `--overrides <file>` (this task) forces a *single* package to a specific version *unconditionally*, bypassing marker-based constraint satisfiability checking entirely — the fix when one package's own upstream constraint graph is unsatisfiable for a Python version this project will never actually run on, and a plain `dependencies`-list pin can't express that no matter how its own marker is scoped. Reach for `-c` when two lockfiles disagree with each other; reach for `--overrides` when one package's own transitive constraint graph is internally unsatisfiable for an unreachable split. Fully stated in the Makefile's own `UV_COMPILE` comment, not just here.

  ASSUMPTION: none. UNCERTAIN: none. TODO: none new. Current/next/counter unchanged by this entry — folded into 2.5.b's own task, not a step of its own.

  Verified: `pip install --require-hashes -r backend/requirements-dev.lock` (real install) succeeded cleanly. `make lock-check` exit code 0, zero diff lines. `make audit`: no known vulnerabilities. `git status --short` at this point showed `backend/pyproject.toml`, `backend/requirements.lock`, `backend/requirements-dev.lock`, `Makefile` (modified) plus `backend/requirements-overrides.txt` (new) — all expected, still mid-task (2.5.b's own feature code not yet built), `.env` correctly absent.

- 2026-10-06: Step 2.5.b — sparse (BM25-style) embedding primitive (extends `backend/app/ingest/embedding.py`: `embed_sparse()`, `SparseVectorData`, `SPARSE_EMBEDDING_MODEL`; new dependency `fastembed==0.8.1` plus the `onnxruntime==1.23.2` override detour recorded as its own separate decision entry above; new `backend/tests/test_embedding_sparse.py`). SECURITY rigor applied per the Step 2.5 decision entry's own note, matching 2.5.a.

  **A design premise corrected live, not assumed from the task's own wording.** The task's own design point 3 called for `embed_sparse()` to produce "RAW TERM-FREQUENCY" values. Reading the installed `fastembed.sparse.bm25.Bm25` class's own source (`_term_frequency()`) and inspecting real output together disproved this literally: `Bm25.embed()` does not emit bare occurrence counts. It computes the TF-SATURATION half of the BM25 formula — `count * (k + 1) / (count + k * (1 - b + b * doc_len / avg_len))`, defaults `k=1.2, b=0.75, avg_len=256.0` — confirmed live (a single occurrence in a 3-token document returns `≈1.678`, not `1.0`). The class's own docstring confirms this is deliberate and matches Qdrant's own documented pairing: `Qdrant/bm25` supplies TF-saturation, Qdrant's own `modifier="idf"` (already configured at Task 1.3.c on this exact sparse vector slot) supplies the remaining IDF factor at query time — together the two halves form the complete, standard BM25 score. Flagged to the user before writing any code; confirmed by the user that the task's own "raw term-frequency" wording was imprecise, not a design requirement to engineer around — `embed_sparse()` uses fastembed's default behavior directly, unmodified, and the wording here (and in the code's own comments/tests) describes the actual TF-saturation property plainly rather than repeating the imprecise original phrasing.

  **Model download/caching, investigated live, mirroring the `tiktoken`/`cl100k_base` finding from 2.4.c.** Confirmed `~/.cache` was empty before any use; the `Qdrant/bm25` model's own first instantiation fetches 18 small files (per-language stopword lists) from HuggingFace Hub (~1.4–3.7s cold across repeated measurements, including the download), then caches them under `FASTEMBED_CACHE_PATH`/the OS temp dir — a second call on the same process is ~0.000s. A new Phase 7 Open marker was added, mirroring the `tiktoken` marker's own wording and reasoning exactly (see Open markers section).

- 2026-10-06: Step 2.5.e — end-to-end `embed_and_upsert()` (extends `backend/app/ingest/qdrant_writer.py`: `embed_and_upsert()`; extends `backend/tests/test_qdrant_writer.py` with a new section). [SECURITY]-level rigor applied throughout, matching the rest of Step 2.5 despite docs/SPEC.md not tagging this step `[SECURITY]` (per the Step 2.5 decision entry's own note) — this is the step's own hostile tenant-isolation proof.

  **Batching, re-confirmed, not re-assumed**: `embed_dense()`/`embed_sparse()` each already accept the WHOLE per-document chunk-text list and make exactly ONE call internally (one real OpenAI request; one `fastembed` `model.embed()` call) — confirmed at 2.5.a/b, re-confirmed here by direct reading before writing `embed_and_upsert()`. This function therefore calls each exactly once per document, never once per chunk.

  **All-or-nothing atomicity, achieved by construction, not by added try/except machinery.** Both embedding calls are awaited BEFORE any point is built or upserted. Because each is a single batched call over the whole chunk list, there is no mechanism by which "chunk 3 of 5 failed" could leave chunks 1-2's own embeddings sitting around half-used — the one call either returns every embedding or raises before returning any of them. A raised exception propagates UNCAUGHT and unwrapped (matching `embed_dense()`'s own "no redundant translation layer" precedent from 2.5.a), with zero points ever built or upserted for that document. **Proven live, not just reasoned**: a stubbed `embed_dense()` raising mid-call for a real 3-chunk document left the collection at exactly 0 points, not a partial 0-2 subset.

  **Nearly-empty content: no explicit `is_nearly_empty` check added, deliberately.** That decision belongs to whichever caller has it — 2.6's future adapter can simply choose not to call `embed_and_upsert()` at all for such content; adding a second, redundant check here would duplicate a decision the caller is already positioned to make (rule 11). Proven live that the natural composition already handles the case correctly for free: a real empty-body HTML document through real `extract_html()` produces `ExtractedContent(blocks=(), word_count=0)`, `chunk_text()` on that produces zero chunks, and the already-established empty-input handling at every layer below (`embed_dense([])`/`embed_sparse([])` return `[]` with no API call, 2.5.a/b; `upsert_points()` with an empty list is a clean no-op with no network call, 2.5.d) composes into a clean, error-free no-op — not merely assumed from the individual pieces.

  **THE DECISIVE HOSTILE ISOLATION PROOF**: two real documents (different real content, different tenants) run through `embed_and_upsert()` independently against the SAME real collection. `tenant_filter(tenant_a)` found exactly tenant A's 2 points (correct text, correct `client_id`); `tenant_filter(tenant_b)` found exactly tenant B's 2 points; the two id sets were confirmed disjoint. This is the complete, final version of this property proven through the single real top-level callable function — not synthetic points (`test_qdrant_isolation.py`, Task 1.3.d) or an isolated sub-component (2.5.c's own one-tenant-then-two-tenant proof through `build_payload()` alone).

  **The idempotency property**, now proven through this exact top-level function too (not just `upsert_points()` directly, as at 2.5.d): the same document run through `embed_and_upsert()` twice left the collection at 2 points, not 4.

real `job_type` and call `enqueue()`/`claim_next_job()` for real (2.1.c/d),
and the first real caller of `fetch_with_redirects()` (2.3), `extract_html()`
(2.4.b), `chunk_text()` (2.4.c) and `embed_and_upsert()` (2.5.e) together.

| ID | Goal | Status |
|----|------|--------|
| 2.6.a | File/meta-tag domain verification LOGIC: the actual HTTP fetch (`fetch_with_redirects()`, 2.3) plus well-known-path/meta-tag-content parsing for the two methods whose schema already exists (`verified_domains.method`, 2.3.e). Resolves the Step-2.3.e-owned marker. Self-contained, blocks nothing below. | Done |
| 2.6.b | Two of the four `2.4–2.8`-range markers resolved, now that a real `job_type` vocabulary and real `enqueue()` caller finally exist: (1) `job_type` validation — `enqueue()` rejects an unregistered `job_type` up front; (2) `enqueue()`'s unchecked `source_id` FK fixed (catch the violation, roll back, translate — matching `create_site_key()`'s own precedent) | Done |
| 2.6.c | Per-URL ingest primitive: `fetch_with_redirects()` → `extract_html()` → a content-hash change-check against the stored `documents.content_hash` (skip re-embedding when unchanged) → `chunk_text()` → `embed_and_upsert()`; creates/updates the `documents` row. First real caller of all four primitives together | Done |
| 2.6.d | `urls` adapter: the real `job_type` handler (`sources.config = {"urls": [...]}`) calling 2.6.c once per tenant-supplied URL, enforcing verified-domains-only. First real `JOB_HANDLERS` registration and `enqueue()`/`claim_next_job()` caller | Done |
| 2.6.e | `crawl` adapter: `job_type` handler (`sources.config = {"seed_url": "..."}`) — sitemap-first discovery (`robots.txt`'s own `Sitemap:` directive first, else the fixed `/sitemap.xml` path), otherwise BFS same-domain link-following (the whole verified domain, not path-scoped; URL-normalized dedup — case-insensitive scheme/host, no trailing slash, no fragment, query string preserved) — `protego` (escalated from stdlib `urllib.robotparser`, see this row's own decision log entry) for disallow rules, a `Settings`-configurable User-Agent, 2 concurrent requests per host with a delay, the per-crawl safety cap; feeds discovered URLs through 2.6.c. Also builds the heartbeat progress-reporting mechanism (per-page heartbeat writes during the crawl's own loop), resolving the Step-2.1.f-owned `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER` marker | Done |
| 2.6.f | Close-out: a minimal stuck-job reaper (`claimed_at`/lease-expiry sweep), resolving the Step-2.1.e-owned marker; live-confirms 2.6.e's own heartbeat-progress mechanism genuinely keeps a long-running crawl from being marked stale; update PROJECT_SPEC.md | Done |

closing the last of the four `2.4–2.8`-range markers, waiting since Task
2.1.e.

investigation (2.6.e) that confirmed `xml.etree.ElementTree`'s own
real behavior already closes the attacks `ruff`/bandit's S314 rule
warns about, avoiding an unnecessary second dependency.

sourcing revisit; the sitemap-index scope limit; the duplication
candidates flagged across 2.6.a–d) — not repeated here.

- 2026-10-06: Step 2.6 breakdown approved (docs-only, no code) — six subtasks 2.6.a–2.6.f (see the task list above), based on docs/SPEC.md §5.1 (the `urls`/`crawl` source types), §5.5 (crawler/URL-fetch safety rules) and the research reported separately before this approval (confirming the step's own five owned open questions: the crawl algorithm, robots.txt/User-Agent, the `urls`-vs-`crawl` code-path split, file/meta-tag verification's placement, and the resolution of all four `2.4–2.8`-range markers). Five decisions confirmed:

  (a) **Crawl algorithm**: BFS, no explicit depth limit beyond the plan's own per-crawl safety cap (docs/SPEC.md §5.5) — the cap is the real stopping condition, a separate depth limit would be a second, redundant one. Sitemap location: check `robots.txt`'s own `Sitemap:` directive first; fall back to the fixed `/sitemap.xml` well-known path only if `robots.txt` doesn't specify one or doesn't exist — the standard, well-known convention (a `robots.txt`-declared sitemap takes precedence over guessing the default path). URL normalization, applied before dedup: case-insensitive scheme/host, strip trailing slashes, strip fragments, **preserve query strings** (they can produce genuinely different content, unlike a fragment) — two URLs are the same page only after this normalization, never compared raw. Same-domain scope: the **whole verified domain**, not just the seed URL's own path subtree — `verified_domains` verifies at the domain level, not the path level, so scoping discovery any narrower would be an invented restriction docs/SPEC.md never states.

  (b) **User-Agent and robots.txt parsing**. User-Agent: a `Settings`-configurable field (not hardcoded), default `"SamoFlowBot/1.0 (+https://samoflow.app/bot)"` — a placeholder pattern, since the real production domain isn't decided yet; kept easy to change later rather than baked into a constant. robots.txt parsing: start with Python's stdlib `urllib.robotparser` (no new dependency, rule 8 N/A) — to be verified live against real-world `robots.txt` files at 2.6.e's own build time; if genuinely inadequate, escalate to a well-known third-party library with its own rule-8 justification at that point, never a silent substitution decided in passing.

  (c) **`urls` vs `crawl`: one shared primitive, two job types, confirmed.** `sources.type`'s own `CheckConstraint` already names these as two distinct values (Task 2.1.a); the components table's own "adapters" (plural) naming matches. One shared per-URL ingest primitive (2.6.c) underneath; `urls`' own `sources.config` shape is `{"urls": [...]}` (a literal tenant-supplied list, no discovery); `crawl`'s own shape is `{"seed_url": "..."}` (one seed, discovery finds the rest). Neither shape existed anywhere in the codebase before this decision — `sources.config` was deliberately left unstructured JSONB at 2.1.a for exactly this reason (adapter-specific shape, decided when the adapter is actually built, rule 11).

  (d) **File/meta-tag domain verification LOGIC placed at 2.6.a, first subtask, as proposed.** Not a hard dependency of the `urls`/`crawl` adapters (they only ever check `verified_domains.status`, indifferent to which of the three methods produced it — DNS verification already works end to end), but small, fully self-contained, and blocks nothing below — finishing it first means domain verification is genuinely complete before building adapters on top of it, rather than leaving a known-incomplete piece sitting alongside new work.

  (e) **All four `2.4–2.8`-range markers resolved, each now explicitly in 2.6's own scope** (previously each was a judgment call deferred until a real caller/long-running handler existed — that precondition is now true): `job_type` validation — `enqueue()` will reject an unregistered `job_type` up front once 2.6 establishes the first real vocabulary, built at 2.6.b (not merely documented). `enqueue()`'s unchecked `source_id` FK — fixed for real at 2.6.b, matching the marker's own stated trigger ("once a real caller exists"), not deferred again. Stuck-job reaper — a minimal `claimed_at`/lease-expiry sweep, built at 2.6.f: crawls genuinely can run long, so this is no longer a hypothetical risk. `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER` — resolved by having the crawl handler itself write progress heartbeats periodically during its own long-running work (crawling has natural per-page checkpoints to hang this on), not by growing the static threshold, which would only delay the identical problem at a bigger N; the mechanism is built at 2.6.e (inside the crawl handler's own loop, where the per-page checkpoints actually are) and live-confirmed at 2.6.f's own close-out.

- 2026-10-06: Step 2.6.a — file/meta-tag domain verification logic (extends `backend/app/ingest/domain_verification.py`: `FILE_VERIFICATION_PATH`, `META_TAG_NAME`, `check_file_verification()`, `check_meta_tag_verification()`, plus two private URL-builder functions; extends `backend/app/ingest/repository.py`: `claim_domain()` gains a `method: str = "dns"` parameter, `confirm_verification()`'s dispatch now method-based; new/extended `backend/tests/test_domain_verification.py`, `backend/tests/test_ingest_repository.py`). [SECURITY]-level rigor throughout, per the Step 2.5 precedent and because this logic was deliberately deferred until the SSRF guard existed.

  **A real, necessary scope expansion found and resolved before building, not assumed away.** Reading `confirm_verification()`'s actual current code (not from memory, per this task's own instruction) confirmed it unconditionally calls `check_dns_verification()` — there was no method-based dispatch to extend, only a hardcoded single branch to replace. Reading further, `claim_domain()` itself hardcodes `method="dns"` with **no parameter at all** — extending `confirm_verification()`'s dispatch alone would have left the new branches structurally unreachable, since nothing could ever create a `file`/`meta_tag` row in the first place. This is not an unplanned gap: Task 2.2.c's own decision entry explicitly named this exact moment — *"No `method` parameter — DNS-only right now... a parameter for a choice that doesn't exist yet would be speculative (rule 11), added back once 2.3 lands and file/meta-tag return."* That trigger is now true. Flagged plainly before writing any code, then resolved: `claim_domain(self, domain: str, method: str = "dns")` — the default keeps every existing caller's own behavior byte-for-byte unchanged (confirmed: all 25 pre-existing `test_ingest_repository.py` tests pass unmodified). No Python-side validation of `method` added — the DB's own `ck_verified_domains_method` CheckConstraint (2.3.e) already owns that closed vocabulary; duplicating it would be a second, independently-maintained copy of the same rule (rule 11), matching this project's own precedent of trusting a CheckConstraint rather than re-validating in Python (`sources.type`, `documents.status` are treated identically).

  **`confirm_verification()`'s dispatch, extended with a real constraint found and respected, not overlooked.** `test_ingest_repository.py` has existing tests that monkeypatch `repository_module.check_dns_verification` directly (`_fake_true`/`_fake_false`/`_never_call_check_dns_verification`). A dict-based `{"dns": check_dns_verification, ...}` registry (the shape this project uses elsewhere, e.g. `JOB_HANDLERS`) was considered and rejected: building such a dict at import time would capture the *original* function object, making every one of those existing monkeypatches silently no-op (the patched name would never be consulted) — confirmed by reading those tests before choosing a shape, not assumed. Resolved with a plain `if/elif/else` dispatching on `domain.method`, calling each check function by its own bare, module-level name — preserving exact bare-name monkeypatchability for all three methods, confirmed by the full existing suite passing unmodified plus 6 new tests (2 `claim_domain()` method-parameter tests, 4 `confirm_verification()` dispatch tests — positive and negative for both new methods, matching `check_dns_verification`'s own established test shape exactly) all passing.

  **Well-known path and meta-tag format, both decided here since docs/SPEC.md gives no hint either way** (confirmed by reading §5.5 before deciding, matching `TXT_RECORD_PREFIX`'s own precedent): file verification uses `/.well-known/samoflow-verify.txt` — under `/.well-known/`, matching RFC 8615's own standard convention for exactly this class of "a service proves it controls this site" file (the same directory ACME/Let's Encrypt and others use), with a project-specific filename to avoid colliding with any other service's own file in that directory. The entire body is expected to be exactly the token, compared after stripping leading/trailing whitespace only (not a substring search) — tolerates a trailing newline (near-universal from real text editors/webservers) without weakening the "nothing else" requirement, the identical usability reasoning already applied to the DNS method's own TXT record format. Meta-tag verification uses `<meta name="samoflow-site-verification" content="TOKEN">`, expected inside `<head>` — mirroring Google Search Console's own identical real-world convention. Parsed with BeautifulSoup (`"lxml"`, matching `extract_html.py`'s own parser choice, Task 2.4.b) and searched specifically within `soup.head`, never a raw string search across the page — proven live: a token present elsewhere on the page (an HTML comment, body text) but absent from the correct tag's own `content` attribute correctly returns `False`, not a false positive.

  **Exception handling, confirmed live, not assumed.** Two exception types are caught in both check functions, matching `check_dns_verification()`'s own "any clean failure means not verified" contract exactly: `UnsafeFetchError` (`safe_fetch.py`, 2.3 — the SSRF rejection itself, plus the size-cap/redirect-cap/unsupported-scheme cases) and `httpx.HTTPError` — confirmed live, by reading the installed `httpx==0.28.1`'s own real exception hierarchy (not assumed), that *every* transport/timeout/protocol error (`ConnectError`, `ConnectTimeout`, `ReadTimeout`, `RemoteProtocolError`, etc.) derives from this one base class, since `safe_fetch.py`'s own `_fetch_pinned()` makes a real httpx connection with no try/except of its own, letting these propagate raw. A non-matching token, a missing meta tag, or a non-2xx status (e.g. a 404) need no special-casing at all — `fetch_with_redirects()` never raises for an ordinary non-redirect status code, and that response's own body simply won't equal the expected token, proven live with a dedicated 404 test.

  **A genuine testability problem, found and resolved with the established pattern, not a workaround.** Both check functions fetch over `https://` in production — deliberately, not merely matching the task's own literal wording: verification is a proof-of-control mechanism, and plaintext HTTP would make that proof interceptable by anyone on the network path, a materially weaker guarantee for no benefit. But a real local test server (this project's own established testing discipline, matching 2.3's precedent — "not mocked responses") speaks plain HTTP, not TLS, so the hardcoded `https://{domain}` URL is not directly testable against one. Resolved by applying this file's own already-documented "pure-function-plus-thin-IO-wrapper split" (its own header comment, from 2.2.d) one level further: URL construction factored into two tiny private functions (`_file_verification_url()`, `_meta_tag_verification_url()`), monkeypatched directly in tests to point at the local server, while the real `fetch_with_redirects()` call, the real SSRF validation, and the real token-comparison logic all stay genuinely exercised, completely unmodified. The existing `_fake_is_unsafe_except_loopback()` helper (`test_safe_fetch.py`, 2.3.d, already documented there as "not extracted... only this file needs it so far") now has a second user — a local copy was added to `test_domain_verification.py` instead of extracting a shared version, noted explicitly as a candidate for a future duplication check, matching this project's own established "flag, don't fix mid-task" discipline.

  ASSUMPTION: the well-known path/meta-tag name are new, project-specific conventions with no existing dashboard UI to expose them yet (Phase 6) — if the dashboard's own onboarding flow later needs a different name/path for display reasons, these constants are the one place to change, not duplicated anywhere else. UNCERTAIN: none. TODO: none new beyond the duplication-check candidate noted above. Current task set to 2.6.b, next to 2.6.c. **Counter reaches n=1.**

These are NOT the same source of truth and are not expected to ever be byte-identical: `VALID_JOB_TYPES` answers "may a real caller ever enqueue this" (a write-boundary concern, owned by `repository.py`); `JOB_HANDLERS` answers "which function processes this job_type" (a worker-dispatch concern) and will always be a superset, keeping `"noop"`/`"sleep"` indefinitely for loop-mechanics testing that no real vocabulary entry should ever need to accommodate. The real invariant going forward: `JOB_HANDLERS` must register a handler for every value in `VALID_JOB_TYPES` once 2.6.d/e build them (so a real job never reaches the permanent-failure path) — not kept in lockstep as one shared dict.
