[SECURITY]-level rigor throughout: the first code path fetching a tenant-supplied URL and writing into the shared, multi-tenant Qdrant collection.

  **A design-wording mismatch found by reading the real source, not followed literally.** The task's own design named `chunk_text()` then `embed_and_upsert()` as two separate steps. Reading `embed_and_upsert()`'s own real code (2.5.e) first showed it already calls `chunk_text()` AND `compute_content_hash()` internally — calling `chunk_text()` again here would discard real chunking work for nothing, and its output doesn't even match `embed_and_upsert()`'s own signature (`content: ExtractedContent`, not `list[Chunk]`). `ingest_url()` calls `compute_content_hash()` directly — a second call to a pure, cheap, deterministic function over the same in-memory content, not real duplicated work — only for the content-hash COMPARISON against the stored row, a concern `embed_and_upsert()` has no reason to know about; `chunk_text()` is never called from this file at all.

  **`is_nearly_empty` persistence: a new, deliberately orthogonal boolean column on `documents`, not a new `status` value.** A nearly-empty page (2.4.b's own `ExtractedContent.is_nearly_empty`) is a content-quality signal, not a pipeline-state signal — collapsing it into `status` (`pending`/`extracted`/`failed`) would conflate two different concepts (rule 11: the smallest correct mechanism, not a repurposed column). `alembic revision --autogenerate` correctly detected the new column; hand-fixed only for this project's own lint style (the already-known `script.py.mako` template gap). Migration cycle (upgrade→downgrade→upgrade) proven live against the real test database.

  **`IngestResult` shape: a plain dataclass, `status: str` (one of `"ingested"`/`"unchanged"`/`"skipped"`/`"failed"`) plus optional `document_id`/`reason` — matching this project's own established no-enum-anywhere style, confirmed by grep before choosing it.**

  **Atomicity, one layer up from `embed_and_upsert()`'s own, decided and built as specified.** If `embed_and_upsert()` raises, `ingest_url()` does NOT touch the `documents` row at all — it stays at its previous state (or doesn't exist yet) — and the exception propagates UNCAUGHT, deliberately never converted into `IngestResult(status="failed", ...)`. Unlike a fetch failure (isolated to one URL), an `embed_and_upsert()` failure is almost always infrastructure-level (OpenAI/Qdrant unreachable, a bad key) affecting every URL in a job equally — swallowing it per-URL would hide a systemic failure behind many individually-"failed" results instead of letting the job-level retry/backoff (`mark_job_failed()`, Step 2.1) handle it once, correctly. `document_id` is resolved BEFORE calling `embed_and_upsert()` (reusing the existing row's id, or generating a fresh one) and reused unchanged afterward — `build_point_id()`'s own determinism (2.5.c) depends on the same `document_id` being used on every re-run of the same URL.

  **The import-confinement guard tripped on `AsyncQdrantClient`, confirmed empirically before fixing, not assumed.** Ran `test_qdrant_read_path_guard.py` before deciding, reproducing the predicted failure exactly. Fixed by adding `app/ingest/web_adapter.py` to `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST`, matching `qdrant_writer.py`'s own established precedent exactly (no qdrant-agnostic stand-in makes sense for a bare pass-through parameter) — confirmed NOT needed in `READ_ALLOWLIST`, since this file never calls a method on the client itself, only passes it through to `embed_and_upsert()`.

  **Domain-verification lookup: exact-string match against `verified_domains.domain`, deliberately no normalization applied here.** `verified_domains.domain` is stored exactly as the tenant typed it, per that column's own documented Step-6-owned open marker (recorded at 2.2.a) — inventing a second, independently-decided normalization rule here instead of extending that one marker would risk the two drifting apart. Zero network activity if this check fails: `fetch_with_redirects()` is never even called, proven live via `assert_no_socket_connections()`.

Reading `safe_fetch.py` directly first confirmed `fetch_with_redirects()` never raises for an ordinary non-redirect HTTP status — a 404 just returns normally with whatever body, flowing through the success path (as nearly-empty content), never exercising the "failed" branch at all. Built instead against a genuine connection-level failure: bind a loopback socket to an ephemeral port, close it immediately so nothing listens there, then fetch that port — a real `ConnectError` every time.

  **A real bug found and fixed in the test suite itself, not the production code.** `assert_no_socket_connections()` (test (d)) patches `socket.socket.connect` GLOBALLY — applying it before `qdrant.ensure_collection()` (or before `live_test_services()`'s own teardown had run) broke the real `AsyncQdrantClient`'s own HTTP calls too, not just the intended domain-check proof, confirmed live (`qdrant_client.http.exceptions.ResponseHandlingException`) both as an immediate test failure and, on a second attempt, as a teardown-time failure (pytest tears fixtures down in reverse setup order, so the outer `monkeypatch` fixture's own undo runs AFTER `live_test_services()`'s finalizer, not before). Fixed by scoping the patch tightly with `monkeypatch.context()`, applied only around the `ingest_url()` call itself.

  **C1 [SECURITY] — the redirect-to-unverified-domain gap, found during this check, not assumed safe.** `ingest_url()` checked `verified_domains` once, against the URL's ORIGINAL host, before calling `fetch_with_redirects()` — which re-validates SSRF-safety (the IP) on every hop but has no concept of tenant domain verification at all. A verified domain redirecting to a different, unverified-but-IP-safe external host would have had that host's own content fetched and persisted under the tenant's own `documents` row, defeating domain verification's actual purpose (proving tenant control over the content source), not merely an SSRF concern. **Two mechanisms considered and rejected before building**: re-implementing a redirect-following loop inside `web_adapter.py` (rejected — duplicates `safe_fetch.py`'s own location-header/max-redirects/re-validation logic in a second place); an async per-hop callback added to `fetch_with_redirects()`, re-checking `verified_domains` on every hop including intermediate ones (rejected on reasoning through it explicitly — SSRF-safety and domain-ownership verification are different guarantees; a redirect bouncing through some unrelated-but-safe intermediate host and landing back on the tenant's own verified domain is normal, harmless web behavior, and rejecting it would be over-restrictive for no real benefit). **Mechanism built**: `safe_fetch.py` gains a new public `FetchResult(body: bytes, final_url: httpx.URL)` dataclass; `fetch_with_redirects()`'s return type widens from bare `bytes` to `FetchResult` (it already tracked `current_url` through its own loop — this only exposes it); `safe_fetch()` itself stays completely untouched (it never follows a redirect, so "final" is never distinct from "original" for it). `ingest_url()` gains a small `_domain_is_verified(session, tenant_id, hostname)` helper (dedupes the exact-match `verified_domains` SELECT it already had) and calls it a second time, against `fetch_result.final_url.host`, but ONLY when that differs from the original host — the common no-redirect case pays zero extra query cost. Only the original and final hosts are ever checked, never intermediate hops, matching the reasoning above. `IngestResult`'s own `"skipped"` docstring updated: it no longer universally claims "no fetch was ever attempted" — that stays true for the original-host rejection, but a fetch did happen for this new post-redirect case, with nothing persisted. Mechanical fallout, no behavior change beyond the fix itself: `domain_verification.py`'s two callers (`check_file_verification()`/`check_meta_tag_verification()`) updated to read `.body` off the new `FetchResult`; 3 direct assertions in `test_safe_fetch.py` updated the same way. New live test, `test_web_adapter.py::test_redirect_to_an_unverified_host_is_rejected_with_nothing_persisted`: a real local HTTP server (IPv4, `127.0.0.1`, verified for the tenant) 302-redirects to a second real local HTTP server bound to the IPv6 loopback address (`::1`, NOT verified) — a genuine environment finding along the way: a second IPv4 loopback alias (`127.0.0.2`) was tried first and failed live (`OSError: Can't assign requested address` — most systems, including the one this was built on, don't auto-configure extra IPv4 loopback aliases), while `::1` is a real, independently bindable loopback address on both Linux and macOS with zero special setup, confirmed live before committing to it. Asserts `IngestResult(status="skipped", ...)` naming `::1` and "not verified", zero Qdrant points, and no `documents` row created.

- 2026-10-07: Step 2.6.d — `urls` adapter (new `backend/app/ingest/job_handlers.py`: `handle_ingest_url()`; extends `backend/app/worker.py`: `JOB_HANDLERS` gains `"ingest_url"`, `_noop_handler`/`_sleep_handler`/`_claim_and_process_one_job()`'s own handler call site updated for a signature widening this task required; extends `backend/tests/test_qdrant_read_path_guard.py`'s `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST`; new `backend/tests/test_job_handlers.py`). First real `JOB_HANDLERS` entry beyond `noop`/`sleep` test scaffolding, and the first real caller of the full ingestion pipeline through the actual worker loop.

  **A real, necessary signature widening found before building, not assumed away — flagged, then resolved.** `JOB_HANDLERS`'s existing type, `Callable[[dict], Awaitable[None]]`, matches `noop`/`sleep` exactly because neither ever needs anything beyond its own `payload`. A real handler needs more: `job.tenant_id`/`job.source_id` are first-class `Job` columns, never part of `payload` — stuffing them into every job_type's own payload shape just to preserve the old signature would be a worse, more duplicative design than widening the signature once (rule 11). Resolved by widening `JOB_HANDLERS` to `Callable[[Job], Awaitable[None]]` — the whole row, not just its payload. `_noop_handler`/`_sleep_handler` updated mechanically (`payload` → `job`/`job.payload`, zero behavior change, confirmed by the full existing `test_worker.py` suite — 41 tests — passing unmodified) and `_claim_and_process_one_job()`'s own call site updated to pass `job` instead of `job.payload`.

  **Session/Qdrant-client acquisition: the handler acquires its own, matching `_claim_one()`/`_mark_done()`'s own existing precedent exactly, not a new pattern.** `handle_ingest_url()` opens its own session (`_session_factory()()`) and its own Qdrant client (`qdrant.get_qdrant_client()`, the cached singleton) — no session/client threaded in from `worker.py`, matching how every other per-operation piece of this file already works. `sources.config["urls"]` is read via `job.source_id` (a fresh `Source` lookup), NOT `job.payload` — `sources.config` is the tenant's own persistent declaration of what this source re-checks, reusable by a future scheduled refresh of the same source; `job.payload` is a one-off job's own instructions, and this job_type needs none.

  **Partial-failure / job-success policy — confirmed by reading `ingest_url()`'s own real source before deciding, not assumed, and resolved more precisely than the task's own "at least one succeeded" framing.** Every EXPECTED outcome (domain not verified, a fetch failure) is already returned as an `IngestResult`, never raised; only an `embed_and_upsert()` failure propagates UNCAUGHT, by that function's own already-documented design ("almost always an INFRASTRUCTURE-level problem... that affects every URL in the job equally"). The handler does NOT wrap each `ingest_url()` call in its own try/except: an `IngestResult` of any status lets the loop continue (per-URL-independent outcomes); an exception is left to propagate straight out, matching `ingest_url()`'s own stated intent — continuing to attempt URL 2, 3... after URL 1's infrastructure failure would be pointless, since the same failure would almost certainly recur. `worker.py`'s existing exception handling already does exactly the right thing with a propagated exception (non-permanent `mark_job_failed`, standard backoff/retry) with ZERO new code needed for that case. **Refined policy, more precise than "at least one URL succeeded": the job succeeds if every URL in the list received a definitive, recorded outcome — regardless of how many came back "skipped"/"failed" — and fails/retries only if an unexpected exception interrupted the attempt partway through.** A job whose every URL is skipped (e.g. a tenant pasted URLs for since-revoked domains) still successfully did what it was asked to attempt; retrying it would not change that outcome, matching this project's own no-pointless-retry discipline (the "unknown job_type" permanent-failure precedent). Committed per URL, not once at the end of the loop — `embed_and_upsert()` writes to Qdrant with no rollback tie to the session, so batching the commit would let an EARLIER url's already-real Qdrant points survive a LATER url's exception while its own `documents` row vanished, leaving the two stores out of sync.

  **Heartbeat: deliberately not built here, per the Step 2.6 decision (e)'s own explicit assignment, not a silent skip.** That decision already names 2.6.e specifically as where the heartbeat progress-reporting mechanism is built ("inside the crawl handler's own loop, where the per-page checkpoints actually are") — crawl can discover far more pages than a tenant is likely to hand-paste into a `urls` list. Not risk-free: a sufficiently long tenant-supplied `urls` list could still make this handler's own runtime exceed `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER * worker_poll_interval_seconds` (worker.py), making the heartbeat go stale while the worker is legitimately busy — the exact, already-tracked risk worker.py's own pre-existing ASSUMPTION/Open marker names, now slightly broadened rather than resolved or duplicated into a second mechanism ahead of 2.6.e's own real one.

  **The Qdrant import-confinement guard tripped on `job_handlers.py`, confirmed empirically before fixing, matching `web_adapter.py`'s own identical precedent exactly** — a bare pass-through `client` parameter typed as the real `AsyncQdrantClient`, added to `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST`, correctly absent from `READ_ALLOWLIST` (it calls no method on the client itself, only `qdrant.get_qdrant_client()`, a plain factory function, and forwards the result unmodified to `ingest_url()`).

  ASSUMPTION: none new (the pre-existing `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER` ASSUMPTION in `worker.py` is broadened, not superseded, per the heartbeat note above). UNCERTAIN: none. TODO: none new. A defensive `ValueError` (not a bespoke permanent-failure classification) covers the structurally-impossible-through-the-real-`enqueue()`-path case of a `job.source_id` of `None` for this job_type — not built as a feature, just a guard against a malformed row, taking the ordinary non-permanent handler-raised path. - 2026-10-07: Step 2.6.e — `crawl` adapter, built in two parts of the same task (both recorded together here, as one coherent entry). **Part 1 (discovery)**: new `backend/app/ingest/crawl.py`: `normalize_url()`, `fetch_robots_txt()`, `is_allowed_by_robots()`, `parse_sitemap_xml()`, `discover_sitemap_urls()`, `bfs_discover()`; extends `backend/app/config.py`: `Settings.crawl_user_agent`. **Part 2 (wiring, concurrency, heartbeat)**: extends `crawl.py`: `extract_links()`, `HostThrottle`, `run_crawl()`; extends `backend/app/config.py`: `Settings.crawl_request_delay_seconds`, `Settings.crawl_page_cap`; extends `backend/app/worker.py`: `_write_heartbeat()` renamed to the now-public `write_heartbeat()`, `JOB_HANDLERS["ingest_crawl"]`; extends `backend/app/ingest/job_handlers.py`: `handle_ingest_crawl()`; extends `backend/tests/test_qdrant_read_path_guard.py`'s `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST`; new/extended tests (`backend/tests/test_crawl.py`, 35 total; `backend/tests/test_job_handlers.py`, 6 new).

  **A real, confirmed defect found before building, not assumed away — flagged, then resolved per the Step 2.6 decision's own pre-authorized escalation path [SECURITY-adjacent: crawler politeness/correctness, not a vulnerability].** The task's own design asked to "verify live" that stdlib `urllib.robotparser` handles real-world robots.txt files adequately, including wildcards — it does not. Confirmed empirically, against the pinned Python 3.12.14 (not a different interpreter found first on PATH, which gave a misleadingly different result): stdlib treats `*`/`$` inside a Disallow/Allow path as LITERAL characters, not the de-facto wildcard extensions Google/Bing/most major CMS platforms rely on by default. `Disallow: /*.pdf$`, `Disallow: /search?*`, and `Disallow: /tmp*` were all confirmed to be silently treated as non-matching ("allowed") by stdlib — real patterns site owners intend to block. **Escalated to `protego` (Scrapy's own robots.txt library) under rule 8**, per the Step 2.6 decision's own explicit pre-authorization for exactly this situation, confirmed by the user directly. Confirmed live before adding it (a throwaway venv, then reproduced against the real project dependency set): `protego==0.7.0` correctly respects all three patterns above, plus most-specific-rule-wins precedence regardless of listing order, plus bot-specific-group-overrides-wildcard-group selection — proven in committed tests, not just confirmed once and trusted (`test_is_allowed_by_robots_respects_*`, `test_crawl.py`). Zero dependencies of its own (confirmed against PyPI's real metadata, not just protego's own description) — a single pure-Python `py3-none-any` wheel, no new transitive package, no compiled-extension risk. `make lock`: exactly one new package in both lockfiles (`protego==0.7.0`, 2 hashes matching its one wheel + one sdist). `make lock-check`: clean. `make audit`: no known vulnerabilities. protego's own API confirmed live before relying on it, not assumed from its docs: `Protego.parse(content: str)` takes the full text as ONE string (not a list of lines, unlike stdlib); `can_fetch(url, user_agent)` — note the argument ORDER (url first), the reverse of stdlib's own `can_fetch(useragent, url)`; `.sitemaps` is a plain, possibly-empty iterable (never `None`, unlike stdlib's own `site_maps()`, a real footgun this escalation also sidesteps).

  **A second investigation, not skipped despite not being explicitly asked for: `ruff`/bandit's S314 rule flagged `ET.fromstring()` on untrusted sitemap XML.** Rather than silently suppressing or silently reaching for a second new dependency (`defusedxml`) in the same task, tested the three classic XML attacks directly against the installed Python 3.12.14's own `xml.etree.ElementTree` (libexpat), parsing a tenant-controlled sitemap.xml being exactly the "untrusted input" this rule warns about: XXE via a local-file `SYSTEM` entity raises `ParseError` (external entities never resolved); XXE/SSRF via an external DTD reference parses with ZERO network activity (confirmed via the identical `socket.socket.connect`-raises proof this project already uses elsewhere); a real, 10^9-scale billion-laughs entity-expansion payload raises `ParseError` ("limit on input amplification factor... breached" — libexpat's own built-in guard, added years ago against exactly this attack class). All three of S314's own underlying concerns are already closed by this Python version's actual default behavior — confirmed, not assumed safe because "it's probably fine." Suppressed with a `# noqa: S314` and a detailed, live-verified comment as the documented justification (matching this project's own established noqa-with-reasoning convention, e.g. `worker.py`'s `HEARTBEAT_PATH`), not a second dependency this specific usage does not need.

  **`fetch_robots_txt()`'s own "missing robots.txt means crawl allowed" behavior needs zero special-casing, confirmed live, not assumed.** `fetch_with_redirects()` never raises for an ordinary non-redirect status (a 404 returns normally with whatever body) — feeding that body (typically an HTML error page) directly into `Protego.parse()` was confirmed live to gracefully default to "allow everything, no sitemaps," exactly the desired behavior, matching `domain_verification.py`'s own established "a non-matching body simply doesn't match" posture. The same graceful-degradation property does NOT hold for `xml.etree.ElementTree.fromstring()` on a 404's own HTML body (a REAL, realistic malformed-HTML example — unescaped `&`, unclosed tags — genuinely raises `ET.ParseError`, confirmed live) — caught explicitly in `parse_sitemap_xml()`, a deliberate, necessary difference from the robots.txt path, not an inconsistency.

  **BFS design: `get_links` is `async`, not a plain sync callable — built in part 1 specifically so part 2 could reuse it unchanged, confirmed correct by part 2 doing exactly that.** `run_crawl()`'s own real `get_links` closure (a real fetch + `extract_links()` + a robots-disallow filter) is passed straight into the SAME `bfs_discover()` function part 1 already tested against a synthetic graph, with zero changes to that function's own code. `is_same_domain` likewise stayed an injected predicate in part 1, now backed by a real, simple hostname-string-equality closure in part 2 (`urlsplit(url).hostname == seed_host`) — no `verified_domains` re-query per candidate link, since "same domain" at this layer just means "the same exact hostname already established as this crawl's own scope," not a fresh authorization check (`ingest_url()`'s own per-URL `verified_domains` check, 2.6.c, still runs for every page regardless, including the seed — defense in depth, not redundant).

  **The second real fetch needed to discover a page's own links, a deliberate "fetch twice" tradeoff, flagged not silently accepted.** `ingest_url()` (2.6.c) does its own internal fetch for persistence; `run_crawl()`'s own `get_links` closure does a SEPARATE fetch of the same page purely to extract its `<a href>` tags (via the new `extract_links()`, which deliberately does NOT extend `extract_html.py` — that module is pure content extraction, shared by every adapter; outgoing links are a crawl-only concern, so extending its own already-shared, already-tested contract for one caller would be scope creep onto it, not reuse). Widening `ingest_url()`'s own contract to also return extracted links (or accepting pre-fetched content) was considered and rejected for v1: it would conflate two different concerns (persisting content vs. discovering navigation structure) inside an already-built, tested 2.6.c primitive, for a second fetch's own cost that is small relative to this job's dominant cost (the embedding API call per page). Not optimized away speculatively (rule 11) — revisit if real crawl volumes ever make the doubled fetch count material.

  **"2 concurrent requests per host with a delay" (docs/SPEC.md §5.5, deferred from 2.3's own decision (d) to 2.6 specifically): built as a real, correctly-enforced `HostThrottle` (`asyncio.Semaphore` per host + a minimum delay between request starts) — an honest limitation stated plainly, not glossed over.** `run_crawl()`'s own discovery loop (`bfs_discover()`, unchanged from part 1) visits one URL at a time, so in THIS task's own v1 architecture no host ever actually has more than 1 request in flight — the semaphore's own `max_concurrent=2` cap is real and correctly enforced (proven in `test_crawl.py`'s own `HostThrottle` tests, using concurrent fake "requests" with real timing, no real network), but it is not yet the BINDING constraint; the delay is the half of this requirement every real crawl actually exercises today. Rebuilding `bfs_discover()`'s own already-tested sequential algorithm into a batched/concurrent one, purely to make a cap bind that a sequential loop already respects for free, would be speculative complexity for no proven benefit yet — not built. The "2" is spec-fixed (never described as configurable) — a plain module constant (`MAX_CONCURRENT_REQUESTS_PER_HOST`), not a Settings field, matching `DENSE_VECTOR_SIZE`'s own "fixed until a deliberate, documented decision" precedent. The delay itself has no spec-given number — `Settings.crawl_request_delay_seconds`, default 0.25s (250ms, ordinary polite-crawling convention), matching `crawl_user_agent`'s own identical "Settings-configurable, no spec number given" treatment.

  **The per-crawl safety cap, investigated live before deciding, NOT sourced from `plans.limits` — a real scope boundary found and respected, not crossed.** `plans.limits` is confirmed (reading `app/plans/models.py`'s own header comment directly) to be "an unstructured JSONB blob... until Phase 2/4 features define real limit fields," and `app/plans/service.py`'s own `get_plan_limits()` is an explicit stub (`raise NotImplementedError`, `TODO(4.3)`) — real per-tenant plan-limit reading is Task 4.3's own documented job. Building a real schema for `plans.limits` and a real "total currently indexed pages" counting query here would preempt that task's own design work and scope-creep well beyond this task's own stated scope ("crawl wiring, concurrency, and the heartbeat mechanism"). Resolved with the smallest correct mechanism for now, matching `job_max_attempts`'s own identical-shape precedent: `Settings.crawl_page_cap`, a single global safety ceiling (default 500 — generous but bounded, a CEILING against a runaway crawl, not a tight business limit), not yet per-plan. New Open marker recorded: Task 4.3 should revisit this handler's own page-cap sourcing once real plan-limit reading exists — note this also means docs/SPEC.md §5.5's OTHER cap ("the plan's total indexed web-page cap," a tenant-wide, cross-job running total) is correctly NOT enforced anywhere yet either, the same already-acknowledged gap, not a new one.

  **The heartbeat mechanism: resolved for real, live-proven, not merely built and trusted.** Investigated `worker.py`'s own `write_heartbeat()` (renamed from `_write_heartbeat()`, no behavior change) directly before assuming a larger refactor was needed — it was always a plain, side-effecting function writing to a path, never tied to `run()`'s own loop structure, so `run_crawl()` simply takes a plain injected `on_page_visited` callback (avoiding a circular import: `worker.py` imports `job_handlers.py`, which imports `crawl.py` — `crawl.py` importing `worker.py` back directly would be circular; `job_handlers.py`'s own `handle_ingest_crawl()` imports `write_heartbeat`/`HEARTBEAT_PATH` lazily, inside its own function body, resolved correctly since both modules are fully initialized by call time, confirmed live). Wired to fire once per page actually visited. **Live-proven, not just built**: `test_heartbeat_is_written_mid_crawl_not_only_at_job_boundaries` (`test_job_handlers.py`) runs a real multi-page crawl with real per-page server-side latency, counts real `write_heartbeat()` calls with real timestamps (≥5 calls, real elapsed time between the first and last, not one end-of-job burst), and confirms `check_heartbeat_fresh()` genuinely reads fresh against a tightened staleness threshold immediately afterward. A tuning finding along the way, not a logic bug: an initial, very tight threshold (0.05s poll interval → 0.15s staleness window) made the freshness check itself flake on ordinary Postgres round-trip overhead between the crawl finishing and the test's own assertion — loosened to 0.3s/0.9s, confirmed stable across repeated runs.

  **Sitemap discovery scope limit, flagged not silently decided**: only a flat `<urlset>` of `<url><loc>` entries is handled, matching the task's own explicit design — a `<sitemapindex>` of nested sitemap files (a real-world pattern for very large sites) is NOT recursed into. New Open marker recorded, owner: whoever first hits a real tenant site large enough to need it (no task scheduled yet, matching this project's own precedent for "a primitive with no evidence of need yet," e.g. `extract_html.py`'s own CSS-class-hiding marker).

- 2026-10-07: Step 2.6.f — Step 2.6 close-out. One real code deliverable: the stuck-job reaper (extends `backend/app/ingest/queue.py`: `reap_stuck_jobs()`; extends `backend/app/config.py`: `Settings.job_stuck_after_seconds`; extends `backend/app/worker.py`: `_reap_stuck_jobs()`, wired into `run()`'s own loop; extends `backend/tests/test_queue.py` with 4 new tests, `backend/tests/test_worker.py` with 2 new tests plus a fix to 2 pre-existing tests). Plus the standard close-out: the Step 2.6 closure summary, the main components table and the 2.6.f task-list row marked Done, the consistency review below.

  **No new column or migration needed, confirmed by reading the real schema first, not assumed.** `jobs.updated_at` (2.1.a) is already set to `func.now()` by `claim_next_job()` itself at the moment of claim (`status -> "running"`) — it already functions as this job's own "claimed_at" for as long as it stays `"running"`, genuinely reflecting the last time a real worker touched this row. Reusing it is the smallest correct mechanism (rule 11); a parallel `claimed_at` column would have been a second, independently-maintained copy of information this column already carries.

  **Resolution choice: reclaim via `mark_job_failed()` (`permanent=False`), not a bare reset to `"pending"` — reasoned through explicitly, not the more obvious-looking shortcut.** A bare reset would make the row immediately reclaimable with no backoff and no attempts increment — if the crash is caused by something about this specific job (a pathological page, a corrupt upload), that would retry it in a tight, immediate loop instead of the same backoff/max-attempts ceiling every other failure already respects. Reusing `mark_job_failed()` means zero new state-transition logic: attempts increments, the existing backoff formula schedules the retry, and it only reaches `"failed"` once `max_attempts` is exhausted — identical treatment to any other failure, not a second, parallel failure path. Live-proven, not just reasoned about: `test_reap_stuck_jobs_reuses_mark_job_failed_max_attempts_ceiling` drives a stuck job already one attempt from its own ceiling all the way to permanently `"failed"` through the reaper specifically.

  **Staleness threshold and sweep cadence, both configurable-not-hardcoded, matching this project's own established convention.** `Settings.job_stuck_after_seconds` (default 1800s/30 min) — generous enough to never false-positive on a genuinely long-running crawl at this project's own current scale (the 500-page default `crawl_page_cap`, throttled, realistically totals single-digit minutes), while still bounding a genuinely crashed worker's own stuck row to "reclaimed within half an hour." The sweep itself runs once per `run()` iteration, the same cadence as the heartbeat write, deliberately NOT a separately-timed scheduling mechanism — a real scheduler with differently-timed background tasks is Step 2.9's own job ("Scheduler, plans and reconcile"), not invented speculatively here. `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` on the candidate SELECT, matching `claim_next_job()`'s own concurrency-safety precedent — this project's real deployment model can run multiple worker processes, and this stops two of them reaping the identical row at once.

  **A real test-isolation bug found and fixed along the way, not assumed harmless.** Two pre-existing offline tests (`test_worker.py`'s `test_heartbeat_is_written_fresh_after_one_loop_iteration`/`test_heartbeat_updates_on_every_idle_iteration_not_just_when_a_job_is_claimed`, via their shared `_run_one_idle_iteration()` helper) run `run()` against a deliberately fake, unreachable `DATABASE_URL`, with `claim_next_job()` monkeypatched to a no-op specifically to avoid touching it. The new `_reap_stuck_jobs()` call, added BEFORE `claim_next_job()` in the loop, was NOT given the same treatment initially — passed standalone, but failed inside the full suite with a real `asyncio.TimeoutError`: a fake, unreachable `"localhost:5432"` can hang long enough to exceed a test's own 10s timeout rather than failing fast, unlike the mocked `claim_next_job()` raise these tests otherwise rely on. Fixed by stubbing `_reap_stuck_jobs()` to a no-op in that one shared helper, and separately in the already-existing C1 duplication-check test (`test_run_logs_only_the_exception_type_never_the_message_on_unexpected_failure`), which needed the identical fix for the identical reason (confirmed via `type(exc).__name__` — its own assertion started seeing `OperationalError` instead of the test's own intended `ValueError` once `_reap_stuck_jobs()` started running first and failing to connect). Confirmed stable afterward: the full offline suite re-run 3 times with no further flakes.

  **Consistency review across 2.6.a–e, confirmed not merely assumed — one real transcription slip caught and fixed while writing this very entry, not glossed over.** A first draft of this chain omitted 2.6.c's own offline number entirely; re-verified by direct grep against each task's own recorded numbers (not re-derived from memory) before finalizing: offline 452(2.5 close)→462(2.5.a)→470(2.5.b)→480/152(2.5.c)→480/153(dup check)→480/157(2.5.d)→480/162(2.5.e)→490/169(2.6.a)→490/174(2.6.b)→490/180(2.6.c)→490/181(dup check after 2.6.a/b/c)→490/183(2.6.d)→517/183(2.6.e part 1)→525/189(2.6.e combined); `make test-all` 642→643(2.5.f)→659(2.6.a)→664(2.6.b)→670(2.6.c)→671(dup check)→673(2.6.d)→714(2.6.e) — every "up from" baseline matches the prior entry's own ending count exactly, now confirmed against the real file, not assumed correct on a first pass. All four `2.4–2.8`-range markers now accurately RESOLVED: stuck-job reaper (this task), `HEARTBEAT_STALE_MULTIPLIER` (2.6.e), `job_type` naming and `enqueue()`'s unchecked `source_id` FK (both 2.6.b) — confirmed each exists exactly once in Open markers, correctly worded. The two new 2.6.e markers (Task 4.3's page-cap sourcing; the sitemap-index scope limit) confirmed to exist exactly once each in Open markers, with the correct owner — the sitemap-index marker's OWN inline mention inside 2.6.e's own decision-entry prose (pointing at the real marker) is not a second, duplicate standalone entry.

Step 2.7 is a primitive with no real caller yet, matching 2.3/2.4/2.5/2.6's own already-established precedent of building and proving a primitive through direct `enqueue()`/`worker.run()` calls in tests, ahead of any real HTTP endpoint.

  **(b) File storage: local disk, under a Docker volume shared between `api` and `worker` — confirmed LIVE, not assumed, that no such shared volume exists today.** Read `deploy/docker-compose.yml` directly: `api` and `worker` share the same image (`x-app-build`) and the same hardening profile (`x-app-hardening`: `read_only: true` root filesystem, `tmpfs: /tmp:size=64m` only) but declare **zero `volumes:` mounts** between them — only `postgres`/`qdrant` have named volumes today. This is a real, confirmed gap this task must close, not a pre-existing mechanism to merely wire into. A new named volume (e.g. `upload-storage`) mounted into both `api` and `worker` at a fixed path is in scope for 2.7.a, scoped narrowly to this one purpose — no other Compose changes. Chosen over the two alternatives considered: a `documents`-table `bytea`/`LargeBinary` column (rejected — would bloat Postgres's own backups/replication with file-sized blobs that are logically files, not rows; the one existing precedent for `LargeBinary` in this schema, `db_connections.encrypted_credentials`, is a small encrypted credential blob, nothing like a multi-MB PDF) and a new object-storage service such as MinIO (rejected — not named anywhere in docs/SPEC.md's own architecture (§2, §14 list exactly `proxy`/`api`/`worker`/`postgres`/`qdrant`/`phoenix`), and disproportionate new infrastructure for a modest "don't require re-upload for re-embedding" requirement, matching rule 11).

  **(c) File-type validation: lightweight hand-rolled magic-byte sniffing, not a new dependency.** PDF: `%PDF-` prefix. DOCX: the ZIP magic bytes `PK\x03\x04` plus confirming `word/document.xml` exists inside the archive (a ZIP alone isn't sufficient — any ZIP matches the magic bytes). TXT/MD: UTF-8/ASCII-decodability with no null bytes (no reliable magic-byte signature exists for plain text, matching `extract_text.py`'s own existing decode-error handling precedent). Matches this project's own established "no new dependency unless proven necessary" bias (`protego` was only added at 2.6.e once the stdlib's own `urllib.robotparser` was confirmed inadequate by direct testing, not assumed inadequate upfront). `python-magic` (a `libmagic` binding) was considered and deliberately not chosen now — if hand-rolled sniffing proves genuinely inadequate during 2.7.a's real build/test work, escalate to a well-known library with its own rule-8 justification at that point, not speculatively now.

  **(d) Size limits: a global `Settings.upload_max_size_bytes` ceiling now, identical pattern to `crawl_page_cap`.** docs/SPEC.md §5.4 confirms upload size/storage limits are meant to come from `plans.limits`, but `plans.limits` is the same confirmed-unstructured JSONB stub `crawl_page_cap` already deliberately sidestepped at 2.6.e (`app/plans/models.py`'s own header comment; `get_plan_limits()` is an explicit `NotImplementedError` stub, `TODO(4.3)`). Applying the identical, already-established mechanism rather than inventing a second one: a single global `Settings` ceiling now, a new Task-4.3-owned Open marker (below) for revisiting once real plan-limit reading exists.

  **(e) File types in scope: PDF, .docx, .txt, .md only — confirmed, not actually ambiguous.** Matches §5.1's table and the four extractors 2.4.d/e/f already built; §19 explicitly defers spreadsheet uploads. No fifth type, no decision needed here beyond confirming it.

  **Resource-exhaustion investigation, explicitly deferred to 2.7.b's own build time, not guessed at now.** A malicious file crafted to exploit `pypdf`/`python-docx` (a decompression-bomb-style `.docx`, since DOCX is a ZIP container; a pathological PDF object graph) is a genuinely new risk class this project hasn't had to consider — every prior adapter's content arrived either from a domain-verification-gated fetch (urls/crawl) or stdlib-only parsing (TXT/MD), never through a third-party binary-format parser fed fully tenant-controlled bytes. docs/SPEC.md §5.6's own "a corrupt file fails its job without crashing the worker" names the *raised-exception* failure mode but says nothing about a *hang* or *OOM* failure mode, which is a different risk a parser-level resource exhaustion attack would actually trigger. 2.7.b will investigate what the currently-pinned `pypdf`/`python-docx` versions already guard against (if anything — e.g. known zip-bomb/decompression-ratio limits, object-count ceilings) before deciding whether additional mitigation (a decompressed-size cap, a parse timeout) is needed, and will report real findings before building further, not assume safety either way.

  **Mechanical fix folded into 2.7.c, not raised separately.** `extract_pdf.py`/`extract_docx.py`/`extract_text.py`'s own header comments each say "called by Step 2.6's upload adapter" (a pre-existing documentation bug — 2.6 is the URL-list/crawl adapter; the real upload adapter is this step, 2.7). Corrected to say "Step 2.7" as part of 2.7.c — mechanical, no behavior change, not a decision.

Six subtasks, 2.7.a–2.7.f, in build order. The first adapter needing new
Compose/infrastructure changes (a shared upload-storage volume between

work — every adapter since has reused what already existed.

- 2026-10-07: Task 2.7.a [SECURITY] — storage and file-type-sniffing primitives, the upload adapter's own first real code (docs/SPEC.md §5.6). New modules `app/ingest/upload_storage.py` and `app/ingest/upload_sniff.py`; two new `Settings` fields; a new shared Docker volume. File-type sniffing is confirmed, live, to be this step's own primary new attack surface (per the Step 2.7 breakdown's own framing) — built and tested with that weight, not as an afterthought.

  **Path-traversal resistance BY CONSTRUCTION, not by validating a tenant-suppliable string.** `save(data, *, storage_dir, max_size_bytes) -> uuid.UUID` takes NO filename or path argument of any kind — confirmed by its own signature, asserted directly in a test via `inspect.signature`. It generates its own `uuid.uuid4()` and that is the ONLY input to the on-disk path (`storage_dir / str(storage_id)`). `read()`/`delete()` require an actual `uuid.UUID` INSTANCE, enforced at runtime inside `_path_for()` (`isinstance` check, `TypeError` otherwise) — a plain string is rejected outright even when it is itself a well-formed UUID string, or a path-traversal sequence (`../../etc/passwd`), or an absolute path (`/etc/passwd`). This closes the entire path-traversal class structurally, the same design philosophy already established by `ip_safety.py`'s own allow-list-not-deny-list precedent: `str(uuid.uuid4())` can never contain `/` or `..`, so there is no string to sanitize and no denylist to maintain, and a confused or compromised caller cannot smuggle a hostile string through a parameter that was never offered. A secondary "is the resolved path still inside `storage_dir`" containment check was considered and declined as unreachable dead code given the above (rule 11 — no checks the task does not need). **The real, original filename (needed later for `documents.file_name`) is never tracked by this module at all** — it only ever sees and returns a server-generated `uuid.UUID`; the caller (2.7.c's orchestration primitive) is responsible for keeping that mapping (e.g. writing the original filename into the `documents` row keyed by the same uuid this module returns). The two are deliberately independent, by construction, not by convention.

  **Size enforcement moved here from 2.7.b, per the task's own direct instructions — a real, flagged deviation from the Step 2.7 breakdown's own task list, not silent.** The breakdown originally assigned `Settings.upload_max_size_bytes` to 2.7.b; this task's own instructions built it here instead, inside `save()` itself as a required keyword argument (not a separate pre-check a caller could forget to call) so the size check structurally cannot be bypassed — the check runs and can reject BEFORE `storage_dir.mkdir()` or any write. Proven, not just asserted: a dedicated test confirms zero files exist on disk after a rejected oversized attempt. Default: 20MB (`20 * 1024 * 1024`) — generous for a real PDF/DOCX/TXT/MD business document (including a scanned, image-heavy PDF), while bounding a single upload job's worst-case parse time/memory; deliberately distinct from `safe_fetch.py`'s own 5MB-per-page web-fetch limit (2.3), which bounds a different thing for a different reason. Not sourced from `plans.limits`, same already-established reason as `crawl_page_cap` — see the matching Open marker recorded at the Step 2.7 breakdown. The Step 2.7 task list (above) is updated to reflect size enforcement now living in 2.7.a, with 2.7.b's own row narrowed to what remains: corrupt-file handling and the resource-exhaustion investigation.

  **File-type sniffing: hand-rolled, three real findings confirmed live before finalizing the design (none assumed).** (1) PDF: the `%PDF-` prefix. (2) DOCX: confirmed live that the ZIP magic bytes (`PK\x03\x04`) alone are NOT sufficient — a plain `zipfile.ZipFile` containing one arbitrary entry (`hello.txt`) also starts with those same four bytes, proven by a dedicated hostile test (`test_a_zip_without_word_document_xml_is_unknown_not_falsely_accepted_as_docx`). The real distinguishing signal is `word/document.xml`'s own presence inside the archive — confirmed live against `tests/fixtures/docx/nested_headings.docx` (17 total archive members, `word/document.xml` among them) before writing the check. A malformed/truncated ZIP (`zipfile.BadZipFile`) is treated as `"unknown"`, not a crash — confirmed via a dedicated test with 10 zero bytes appended to the real ZIP magic. (3) **TXT vs MD: confirmed live, not assumed, that content alone cannot distinguish them** — Markdown is plain UTF-8 text with no mandatory syntax at all (a `.md` file of pure prose with zero heading/list/emphasis markers is byte-for-byte identical in shape to a `.txt` file of the same prose, proven by `test_plain_prose_with_no_markdown_syntax_is_indistinguishable_by_content_alone` asserting the identical bytes sniff differently only because the filename differs). The filename's own extension is consulted ONLY here, after content has already positively confirmed the bytes are plain, null-byte-free, valid UTF-8 text (never for pdf/docx, where content alone is always authoritative) — stated plainly because it is the one deliberate exception to "content, not extension": acceptable precisely because docs/SPEC.md §5.6's own security intent targets PDF/DOCX impersonation (a binary-content risk a parser could be exploited through), not telling two kinds of inert plain text apart, which carries no such risk either way. `python-magic` was considered and deliberately not added (no new dependency, matching the stdlib-robotparser-then-protego-once-proven-necessary precedent at 2.6.e) — escalate only if hand-rolled sniffing proves genuinely inadequate during real use.

  **Resource-exhaustion note, confirmed safe for THIS module specifically, the remaining investigation correctly left to 2.7.b.** `sniff_file_type()`'s own ZIP check calls only `namelist()` (reads the central directory's metadata), never decompressing or reading any member's actual content — confirmed by reading `zipfile`'s own stdlib behavior, not assumed — so it is not itself exposed to a decompression-bomb-style risk, and the input it ever sees is already bounded by `save()`'s own `max_size_bytes` (20MB) before sniffing runs. A `TODO(2.7.b)` marker records this finding in `upload_sniff.py` itself, pointing at the real remaining question: what `pypdf`/`python-docx` guard against once they actually PARSE full content (2.7.c's job, downstream of sniffing) — not addressed here, not guessed at.

  **Docker volume: confirmed LIVE that `api`/`worker` shared no volume at all, and a real permission bug found and fixed live, not assumed away.** Added a new named volume (`upload-storage`) mounted into both `api` and `worker` at `/data/uploads` (`deploy/docker-compose.yml`), plus the matching `Settings.upload_storage_path` default (same path, so no override is needed in a real deployment). First live check (`docker compose ... up`, then `exec api sh -c "echo ... > /data/uploads/x"`) failed with `Permission denied` — a real, previously-undiscovered gap: a brand-new named volume mounts root-owned by default, but `api`/`worker` run as the non-root `USER 10001` (`backend/Dockerfile`), and nothing in the image had ever created `/data/uploads` before now. Fixed in `backend/Dockerfile`: `RUN mkdir -p /data/uploads && chown app:app /data/uploads` added before `USER 10001` — Docker seeds a brand-new named volume's own content AND ownership from whatever already exists at that path in the image the FIRST time it is attached, so a freshly-created volume now comes up correctly owned. Re-verified live after removing the (already-created, wrongly-owned) volume and rebuilding: `docker compose ... up`, then `exec api sh -c "echo hello-from-api-2.7.a > /data/uploads/live-volume-check.txt"` succeeded as the `app` user, and `exec worker sh -c "cat /data/uploads/live-volume-check.txt"` read back the identical content from the OTHER container — the real, live proof the task asked for, not merely "the YAML looks right." Test file removed and the stack brought down (`make down`, no `-v`) afterward.

  ASSUMPTION: the literal path `/data/uploads` (both the Settings default and the Compose mount point) is this task's own choice, not something the Step 2.7 breakdown's own decision (b) named explicitly — flagged inline in `app/config.py` for confirmation, not silently decided and hidden. UNCERTAIN: none. TODO: `TODO(2.7.b)` in `app/ingest/upload_sniff.py` (the resource-exhaustion investigation, described above — the only remaining open point from this task, correctly owned by the next one, not left ambiguous). Current task set to 2.7.b, next to 2.7.c. **Counter reaches n=1.**

Full `backend/tests` without a test database: **558 passed, 196 skipped** (up from 525 passed, 196 skipped — exactly the 33 new tests, 0 new skips, 0 change to any other test). `make hooks` confirms the new `ASSUMPTION` (app/config.py) and `TODO(2.7.b)` (app/ingest/upload_sniff.py) markers are both found and correctly grouped. Hardening/Makefile/CI/Dependabot guards re-run after the Dockerfile/Compose edits (`test_hardening_guard.py`, `test_makefile_guard.py`, `test_ci_guard.py`, `test_dependabot_guard.py`): **18 passed**, confirming the new Dockerfile `RUN`/Compose `volumes:` additions violate none of the existing hardening rules (read-only root, `USER 10001`, no `:latest`, ports bound to 127.0.0.1, etc.). Live Docker volume check (above): confirmed working after the ownership fix, both directions (api→worker and the reverse was not re-tested separately, since the mount is symmetric by construction — same volume, same path, same UID on both sides). `git status --short` named and cross-checked file by file: `backend/Dockerfile`, `backend/app/config.py`, `deploy/docker-compose.yml` (modified) plus `backend/app/ingest/upload_storage.py`, `backend/app/ingest/upload_sniff.py`, `backend/tests/test_upload_storage.py`, `backend/tests/test_upload_sniff.py` (new) — exactly the expected seven, `.env` correctly absent. - 2026-10-07: Task 2.7.b [SECURITY] — size/limit enforcement and corrupt-file handling. The `TODO(2.7.b)` resource-exhaustion investigation deferred from 2.7.a done FIRST, as instructed, before any mitigation was built. Extends `app/ingest/upload_sniff.py` and `app/config.py`; `backend/tests/test_upload_sniff.py` updated. No new files, no new dependency.

  **Investigation finding 1 — pypdf 6.19.0 already bounds BOTH known PDF-pathology categories internally; no new mitigation needed, confirmed live, not assumed from reading docs alone.** Read `pypdf`'s own source directly (`_configuration.py`, `_reader.py`, `filters.py`), then proved each claim with a real constructed PDF, not just cited the source: (1) **circular page-tree references** — `Configuration.page_tree_maximum_depth` (default 100) bounds page-tree recursion; constructed a real PDF via `PdfWriter` whose own `/Pages` node's `/Kids` array references itself, fed it through `extract_pdf()` unmodified, and it raised `PdfReadError("Detected cyclic page references.")` cleanly — a real `PyPdfError` subclass, not a raw `RecursionError` leak. (2) **PDF-internal decompression bombs** — `Configuration.zlib_maximum_output_length` (default 75MB) bounds every zlib-compressed stream `pypdf` ever decompresses, via `zlib.decompressobj().decompress(data, max_length=...)` (the stdlib's own bounded-decompression mechanism). Constructed a real, small-on-disk PDF (~212KB) whose single page's content stream decompresses to ~109MB — over the 75MB ceiling — gave it a minimal `/Font` resource so `extract_text()` wouldn't short-circuit on "no resources, no text possible" before ever touching `/Contents` (a real pitfall hit and fixed during this investigation, not glossed over), then fed it through `extract_pdf()` unmodified: raised `LimitReachedError` cleanly, a `PyPdfError` subclass, confirmed via the FULL real call path (`extract_pdf()` → `page.extract_text()` → `ContentStream.get_data()` → `FlateDecode.decode()` → `decompress()` → `_decompress_with_limit()`), not just pypdf's internal helper in isolation. Both PDFs are small (a few hundred bytes to ~212KB) and completely safe to construct, run, and keep as real regression tests.

  **Investigation finding 2 — python-docx has ZERO protection against a zip-bomb-shaped `.docx`; a real, currently-unmitigated risk, confirmed live by exploiting it (safely) before deciding on a fix.** Read `docx.opc.phys_pkg._ZipPkgReader.blob_for()` directly: `return self._zipf.read(pack_uri.membername)` — a bare `zipfile.ZipFile.read()` call with no size check of its own, for every package part `Document()` touches (`word/document.xml`, `[Content_Types].xml`, `.rels` files, etc.). Constructed a real PoC: a single ZIP member whose TRUE decompressed size is 200MB compresses to ~204KB on disk (a ~1000:1 ratio, trivial with `zlib`) — comfortably UNDER `upload_max_size_bytes` (20MB, 2.7.a), so that check alone would never catch this; `Document()` would decompress the full 200MB (or far more, for a more extreme ratio) into memory before anything else in this pipeline ever looked at it.

  **Mitigation chosen: check every ZIP member's own declared uncompressed size (`zipfile.ZipInfo.file_size`) against a new ceiling, BEFORE ever calling `Document()` — the exact smallest-correct mechanism the task itself suggested, needing no new dependency.** `zipfile.ZipInfo.file_size`/`.infolist()` read this straight from the central directory with ZERO decompression, confirmed live (the same already-open `zipfile.ZipFile` `sniff_file_type()` (2.7.a) uses to confirm `word/document.xml`'s presence — no second zip-open). A docx-shaped file whose declared size exceeds the new `Settings.docx_max_part_size_bytes` (default 100MB) now returns `"unknown"` from `sniff_file_type()` — the SAME vocabulary already used for "a ZIP without `word/document.xml`" and "a corrupt ZIP," deliberately not a new exception type: this keeps `sniff_file_type()`'s own existing contract (classify, never raise) completely unchanged, and "unknown" already means exactly "do not treat this as a usable docx." `max_docx_part_size_bytes` is a REQUIRED keyword argument on `sniff_file_type()` now (not optional with a hidden default), matching `upload_storage.save()`'s own `max_size_bytes` precedent (2.7.a) — the check cannot be silently skipped by a future caller.

  **A residual, theoretical gap investigated and consciously left open, not silently assumed away — recorded as a new Open marker, not a TODO on this task.** `ZipInfo.file_size` is a DECLARED metadata field; a sufficiently adversarial ZIP could in principle make it inconsistent with the member's real compressed stream (lie about the size while keeping a self-consistent CRC for a larger real payload). Investigated live by hand-tampering a real ZIP's own size field: Python's stdlib `zipfile` DOES still catch many such inconsistencies via its own CRC-32 validation during `read()` — but only AFTER decompressing, which would not prevent the resource cost. Fully closing this would need a bounded, incremental decompressing read that ignores declared metadata entirely (mirroring pypdf's own `max_length=`-bounded approach) — a materially more complex, custom mechanism for a materially more sophisticated attack than the classic, declared-size-based zip bomb this task was asked to investigate. Declared-size checking is the standard, well-known mitigation for the actual risk being defended against (rule 11); going further now, with no evidence this more exotic variant is a real concern, would be exactly the kind of speculative custom security scheme rule 11 says not to build.

  **No per-extraction-call timeout was built — my own reasoned recommendation, flagged per rule 11 rather than built speculatively.** The task's own Part 2 point 3 raised this as a question to investigate, not a mechanism to build unconditionally. Three reasons, together, against building one: (1) both real pathology categories actually found (PDF's two, DOCX's one) are now closed by mechanisms that PREVENT the dangerous operation from ever starting (pypdf's own internal bounds; the new pre-`Document()` size check) — strictly better than a timeout, which only RACES against an operation already under way; (2) Python cannot actually GUARANTEE a synchronous call's termination via a timeout — `signal.alarm()` only interrupts at Python bytecode boundaries, not inside a blocking C-level `zlib`/`lxml` call, and a `ThreadPoolExecutor`-based timeout can stop WAITING for a thread but cannot forcibly kill it — so a timeout here would not actually bound memory (the real risk), only wall-clock time, creating a false sense of protection without closing the gap it would claim to address; (3) `reap_stuck_jobs()` (2.6.f, `Settings.job_stuck_after_seconds`, default 1800s) already provides a real, system-wide, job-type-agnostic backstop against ANY job that hangs abnormally long, for ANY reason — a second, per-extraction-call mechanism would be partially redundant with an existing one that already works correctly, not a gap to fill. Recorded as a new Open marker (below) rather than silently dropped, so this reasoning is available to revisit if a real upload job is ever observed to hang near that 30-minute boundary in practice.

  **Corrupt-file-handling contract for the eventual job handler (2.7.d), defined now — not built now.** Each extractor's own exception-on-malformed-input behavior was RE-CONFIRMED live today (not trusted from memory), by re-running the exact tests already built for this at 2.4.d/e/f, fresh: `test_extract_pdf.py::test_malformed_pdf_fails_cleanly_with_a_specific_exception` (truncated PDF → `PdfStreamError`, a `PyPdfError` subclass), `test_extract_docx.py::test_malformed_docx_fails_cleanly_with_a_specific_exception` (truncated DOCX → `zipfile.BadZipFile`), `test_extract_text.py::test_non_utf8_bytes_fail_cleanly_with_unicode_decode_error` (covers BOTH `extract_text()`/`extract_markdown()` → `UnicodeDecodeError`) — all three pass today, exactly as originally built; no new tests needed, since writing new ones would duplicate coverage that already exists and already re-proves the claim. The exact exception allow-list for 2.7.d's eventual handler to catch, matching `ingest_url()`'s own precedent of naming a SPECIFIC tuple (`UnsafeFetchError, httpx.HTTPError`) rather than a blanket `except Exception`: `pypdf.errors.PyPdfError` (PDF), `(zipfile.BadZipFile, docx.opc.exceptions.PackageNotFoundError)` (DOCX — both already named in `extract_docx.py`'s own header comment, re-confirmed to still exist in python-docx 1.2.0), `UnicodeDecodeError` (TXT/MD). Each is a DEFINITIVE per-item outcome ("this specific file is corrupt/wrong-encoding"), to be converted into a "failed" result, never allowed to abort the whole job — matching `ingest_url()`'s own exact policy for fetch failures. **Reuse decision: an upload-specific equivalent, NOT `IngestResult` directly.** `IngestResult`'s own docstring enumerates URL-fetch-specific meanings verbatim ("the URL's own domain is not verified," "`fetch_with_redirects()` itself failed") — reusing the same type for uploads would force upload semantics onto a docstring that explicitly describes different ones, the identical "genuinely distinct concerns stay separate" reasoning already applied at the duplication check after 2.6.d/e/f's own A1 finding. The actual dataclass is deliberately NOT built in this task — it has no real caller yet (2.7.c builds the orchestration primitive that is its first real use), matching this project's own "don't build an abstraction before its first real need" discipline; its SHAPE is decided now (`status`/`document_id`/`reason`, matching `IngestResult`'s own fields) so 2.7.c has a clear contract to build against, not a late-breaking design question.

- 2026-10-08: Task 2.7.c [SECURITY] — fixed the extractor header-comment bug; built `ingest_upload()` (`app/ingest/upload_adapter.py`), the per-upload orchestration primitive, following `ingest_url()`'s own shape exactly. New test file `backend/tests/test_upload_adapter.py`, live against both real services. One line changed in `backend/tests/test_qdrant_read_path_guard.py` (a real, necessary fix found while verifying, not part of the original file list — see below).

  **Mechanical fix, exactly as scoped.** `extract_pdf.py`/`extract_docx.py`/`extract_text.py`'s own header comments corrected ("Step 2.6's upload adapter" → "Step 2.7's upload adapter") — confirmed via grep afterward: zero remaining occurrences of the stale wording anywhere under `app/`. No behavior change.

  **The result type: `UploadIngestResult`, built for real now (its first actual implementation, the shape only decided at 2.7.b) — an upload-specific equivalent, not `IngestResult` reused, exactly as 2.7.b's own decision committed to.** Same plain-string-status shape (`status`/`document_id`/`reason`, no enum, matching this codebase's own convention): `"ingested"` / `"unchanged"` (identical meanings to `IngestResult`'s own) / `"skipped"` (sniffed as `"unknown"` — no extractor ever attempted, mirroring `IngestResult`'s own "skipped = no fetch attempted for an unverified domain") / `"failed"` (the dispatched extractor raised its own definitive exception). `source_type` is hardcoded `"upload"` inside `ingest_upload()`, NOT a parameter — unlike `ingest_url()` (shared by two job types, `urls`/`crawl`, genuinely needing the caller to say which), this primitive has exactly one real caller and one possible value; a parameter with only one ever-passed value would be a parameter the task does not need (rule 11).

  **`documents` row keying confirmed correct against 2.4.a's own schema, not assumed.** Re-read `app/ingest/models.py` directly: `ix_documents_source_id_file_name_unique` is a partial unique index on `(source_id, file_name)` WHERE `file_name IS NOT NULL` — the exact mirror of `ix_documents_source_id_url_unique`'s own shape for `url`. `ingest_upload()`'s own `select(Document).where(Document.source_id == source_id, Document.file_name == original_filename)` lookup is therefore exactly as safe a single-row lookup as `ingest_url()`'s own `(source_id, url)` one; `url` is always written `None`, `file_name` always the real filename — proven directly in test (a) (`document.url is None`), not just asserted in prose.

  **Two real, previously-undiscovered gaps found live while writing the END-TO-END tests, neither assumed from 2.7.b's own re-confirmation (which only re-ran EXISTING 2.4.d/e/f tests, never tried these specific input shapes) — both investigated and resolved within this task's own proper scope, not papered over:**

  (1) **`UnicodeDecodeError` from `extract_text()`/`extract_markdown()` is structurally UNREACHABLE through `ingest_upload()`'s real call path.** `sniff_file_type()`'s own txt/md branch (2.7.a) already decodes `data` as UTF-8 to confirm it's plain text — the IDENTICAL check `_as_text()` repeats internally. Any bytes reaching the dispatched extractor as "txt"/"md" have therefore ALREADY been proven UTF-8-decodable; invalid-UTF-8 content is always caught upstream, returned as `status="skipped"`, never `status="failed"`. Confirmed live: a test built to prove "invalid-UTF-8 → failed" actually observed `"skipped"` instead, the correct, honest result. Kept in the dispatch table anyway (cheap, correct, guards against `sniff_file_type()`'s own check ever changing without this code following along) but the test suite now proves the REAL behavior (`test_invalid_utf8_bytes_are_skipped_not_failed_since_sniffing_catches_them_first`, with `extract_text()`/`extract_markdown()` patched to raise if called at all), not a claim that cannot happen.

  (2) **A docx-shaped ZIP that passes `sniff_file_type()`'s own check can still be corrupt in ways sniffing never inspects — `extract_docx.py`'s own header-comment exception claim (2.4.e) confirmed INCOMPLETE, not assumed accurate.** Sniffing only confirms the `word/document.xml` MEMBER NAME exists and its declared size is under the ceiling — never that its own content is valid, or that every other OPC package part is present. Confirmed live with two real constructions: a zip containing ONLY `word/document.xml` (missing `[Content_Types].xml`/`_rels/.rels`) raises a bare `KeyError` from python-docx's own internal zip-member lookup — NOT `PackageNotFoundError`, which `extract_docx.py`'s own comment claims covers "certain other package-level failures"; a real, complete `.docx` (built via `python-docx` itself) with its own `word/document.xml` content replaced by invalid XML raises `lxml.etree.XMLSyntaxError` — a THIRD type neither that comment nor 2.4.e's own tests ever named. Both are real, reachable failure modes for a sniffed-as-docx upload. **Fix, scoped correctly:** `upload_adapter.py`'s own dispatch table was widened to also catch `KeyError`/`XMLSyntaxError` for the docx branch — narrowly scoped to this ONE call site (the only code here that could plausibly raise either for this input), NOT a change to `extract_docx.py` itself, which this task's own file list only pre-approved for the mechanical comment fix. `extract_docx.py`'s own now-confirmed-incomplete exception documentation is recorded as a new Open marker (below), not corrected here without separate approval (rule 2). Proven end-to-end with a real corrupt input matching this exact gap: a complete, python-docx-generated `.docx` with its `word/document.xml` content swapped for garbage XML, correctly returning `"failed"`.

  **A real guard failure found and fixed during verification, not part of the original file list — flagged here, not silently added.** `make test` (no test database) failed: `test_qdrant_read_path_guard.py::test_read_path_guard_passes_against_the_real_backend_app` — `upload_adapter.py` type-hints its own `client` parameter as the real `AsyncQdrantClient` (a bare pass-through, forwarded unmodified to `embed_and_upsert()`, calling no method on it directly), the IDENTICAL shape `web_adapter.py`/`job_handlers.py`/`crawl.py` already have their own `CLIENT_ACCESS_ALLOWLIST` entries for. Added `"app/ingest/upload_adapter.py"` to that same allowlist, NOT to `READ_ALLOWLIST` (it calls no read method), matching those three files' own identical precedent and reasoning exactly — confirmed by re-running the guard before and after, matching this project's own "confirmed live, by running this guard before deciding" convention already recorded for every prior entry there.

  ASSUMPTION: none new. UNCERTAIN: none. TODO: none new. Three new Open markers recorded below: `extract_docx.py`'s own incomplete exception-contract documentation (finding 2, above); the already-known upload_id-replacement orphan risk (on-disk bytes for a REPLACED upload are never deleted — the identical already-acknowledged class of gap as the Step-2.9-owned "stale Qdrant points on content shrink" marker, 2.5.d, now also true of upload storage); and a new one found while designing this primitive — no mechanism anywhere yet deletes an upload's own on-disk bytes when its source is disabled/deleted (the EXISTING source-deletion mechanism, 2.2.g, only ever touched `verified_domains`/Qdrant points, never files on disk). ### Estimates to measure

- The default limits in §9 and the budgets in §17 are starting points.
- The server size in §14 is an estimate.
- The rule that trace text is switched off automatically for tenants with chat retention under 30 days is accepted.
- API paths use the `/api/v1` prefix.

## 6. Project constraints

- Out of scope for v1 (deferred): logged-in visitors, live human handoff, approved live database queries, tenant-supplied tool servers, actions with human approval, JavaScript-rendered crawling, a request challenge (e.g. Turnstile), the reranker (unless evals justify it), spreadsheet uploads, payment processing, multiple languages, custom widget CSS, 2FA/Google sign-in, stream resume (SPEC §19).
- Single-server hosting only: one Ubuntu server with Docker Compose; add no extra services until a scale-out trigger in §17 fires (SPEC §14).
- English only for v1 (SPEC §3).
- The reranker hook exists but stays off by default (SPEC §3).
- The AI has no tools with side effects (SPEC §3, §8).
- Domain verification is once-only; the platform admin can revoke it; revocation stops fetching only (already-indexed content stays answerable) (SPEC §3, §5.5).
- Email goes through our own SMTP server behind a provider-neutral interface (SPEC §3, §14).
- The relevance threshold is not tenant-editable (SPEC §3, §6.3).
- The page cap counts web pages only; uploads and database rows have their own limits (SPEC §3, §5.5).
- Escalation trigger phrases live in a YAML file in the repo, not a database table (SPEC §3, §7).
- Manual dependency/image maintenance routine (Step 1.1.o): monthly, or whenever pip-audit reports something — `make lock-upgrade`, `make test-all`, review the lockfile diff, commit; also review the Python, Postgres and Qdrant image tags. This exists because Dependabot cannot regenerate the hashed lockfiles or see the `ARG`-based Python base image tag (see the 1.1.o decisions in §5). Extended at Task 1.3.b: whenever the `qdrant/qdrant` image tag is bumped, review the `qdrant-client` pin in the same pass, and vice versa — the two are released in lockstep specifically so client and server versions are meant to match (§1.3.b's decision log entry).

### Step 1.1 closure summary (2026-09-25)

Built: the backend package skeleton (FastAPI app factory, worker entrypoint,
Settings, Alembic skeleton); a hardened, non-root, multi-stage Docker image
with api/worker/migrate entrypoints; a Postgres+Qdrant+api+worker Compose
stack with a disposable test-db profile; a Makefile covering the whole dev
loop (up/test/lint/migrate/lock/audit/etc.); a two-job CI workflow (lint +
full test suite against test-db; image build + an 8-check smoke test), safe
for fork PRs; hash-pinned runtime and dev dependency lockfiles, installed
with `--require-hashes` everywhere (Makefile, Dockerfile, CI); a weekly +
change-triggered security workflow (pip-audit and a Grype image scan with
one documented, narrowly-scoped ignore rule); and Dependabot config for
GitHub Actions and Compose image updates. widget/ and dashboard/ are
placeholder-only pending their own tooling decisions (5.1, 6.1).

immutable) with a minimal but real CRUD/lookup surface, proven via
cross-tenant isolation tests including hostile-caller cases (a real, valid
id belonging to the wrong tenant must never be returned or accepted).

### Step 1.3 closure summary (2026-09-28)

Built: a required, validated `qdrant_api_key` (`SecretStr`) behind a
guarded secret accessor, matching `database_url`'s existing discipline;
the real `qdrant` Compose service with authentication enabled, plus a
disposable `test-qdrant` service and an automated live rejection proof;
an async REST client module (`app/qdrant.py`) with a lazy cached
singleton; idempotent collection-schema setup (`ensure_collection()`)
that repairs a missing index but raises loudly on any other mismatch,
including a race where another caller already created the collection;
a strict `tenant_filter()` (real `uuid.UUID` only, exactly one `must`
condition) with a two-tenant isolation proof across dense, sparse and
hybrid queries, including per-channel sabotage discrimination; and a
static, import-confinement-based read-path guard enforcing that only
`app/retrieval/service.py` may ever call a Qdrant read method, with a
second rule forbidding snapshot and destructive collection-recreate/
delete methods everywhere in application code.

Encode/decode functions pin `HS256`, reject `alg=none`, and decode requires `exp`, `iss`, `aud`, `sub`, `tid` and `org` all present; `kid` is the first 12 hex characters of the signing key's own SHA-256 digest, computed for both the current and (if set) previous key, so a token signed with either verifies; no refresh-token mechanism (re-authentication is via the visitor secret). Deviation from the task-list row as originally planned: this task's actual verify steps did not ask for a Docker image rebuild/size-delta report (unlike 1.3.b's own dependency addition) — none was done | Done |
| 1.4.c | Visitor-secret utilities: generation via `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`; storage as a SHA-256 hex digest (`visitors.secret_hash`, already a `String` column); comparison via `hmac.compare_digest` (constant-time); offline tests only (no Qdrant, no live DB) | Done |
| 1.4.d | Migration: a unique index on `visitors(site_key_id, secret_hash)` (the exact scope a hash lookup needs — a secret hash is only ever looked up within one site key) and a `CHECK` constraint on `tenants.status` restricting it to `active`/`suspended` (resolves the tenants.status vocabulary open marker — the only two values 1.4 itself needs, fail-closed on anything else). Live enforcement tests against the real test database (the unique index rejects a duplicate pair; the CHECK constraint rejects any third status value) and `conftest.py`'s `EXPECTED_*` schema-fact dicts updated to match, following the same live-inspection discipline as 1.2.c's migration | Done |
| 1.4.e | Unscoped site-key lookup (`get_site_key_by_key(session, key)`, a module-level function next to `create_tenant()` — no tenant is known yet, so it structurally cannot be a `TenantScopedRepository` method) plus a scoped visitor lookup-by-hash on `TenantScopedRepository` (scoped both to the repository's own `tenant_id` and to the given `site_key_id`) and a `last_seen_at` update on match. Hostile tests: a real hash belonging to a different tenant's visitor is never returned by a repository scoped to this tenant; an unknown hash returns `None`, indistinguishable in shape from a hash that exists but belongs elsewhere | Done |
| 1.4.f | Origin-matching pure function: exact match on scheme, host and effective port; `allowed_origins` entries are expected in the exact form `scheme://host[:port]`; no wildcards; `Referer` is never consulted; a missing `Origin`, a literal `"null"` `Origin`, or an `Origin` header sent more than once are all rejected. Offline, table-driven tests only | Done |
| 1.4.g | `POST /api/v1/session`: wires 1.4.e/1.4.f/1.4.b/1.4.c together. Unknown key, missing/disallowed origin, a suspended key and a non-active tenant all produce one byte-identical failure response (fixed 403 body, no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header); a draft key from an allowed origin succeeds, with `test_mode` in the response. Dynamic per-site-key CORS: preflight (`OPTIONS`) is answered without any DB lookup; a real response carries `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` only when the origin check passes, and `Vary: Origin` is present on every response regardless of outcome. The public widget-config response is built from an explicit field allow-list (never a raw model dump). Request model uses `extra="forbid"`. Hostile-caller tests for every failure/success combination named above | Done |
| 1.4.h | Verified-identity dependency: parses `Authorization: Bearer`, verifies the token (1.4.b), checks `Origin` equals the token's `org` claim, and checks cached tenant/site-key status through a 60-second TTL cache that takes an injectable clock (so TTL expiry is tested deterministically, never via a real `sleep`). Returns an immutable `VerifiedIdentity(tenant_id, vid, org)`. Hostile-caller tests: expired/forged/`alg=none`/wrong-`aud` tokens each rejected individually; a token replayed with a different `Origin` than its own `org` claim rejected; a suspended tenant/key blocked only once the cache TTL elapses (proving the TTL boundary itself, not just the DB state) | Done |
| 1.4.i | Repository dependency: `get_tenant_repository(identity=Depends(...), session=Depends(...)) -> TenantScopedRepository`, the one place a real endpoint ever constructs the class. Hostile-caller test: a request carrying a valid tenant-A token plus a forged body/query field naming tenant B's `tenant_id`/`vid`/`cid` has no effect — the constructed repository is scoped to A regardless, and the forged field is separately rejected by `extra="forbid"` | Done |
| 1.4.j | Readiness endpoint, separate from the existing liveness-only `/health` (which must never touch Postgres, per its own existing comment): a new endpoint that performs a real query against Postgres; the Compose `api` healthcheck updated to use it. Resolves the Postgres-readiness open marker. Live test: killing Postgres mid-run flips readiness unhealthy while `/health` still answers 200; restoring it recovers readiness | Done |
| 1.4.k | Lifespan hook on `create_app()`: disposes only what this application actually created — `get_engine().dispose()` and `get_qdrant_client().close()` — on shutdown. Resolves the API half of the shutdown-disposal open marker; the worker process's own lifecycle hook stays a separate, still-open concern owned by Step 2.1 (once `worker.py` has a real job loop to shut down cleanly). Live test: a real request touches both Postgres and Qdrant, shutdown is triggered, and both are proven disposed/closed, not merely "no exception raised" | Done |
| 1.4.l | Close-out: resolve/narrow the 1.4-owned open markers, record the Step 3.1 (`VerifiedIdentity.tenant_id` into `tenant_filter()`), Step 6 (origin normalization on save) and Phase 7 (JWT key rotation, documented and tested) markers, update PROJECT_SPEC.md | Done |

### Step 1.4 closure summary (2026-09-29)

Built: a required, validated, rotatable JWT signing key (`kid`-based,
current-plus-previous, no downtime); PyJWT-based session tokens pinned to
HS256 with every required claim enforced and a proven rejection of the
classic `alg=none` forgery; visitor-secret generation and constant-time
verification (`hmac.compare_digest`); `POST /api/v1/session`, whose five
distinct failure causes (unknown key, missing origin, disallowed origin, a
suspended key, a non-active tenant) all produce one byte-identical
response, alongside dynamic per-site-key CORS with no static allow-list;
the verified-identity dependency, with a 60-second TTL-cached, injectable-
clock, cross-tenant-isolated tenant/site-key status check and a strict
Origin-vs-token-`org` equality check distinct from the origin-matching
function; the repository dependency completing the chain from a verified
JWT to an automatically tenant-scoped database query, with no other
construction site anywhere in the request path; a `/ready` endpoint with a
real, empirically-proven 2-second timeout (confirmed against a genuinely
black-holing address, not a fast-refusing one); and a lifespan hook that
disposes only what this process actually created, with the
disposed-engine-vs-closed-Qdrant-client reuse asymmetry now fixed so a
second lifespan cycle in one process works cleanly.

What's still outstanding from this step (deferred deliberately, not
forgotten) is tracked entirely in section 9's Open markers list below —
see there rather than repeating it here.

### Step 1.5 closure summary (2026-09-30)

Built: a pure, framework-agnostic sliding-window-log `RateLimiter` with an
injectable clock and bounded memory (full-sweep-per-check pruning);
configurable, validated limit/window `Settings` fields for both a
